his back gave way. His hearing was immediately affected. Examination disclosed to Dr. Palmer a racked vertebra. He persuaded the man to allow him to adjust it, which he proceeded to do by using the spine as a lever and pressing the luxated bone into place. The dawning belief that he had discovered a mighty and fundamental truth was verified when Lillard presently regained his hearing. 

To what degree D. D. Palmer was experiment ing in a theory of which he had already acquired some knowledge has been a mooted subject. The discoverer's own subsequent statement on this score was as follows: "There was nothing 'accidental' about this, as it was accomplished with an object in view, and the result expected was obtained. There was nothing 'crude' about this adjustment; it was specific . . .' د, This declaration was made because of comments to the effect that the discovery had been entirely accidental. Dr. Palmer's frank admission that he had received knowledge of vertebral adjustment from Dr. Atkinson prior to treating Lillard, however, would certainly indicate that the cure was not merely fortuitous. 

Dr. B. J. Palmer, son of the discoverer, in his address at the annual lyceum of the Palmer School of Chiropractic, August, 1930, referred to the Lil- [14]

lard case, saying that an unusual bump appened on Lillard's back and that his father pressed it for three days before the man's hearing was restored. 

Then his father reasoned that if deafness could be cured by treating the spine, other deficiencies might be similarly cured. The report of this address appears in the Chiropractic News of September 18, 1930, the official publication of the American Bureau of Chiropractic. 

Dr. Willard Carver, head of the Carver Chiropractic College at Oklahoma City, who was one of the earliest pupils of D. D. Palmer, is of the opinion that the latter had but a vague notion of what he had discovered when Lillard's deafness was cured. In the Chiropractic Record of May, 1913, published by the organized alumni of Carver Chiropractic College, Dr. Carver says: "He was not by any means certain what he had discovered. So uncertain was he in fact, that he confided the whole matter to his friend, Rev. Samuel Weed, who was his co-student from the very first up to the time when he began to teach what he had learned of the then system of vertebral adjusting."

This was the first adjustment in chiropractic. 

The healing had been accomplished not by manipulation of the entire spine, according to the methods [15]

believed to have been employed by the ancients, but by a specific adjustment using the spinous and transverse processes as levers. This has remained the essential, characteristic method of the chiropractor, despite minor deviations and discoveries in technic from year to year. In the light of his subsequent findings, Dr. Palmer concluded that all pathological conditions were due to nerve impingement at the spinal foramen. Because the brain and spinal cord are of the same substance and carry energy to the entire body, it seemed logical in view of the 

Lillard case that that which is called disease found its origin at the spine. Here causes might be treated and not merely effects, as through the use of drugs and surgery. Assured by continued experiment of the soundness of the theory, he termed disease "aberrated impulse" caused by pressure on the nerves affecting certain functions, as by tension or irritation. The movable vertebrae might be put out of position by falls, heavy blows, strains, faulty carriage, stretched ligaments, contractions caused by disease, inherited weaknesses, etc.

The Religious Cognate 

The philosophy constructed about his discovery by Dr. Palmer reveals deep, sincere conviction that he had hit upon the secret of the life-sustaining [16]

force. While the religious elements of chiropractic have almost entirely fallen away in the course of its professional development, they appear in retrospect as attributes of an inspired belief and in no sense as a fogram attempt to ritualize the discovery. Nor indeed should the zeal of this citizen of 

Davenport be criticized because he had been a magnetic healer, unless one is disposed to outlaw all ministrations of this kind. That he was successful as a healer was indicated by a clientele that might have done honor to the mesmerist Phineas P. Quim by, Mrs. Eddy or Emile Coue'. Mrs. Eddy founded a religion upon her healing. Dr. Palmer erected a philosophy upon his own discovery, with a religious keynote which his followers believed unnecessary. The question whether healing is caused by Nature, God or man is secondary to the fact of healing itself, for none of us is permitted to know the whole truth of life. 

Suffice that D.'D. Palmer, untrained in standardized therapeutics, entered upon an intensive study of disease in its relation to bone luxations, his findings being compiled and published fifteen years later. During the first two years he kept the principles practically secret, while treating a large number of patients with a success which caused much wonder and speculation. It is said that he gave [17]

numerous treatments in a dimly lighted room and received at times as much as $500 from persons who secured admission for the purpose of learning the method. Observation, however, it is said, taught them nothing whatever. His sole confidant was the Rev. Samuel H. Weed of Monmouth, Illinois, who had aided him in naming the system, employing the Greek words chiro, hand, and praktikos, effective. His decision to impart to the world the secrets of chiropractic came in 1897, two years later, when he narrowly escaped death in a train accident at Clinton Junction, Illinois.

It was all the more natural, in view of this friendly clergyman's interest, that considerations of divine source should help to form the foundation of the new healing method. Into its structure went a little of the magnetic theory concerning the human aura, but the discoverer apparently made no effort to introduce it into his subsequent practice. In 1910 he indulged in some satire at the expense of Dr. Willard Carver because the latter had abandoned suggestion as a therapuetic aid to chiropractic. At this period he emphatically announced his stand that mental as well as physical ailments were due to osseous displacements, declaring that even insanity and liquor and tobacco habits depend for their continuance upon mis- [18]

placed vertebrae, the latter being drawn out of place by the contraction of nerves and muscles. 

It had been in the course of treating other diseases that Dr. Palmer professed to have found that unnatural cravings were perpetuated by luxations. 

Validity Attacked 

Although D. D. Palmer frankly declared in and out of print that he had been a magnetic healer for nine years, enemies of chiropractic seized upon this as evidence of a spurious origin of the science. 

That a magnetic healer and a former fish dealer could discover and evolve a new science was incredible, what though the disciples of Christ himself were lowly men. "Everyone knows nowadays that it is a fake,” wrote the osteopath, Dr. Eugene Pellette, of magnetic healing. Mrs. Annie Riley Hale offers in answer to Dr. Pellette the decision of the Royal

Commission at Paris, 1784, where the claims of Mesmer were largely vindicated. These investigators included Jussieu the naturalist, Lavoisier the chemist, and Benjamin Franklin, who happened to be in Paris. Anton Mesmer, following his success in Vienna, had been obliged to flee from Germany. 

The investigation of his theory by five savants of the Academy of Sciences and four physicians is [19]

told by the historian Wilder, who says, "The report failed to meet the issue, and so far from producing conviction, actually imparted new confidence to the champions of the new science." The theory of magnetic fluid unacknowledged by that name, was destined to become vindicated in the recognition of principles employed in hypnotism and manipulative healing, and comprehended in the scope of modern psychical experiment.

Nor is it intrinsically damaging to chiropractic to contend that D. D. Palmer had acquired something of osteopathy from Stother, a student in 

Kirksville, Missouri, the home of osteopathy, or that he had tried without success to establish a school of osteopathy with Stother about twenty years before his discovery of chiropractic.* If the elder Palmer was trained in osteopathy Dr. Pellette's inference that he was a mere adventurer without a sincere calling is untenable, and on the other hand the origin of chiropractic appears validated by a certain amount of scientific experiment on the part of the healer. If he repudiated osteopathy, preferring to accredit Dr. Jim Atkinson and his experiments with the nerves and vertebrae, * From a statement attributed to Dr. Charles Still, son of the founder of osteopathy. [ 20 ]

it is conceivable that Dr. Palmer may not have been actuated by any malice and actually regarded his own discovery and the theory acquired from Dr. 

Atkinson as having little in common with the fundamentals of osteopathy. A fair appraisement of chiropractic does not depend upon the circumstances of its origin but upon the work it accomplishes.

Scientific Verification 

No more impressive scientific corroboration of the fundamentals of chiropractic has occurred than through the revelations of that eminent scholar, Albert Abrams, M.D., F.R.M.S., former professor at Leland Stanford University and graduate of Heidelberg. In his Spondylotherapy (spinal therapy), published fifteen years after the birth of chiropractic, he touched upon theories that startled medical doctors throughout the world. He states in the first edition of the work named: "The subject of spinal therapeutics has received less attention from the medical profession than its deserves. 

Even the laity know that cold applied to the back of the neck may arrest hemorrhage from the nose, and that heat applied to the small of the back may hasten menstruation. The profound and farreaching physiological truths which underlie these [21]

simple phenomena have either been ignored or only given inconsiderate attention. Others, less scientific but more astute, have determined empirically that manipulation of the spine does sometimes cure conditions that have failed in the hands of experienced physicians. The author was led to a deeper study of spinal therapeutics in investigating various visceral reflexes which bear his name. As the years passed on he ascertained that a number of pathological conditions could be more easily and certainly controlled by spondylotherapeutic means than by the conventional measures. Sometimes physicians may consider the remedial methods discussed in this book to be unduly and unworthily simple, on the principle that what is obvious can hardly compete with what is obscure in the treatment of disease. Anybody, however, who investigates the study of spinal therapeutics in earnest will discover that the simplicity is only apparent. The successful practice of spondylotherapy requires knowledge, observation and experience of the highest kind, and is comparable to the best effort in any other department of scientific medicine. Any method of cure that is more or less new is inclined to be viewed critically by the formalist and traditionalist." 

More specific testimony comes from Dr. Henry [ 22 ]

Winsor, of Haverford, Pennsylvania, who rendered a report of his investigations of spinal lesions and their relations to visceral diseases before the American Orthopedic Association in 1924. He discovered spinal subluxations in every one of 100 cadavers. 

The latter were obtained from the operative surgical laboratory of the University of Pennsylvania. 

Fifty cadavers of adults showed vertebral subluxations in the same spinal segments supplying innervation to 139 diseased organs. Of 221 diseased structures in fifty cadavers 212 corresponded to the segments having subluxated vertebrae. That nine of the diseased organs failed to correspond is explained by the fact that nerve filaments from several segments may innervate an organ; also, filaments from one segment may supply several organs. Accordingly, in no case were all the organs found diseased which corresponded to the segments where subluxations occurred. In forty-two cadavers, associated with spinal subluxations in the upper dorsal region, were observed twenty-six instances of pleural adhesions, the lungs being diseased in nineteen of the twenty-six cases. The heart was diseased in six cases. The substance of the findings was that disease exists as a rule in organs and structures experiencing a loss of innervation through subluxation at the spine. [23]

Much other medical opinion is advanced in sup port of chiropractic theories. Notable testimony comes from the Medical Record of July 30, 1921 (vol. 100, no. 5), in an article entitled "Origin and Significance of Minor Vertebral Deformities," by Dr. J. Madison Taylor, professor of physical therapeutics and dietetics at Temple University, Philadelphia. Dr. Taylor writes: "A trained eye and touch will note, on examining the back areas of most individuals, young or old, certain anomalies of structure. Among alterations of structure are deviations of alignment of the vertebral eminences, often sudden, abrupt, some to one side or the other, some projecting posteriorly, others depressed. These are rarely noticed because the spine is seldom examined, but they deserve critical attention and differentiation. The subject has long attracted me and I approach the solution with an open mind.... One fact is sure: far too few physicians do explore backs, and yet fewer seem qualified to interpret the findings. . . . A definite constant relationship exists between certain morphogenic changes in the paravertebral tissues, and many ailments. ""* The late Elbert Hubbard, one of the most bril- 

* From The Verdict of Science on Chiropractic, by A. A. Erz, 1925. 

[24]

liant writers of recent times, whose magazine, The Philistine, was famed for its satire, personally investigated the claims of chiropractic. He wrote in his book, The Science of Keeping Well: 

"Above all things, a good chiropractor has faith in nature. He does not make the proud boast that he cures people. He knows that it is nature that heals. All the chiropractor can do is to put his patient in line with the healing forces of nature. 

Chiropractic never brings an adverse result. The chiropractor does not pin his faith in any single panacea. He simply knows the physical fact that a pressure of bone on the nerve brings about a condition where the telegraph system fails to act properly. With skilled hands he brings about right relationship and proper adjustment. He finds the cause and removes it. Chiropractors are not doctors of medicine. From them we get a new science which is adding greatly to the happiness and welfare of the world." [25]

CHAPTER III Schools-Early Development 

R. PALMER's first pupil was Leroy Baker, of Fulton, Illinois, and with the enrollment on January 15, 1898, of Dr. William A. 

Seeley, a homeopathic physician, the first approach to a scholastic course was undertaken. D. D. Pal mer dated the founding of Palmer School of Chiro practic at Davenport as in the year 1897, but admitted that it was not in session until the following year, when there was but one pupil. His scattered notes indicate that there were three students in 1899, two in 1900, five in 1901, and four in 1902, among whom was his son, B. J. Palmer.

It is very doubtful that chiropractic would have survived its formative period had not the little nucleus of workers possessed a genuine belief that they had unveiled a scientific truth of far-reaching importance, and this seems amply corroborated by the quality of perseverance shown not only by these pioneers but by the thousands of adherents up to the present time. The story of persecution, imprisonment and poverty has no parallel in the [26]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT 

annals of modern scientific pursuit. 

Already in 1903 the school had incurred the censure of physicians, and presently D. D. Palmer was arrested on the charge of practicing medicine without a license and was sent to jail for six months.

At this time he was in debt to the extent of several thousand dollars, but owned some property in 

Davenport. 

During his father's confinement, Dr. B. J. Palmer, who had just become of age, conducted the school under a power of attorney. When the former was released and severed connection with the school, the handful of students were left under the instruction of his son. 

That year, 1903, the elder Palmer opened the 

Portland College of Chiropractic at Portland, Ore gon. A three months' course was provided and two former doctors of medicine whom he had trained in chiropractic were employed as instructors. The venture failed and he returned to Iowa. Affairs were far from encouraging at the Palmer school. 

The Davenport banks would lend no money, newspapers refused to carry the advertising and chiropractic was commonly derided as charlatanry. 

Young Palmer was refused membership in clubs and slighted by old acquaintances. Dr. D. D. Palmer, seeing little chance of effective co-operation, [27]

left for Oklahoma. It appears to have been 1907 when he made a business proposition in Oklahoma 

City to his former pupil, Dr. Willard Carver, at the Carver-Denny Chiropractic College, which had opened its doors in 1906. Dr. Carver had been his attorney at the time of his arrest, and had been one of the first to receive knowledge of chiropractic directly from the discoverer. Between this man of cultured experience and the self-educated itinerant, however, little sympathy remained. Irreconcilable views thwarted negotiations at the very outset. Dr. D. D. Palmer allied himself with A. A. 

Gregory, who had just completed a course at the Carver-Denny College, and an effort was made to establish another school at Oklahoma City. The 

Palmer-Gregory Chiropractic College, however, was of short duration, so Palmer, after a three months' trial, made his way back to Portland, Oregon, where the Pacific College of Chiropractic had been organized. Here again affiliations proved unsatisfactory and he retired to private practice and the preparation of a volume to embody the principles he had brought into life.

Already a compilation of his writings, to which his son had contributed, had appeared under the title, The Science of Chiropractic. Its publication he had opposed, declaring that he had not yet [28]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT 

learned enough about the subject. B. J. Palmer, however, believed that the establishment of principles was being retarded rather than helped by the absence of published material. Other works had appeared, notably those of S. M. Langworthy, Oakley G. Smith and Lenora Paxton; A. P. Davis, author of Neuropathy, and Willard Carver, who contributed Chiropractic Analysis. These products the elder Palmer attacked with characteristic zeal. 

Definitions, premises and deductions came in for trenchant sarcasm with an admixture of joviality. 

It was in 1910 that his tome of 1,000 pages appeared in Portland. In many respects it seemed a challenge to the entire young world of chiropractic as well as to medical science. It was Mosaic in its dicta and Platonic in its thoroughness. Flaying allopathy in particular he denounced the use of drugs and discussed the cure of almost every disease from abasia to zymosis. The book teemed with maxims, controversy, satire, poetry and irrelevancies, but, withal, revealed a genius that must have impressed his most offended colleagues. Least of all was his own son spared in the many pages of diatribe. The full title of the work was The Chiropractor's Adjuster, A Text-book of the Science, Art and Philosophy of Chiropractic for Students and Practitioners. [ 29 ]

An illustration of his trenchant irony occurs on page 45, as follows: "Notwithstanding, Dr. Carver, under date of 

March 1, 1910, wrote me, "The only thing I want distinctly understood between you and me is, that you are not permitted to publish my letters to you or any part of them.' I quote from his of Feb. 

17th, 1910: 'In answering your question-Why not be specific in adjusting? I simply say, that is what I teach.' Giving seven pairs of nerves (14 nerve trunks), the occlusion of which causes consumption, is not precise, definite nor specific. "Dr. Carver states on page 4 of his Chiropractic Analysis that he 'received the very crude application of its principles from the founder.' "Those 'very crude' adjustments relieved him of consumption and are yet used by the founder in that 'very crude application' of adjusting one vertebra—the one displaced and none other. "My adjusting of one vertebra for the relief of consumption in the case of Willard Carver he thinks 'very crude,' but the adjusting of three pairs of cervical and four pairs of dorsal nerves is considered by him to be precise, definite and specific.... دو 

On page 24 he flays his former attorney, pupil [30]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT 

and patient for shunning the word disease and substituting "abnormality," accusing him of employing the latter term forty-one times on four pages and eight times in seven lines. 

He provided a weapon of defense for chiropractors arrested on the charge of practicing medicine without a license, or attempting to heal, which was effectively employed in various cases. Thus, on page 538, D. D. Palmer writes: "The science of Chiropractic does not cure. The adjuster relieves, removes the cause, then Innate can pass the impulses without hindrance." He refers to Innate

Intelligence. 

It is often said that "D. D.'s" claims never extended beyond that which he could demonstrate, and this view seems supported by the thoroughness of his study and exposition, even though this exposition in entirety amounted to a new conception of biology. Unquestionably he desired to establish chiropractic on a religious plane, and much of his philosophy still influences chiropractic education. His capacity for study and concentrated thought, although belied by erratic tendencies, appears to have been as phenomenal as his fondness for the esoteric and elusive. The latter was manifested to a degree astonishing in view of his meager schooling. [31]

D. D. Palmer's Death 

He lived to witness the entrenchment of his theories, and, with deep-rooted irony, to observe hundreds of Palmer School graduates in a profession founded upon what had been secrets-once reserved for the Palmer family. Thus was rendered even more poignant the tragedy of his death and the sensational episode of his last return to Palmer 

School of Chiropractic. 

In August, 1913, was held a home-coming for the former students, one of the early annual "lyceums" which have long since become gala weeks for Davenport. The event was heralded far and wide and "D. D." hastened to that city. His arrival occasioned both surprise and embarrassment. When a street parade of students and graduates was formed, the undaunted founder, carrying a small American flag, placed himself uninvited at the head of the procession. An automobile in passing struck him and he was taken unconscious to a hospital. He spent several weeks recovering from the shock. When able to travel he went to Los Angeles and there succumbed on October 20. 

Daniel David Palmer was born March 7, 1845, near Toronto, Canada, where his grandparents had settled. The Palmer family combined an English [32]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT 

and Germain strain, while the maternal ancestors were Scottish and Irish. The mother of Daniel was a surviving twin and weighed but a pound and a half at birth. Her son recorded that he was "cradled in a piece of hemlock bark." Facts of his early life are obscure, but it is known that his education was very limited and that prior to becoming a magnetic healer in Davenport, to which he had moved in 1886, he had been a fish dealer in the town of What Cheer, Iowa. Those who knew him in his later career declare him to have possessed an unusually magnetic personality and qualities of a psychic order. These were strongly inherited by Dr. B. J. Palmer, although the latter has belittled any such attributes. Both father and son are said to have been privately interested in spiritualism, which seems in fact verified by the latter's possession of various works on this subject. 

At the time the elder Palmer opened his offices in the old South Putnam Building at Second and 

Brady Streets, Bartlett Joshua was a precocious gamin of the streets, who was somewhat forgiven for mischievousness because of an unusual brightness and originality. The lad's scholastic career terminated abruptly after a few months in high school, on the occasion of his releasing in the classroom three rats from a cigar box. "B. J.," as he is [33]

founding of the Palmer called throughout the chiropractic profession, was fourteen years old at the time of his father's discovery coincidental with the adjustment performed on the janitor Lillard. Between the date of this event in 1895 and the school, the youth became absorbed in the subject which so completely occupied his father, and entered upon an enthusiastic study of the spine. Assisting his father in making adjustments, he gradually assumed the role of a teacher, therein incurring the disapproval of the elder by divulging some of the more closely guarded theories. 

In 1905 B. J. Palmer acquired a new location for the school at 828 Brady Street, formerly the residence of J. C. Duncan, and paid seventy-five dollars monthly for the rental. Although able to purchase the property the following year the success of the establishment was by no means assured. 

Not only were his professional efforts still prey to public ridicule, but he received open abuse and even persecution. Nowhere in Davenport could he obtain a dollar's credit. Students were exceedingly few and advertising nearly impossible to provide. 

Yet this was the institution destined to become not only foremost in the chiropractic world, but largest of all schools devoted to the healing arts. In 

1921 it had an enrollment of 2,300 students. [34]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT Growth of Palmer School 

As late as 1906, when D. D. Palmer left Davenport, the tuition fee of $500 which he had originally established (for a few minutes' demonstration) had been maintained to cover the six-months' course, and at that time the number of students varied from fifteen to twenty. The majority of these students sooner or later opened their own schools. Among the early institutions were the 

Parker School of Chiropractic at Ottumwa, Iowa, founded by Charles Ray Parker in 1905, providing a six-months' course for $250; and the American 

School of Chiropractic, established by S. M. Lang worthy in 1906 at Cedar Rapids, Iowa. Parker and Willard Carver both had been instructed by D. D. Palmer and thereafter together went deeply into chiropractic theories. Carver consequently began his teaching at the Parker school, where Lee L. Denny, a youth of twenty-one, took a course and then secured funds to aid in the establishment of the Carver-Denny Chiropractic College at Okla homa City. Upon Denny's retirement in 1908 it became the Carver Chiropractic College, which today commands a prestige comparable with that of the Palmer school. Two of Langworthy's associates at Cedar Rapids were Lenora Paxton and Oakley 

G. Smith. The latter started a school in Chicago. [35]

The fact that the various institutions taught somewhat different theories, no two being in precise agreement, is explained by the absence of a really formulated science at this period. Little more than spinal adjustment had been taught at the Palmer school. Naturally students on their own accord applied themselves to continually new problems of cause and effect, developed their own secondary theories and later passed them on to their pupils. Improvisations in technic were common, and distinction was deemed to rest considerably upon ingenuity. Invention was still in the air and an esoteric pride everywhere in evidence. Many of the newly original methods of address and adjustment were worthless, often ridiculous. The "Minnesota method" became notorious by the use of a wooden mallet and puncheon. For a time chiropractic seemed threatened by an excess of technic and a lack of science. 

The tardy growth of the Palmer school was succeeded by a steady development. In 1915 more than 800 students were enrolled. Within a year thereafter the number increased to 1,400. American participation in the Great War reduced the number of students to 823 in 1917 and 549 in 

1918, but the next year the roster showed 1,882 names. The students were largely of a promising [36]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT 

order and revealed serious habits of study. An impressive number had been identified with the more constructive spheres of life. Among the 

2,000 students registered in 1920 there were 102 former school teachers, 12 ministers of the gospel, 16 former school superintendents, 6 editors and 8 others from journalistic pursuits, 45 musicians, 24 pharmacists, 11 chemists, 7 dentists, 26 officers from the Army and Navy, 51 nurses, 23 manufacturers, 165 students from other schools, several osteopaths, doctors of medicine and veterinary surgeons, engineers, railroad men, artists, clerks, housewives, stenographers, instructors in physical training and many from still other pursuits. The foreigners represented thirty countries, New Zealand sending thirty-four students for enrollment in 1921. 

The business and educational success of Dr. B. 

J. Palmer has been explained by his possession of rare faculties of foresight, idealization and management. The esprit d'accord of this parent institution has become famous in the entire realm of chiropractic, for many thousands of its graduates have carried their praise far and wide, reflecting the strong personal hold of the director over his faculty and student following. Persons interested in the psychology of organization may find in the [37]

Palmer school a very concrete example of that superior cohesiveness that comes from a single dominant leadership. It is perhaps true that but for the leadership qualities of B. J. Palmer, chiropractic would scarcely have survived its country-wide opposition. Under a czar-like influence a democratic, atmosphere has paradoxically prevailed for a quarter of a century in this Davenport institution, and toward "B. J." and his wife, who was Mabel Heath, daughter of the school dean, a spellbound devotion has fostered work and accomplishment. 

The enterprise of the leader, however, nearly wrought the destruction of the monument reared to his name. The advent of the "neurocalometer," a nerve-testing device, was destined to exert a strange influence on chiropractic. It nearly proved the Frankenstein of the Palmer school, yet, today, is the inseparable aide of many a practitioner. 

Concerning the actual responsibility of Dr. B. J. 

Palmer for the disastrous results of this visitation from the domain of mechanics various accounts are given.

He had acquired just fame as a speaker, appearing before many kinds of organizations in all parts of the country. Incidentally, he toured the world, enriching his collection of objets d'arts and adding luster to his profession. On his extensive speaking [38]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT 

trips he refrained habitually from discussing chiropractic, but discoursed eloquently upon business psychology. These lecture tours are still considered at Palmer School among the most important departmental work.

The Neurocalometer 

In 1923 on one of these excursions into the lay world he happened to address an audience of colleagues. He departed from this rule of avoiding reference to professional matters by a statement to the effect that unless chiropractors lived up to their obligations to the science the entire profession would fail. Inference was given that his former pupils should look to him for guidance. The unexpected tenor of the address disturbed the almost hypnotic influence which this professional demi-god had exercised for many years. What was in store for chiropractic?

Some months later every listed chiropractor received a letter announcing the perfection of the neurocalometer, which was able to register the heat of various parts of the body and thus indicate more definitely than the hand where vertebral adjustments should be given. While not for sale, one of these instruments could be leased for $2,200. The first payment would be $600 in cash. Chiroprac- [39]

tors acquiring neurocalometers must charge their clients at least ten dollars for a reading or forfeit their contracts. Subscribers were to appear at 

Palmer School the following August for a series of lessons in the proper use of the instrument.*

Thousands of chiropractors, it is said, sent in money and made their pilgrimage to Davenport, returning home with "the little black box." In a few weeks many of the neurocalometers were on the shelf. Their custodians, nevertheless, were saddled with monthly payments.

Hostility toward "B. J." rose and rapidly welled into a maelstrom of indignation. It was widely supposed that he owned and completely controlled the invention, whose function threatened to displace palpation by hand. Here seemed nothing short of a betrayal of the faith as taught by "B. 

J." himself. Was the loyalty of thousands of disciples to be exploited? Was chiro-praktikos to lose its significance for all time? 

To make matters worse, other nerve detectors began to appear on the market, and the salesmen of the surgical instrument manufacturers suddenly saw their field widened and on their desks a The leasing plan was possibly suggested by the career of the famous Abrams "oscilloclast," an appliance which had come into use among a few chiropractors as well as among physicians and surgeons. [ 40 ]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT 

galaxy of new and unfamiliar names as prospects. 

Some of the rival mechanisms sold outright for only $100. "B. J." was then reported as saying that his machine could be made at this figure and still yield a profit. Into the offices of chiropractors came also the information that the head of Palmer School felt that the entire profession was under a moral obligation to him as its protagonist. 

He had bestowed a livelihood upon innumerable men and women; surely he was entitled to a consideration if he now aided them in increasing their revenues. "Fountain Head" Shaken

Revolt was rampant in every city where chiropractic had a stronghold. Old allegiance was thrown into the discard. Denounced as sheer commercialism the "nerve detecting" contract shattered the good will of the majority of the Palmer graduates, and the domination of Davenport crumbled. 

It is often said of Dr. B. J. Palmer that he is impervious to criticism, although he has more than once remarked in his lectures that "criticism kills." 

That he made no great effort to correct some of the false impressions indicates that the former assumption may be correct. The inside story of the neurocalometer, recently told by a prominent offi- [ 41 ]

cer of the American Bureau of Chiropractic, accredits the invention to Dr. Doss Evans, an electrical engineer. It is said that "B. J." opposed the device at the start and never acquired any property interest in it; that the neurocalometer had been invented not at Palmer School but in Kansas City, Frank Elliott, D.C., aiding Dr. Evans in financing the manufacture and distribution. Dr. 

Elliott, a relative of Dr. Palmer, was business manager at Palmer School at the time the leasing offer was circulated, and is declared to have accepted a fifty per cent interest in the profits. 

It is averred that "B. J." was not interested in selling the device, but was interested in the program involved, namely, that of establishing a new and uniform technic of diagnosis. It appears that after advocating the nerve-tester he realized that if the exceedingly delicate instrument were owned outright by the users, readings would differ in nearly every office. It was imperative not only that the neurocalometers should be kept in order but that they should all register correspondingly. 

Hence the course of instruction to chiropractors and the elaborate service facilities inaugurated. 

Something of a reaction awoke among many of the lessees and a defense of "B. J.'s" policies salvaged more or less good will and many contracts, [ 42 ]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT 

so that the neurocalometer remained more than a mere fixture in most offices. In fact, neurocalometer clubs were formed, notably among schools managed by former Palmer graduates. Under the pressure of competition the price of this early mechanical intruder fell to $150 down and five dollars a month. 

However, the angered chiropractors stopped sending students to the "Fountain Head,” and from 1924 the enrollment at the Palmer School of Chiropractic diminished. Defection spread rapidly until the institution that had been a Mecca to so large a part of the profession was apparently doomed. The impressive million-dollar property on Brady Street extending for three city blocks, the largest of non-medical institutions, with its five-story administration building and roof garden, costing $200,000; its free clinics, its tower and sixteen-bell chimes, its halls, offices, laboratories, auditorium, memorial building; its osteological studio containing the greatest collection of skeletons and bones in the world, its walls with their endowment blocks bearing an eternal testimony of friendship from the chiropractic worldall faced the specter of the auction block. On the platform of the auditorium had appeared many a celebrity: Elbert Hubbard, Governor Henry Allen 

[ 43 ]

White, William Jennings Bryan and others of equal renown. The dreams of the little fish dealer of What Cheer had come true. A wonderful monument had been reared in the name of a new science, whose benefits to mankind would transcend earthly knowledge. Now at the very hands of the disciples themselves was this monument to be overthrown! Gone would be the morale-the sentiment -of an entire profession! And to whose garret might go the bust of the discoverer, for which these rebellious children had once so lovingly subscribed the sum of $10,000?

A confidential letter came to a few graduates whose loyalty was unquestioned. Should the school be turned over to the profession-should "B. J." retire from the scene-should the work continue? 

The question came directly from the "Fountain Head." Many of the alumni convened for three days. "B. J." read a statement, retired and remained absent. His communication was to the effect that the school was being conducted at a loss, as figures presented therewith showed. The deliberations resulted in a resolution urging Dr. 

B. J. Palmer to remain as head of the school, and an advisory board of graduates was selected. 

Under a fresh impetus the Palmer School of Chiropractic held to its work and retrieved a con- [44]

siderable part of its patronage. Its prestige could not be entirely dethroned as long as it was able to function, for the upholders of the Palmer system remained legion and the old institution represented a nuclear energy, an inalienable factor in chiropractic history and growth. The decline of the school, however, was sufficient to cause a perceptible disintegration of standards. Concerted advancement throughout the profession was not only rendered more difficult, but chaotic rivalries sprang up in various parts of the country. New associations and unfamiliar leaderships came into existence, and with them numerous unfledged theories and methods of treatment. Out of this welter arose a side movement toward pan-therapy which seemed to threaten an eventual capitulation to medical authority. This departure from the fundamentals of the science is at the present time an outstanding cause of agitation in the ranks of the profession and is viewed as a left-wing demonstration by the conservatives. The danger from this extreme has tended to unite more closely the fundamentalist element of licensed chiropractors, who have always adhered to the principles enunciated by D. D. Palmer and have regarded any concession to medicinal treatment or surgery as undermining chiropractic. [ 45 ]

Progress of Chiropractic 

In 1906, three years after the establishment of the school at Davenport, there were approximately one hundred chiropractors active in the profession. 

In 1908 the number had at least quadrupled, certain estimates, however, placing the number at six hundred. At present there are in the United States 

13,500 licensed chiropractors in authorized good standing, and almost as many more who are chiropractors in name only. California, one of the foremost states in the development of the science, has approximately one-fifth of the country's total of licensed doctors of chiropractic. It is estimated that there are in the world approximately 30,000 chiropractors, the foreign countries chiefly represented being Great Britain and Ireland, Canada, Germany, France, Spain, Italy, the Scandinavian countries, Portugal, Belgium, Australia, Czecho- 

Slovakia, Jugoslavia, New Zealand, the South

American republics, India, China, Japan, the Philippines, Cuba, Greece and parts of Africa. In Hawaii the profession is well represented. 

The record of accomplishment as presented by the Chiropractic Research and Review Service of 

Indianapolis is remarkably impressive. Complete recoveries are recorded in nearly 90,000 out of 100,000 cases, all having received a medical diag- [46]

SCHOOLS-EARLY DEVELOPMENT 

nosis prior to chiropractic attendance. The list includes practically all ordinary diseases and the names and addresses of patients are available for the purposes of corroboration. Relative to the treatment of the insane one chiropractic hospital reports sixty-five per cent of the cases as cured. [ 47 ]

CHAPTER IV Fundamentals 

INCE the days when Dr. D. D. Palmer began to demonstrate to students how the vertebrae could be moved, the outstanding change in chiropractic has been the virtual abandonment of the religious cognation, although other philosophic elements have been more or less preserved. Technic has altered but little, and theory holds to its original premises. The word disease has been abandoned. Abnormality or some equivalent term is used. This latter is a step forward from the founder's teachings. He had used the word disease freely. Follow his definition of it: "Disease is too much or not enough functionating"; again, "Disease is the result of liberating too much energy, or the retarding-lessening-of Innate stimulus. The former excites, the latter depresses vital force. The ordinary transformation of energy is health. The modifying, or swaying, either way, above or below normal, is disease-augmentation or lessening of impulses and morbidity of tissue. . . . [ 48 ]

FUNDAMENTALS 

As soon as nerves are made taut by pressure, just so soon they become tense; tensity changes the temperature; tensity modifies functions; abnormal temperature modifies tissue." Upon this premise the abandonment of the whole galaxy of Greek and Latin medical terms has taken place. 

Orthodox biology, without changing facts, has assumed a new aspect under chiropractic reasoning. Tone, the basic principle of the science and philosophy of chiropractic, is declared requisite to all animal and vegetable life. It is described as that condition of a body or any of its parts in which the organic or animal functions are performed with the proper vigor. Biologically this implies a normal tension or firmness of the nerves, muscles and organs. The fibers of a plant are in normal tension under the right proportions of light, heat and moisture. 

The orthodox idea of sickness has been derived from a belief that it is a foreign entity with power to attack the body, hence that something more potent than disease is required to combat it. The prehistoric origin of this view is deemed obvious. 

Because the bite of a poisonous snake resulted in sickness all sickness was the result of an exterior malevolence. Casting out devils, in the early Christian era, meant overcoming an evil influence usual- [ 49 ]

ly manifested in bad temper or insanity. Witchcraft was recognized as late as the Seventeenth Century and still commands a following in all parts of the world. Under another guise it is paid homage among esoteric groups which carry thought transference to ultimate conclusions. 

Chiropractic does not endow disease with any supernatural power. In fact it does not recognize disease in the prevailing sense of an invading enemy but as a disturbed condition. The chiropractor's patient consequently comes to understand that there is no invisible monster lodged in his vitals for the purpose of destroying him, but that there is simply a mal-condition. He feels less apprehension and therefore is minded to help competent nature exert its cure as nerve pressure is relievedby opening up the pathway so that vital energy may be restored to every cell in the body. The chiropractor merely sets about to right the body so that it will function. Primarily concerned with subluxations he explains his task to the patient and does not permit mystery to enshroud his case. 

There is little chance of a sufferer becoming unconscious or being thrown into convulsions through fear.

In treating causes rather than effects chiropractic adopts the role of an exact science. Its de- [50]

FUNDAMENTALS 

fenders aver that after many centuries of study, medical science on the other hand still remains in a state of empiricism. The point of view of the chiropractor is declared to be definite, his adjustment specific and the element of experiment almost totally lacking. The diagnosis and deductions of the physician are based on symptomatology. 

He seeks to arrest the abnormal manifestations or to reason backward and apply a remedy at the seat of trouble. Since various affections produce almost identical symptoms, this kind of tracing is held to be vastly more uncertain than nerve tracing and seldom leads to the actual source of the difficulty. 

There is no part of the body which is not governed by the nervous system. In the lower forms of life the latter is represented by a spinal cord only. With the advancement of animal life a brain began to develop at the end of the spinal cord. In the backbone is contained the vital energy that is transmitted to the millions of cells in the body. It has taken as many millions of years to bring into being the marvelous human mechanism, in which perfect harmony and co-ordination depend upon an unimpaired and normal transmission of energy. Hippocrates, ancient healer of 

Greece, who was born about 460 B.C., discovered that there was an inherent healing power in the [51]

body. Aristotle (384-322 B.C.) contributed to anatomy his great discovery of the nerves, which he had observed in animals. Plato, his teacher, had expounded the theory that there was an intelligent life-sustaining force. These three fundamentals are considered as offering basis for an absolutely natural healing method, and chiropractic affirms that the nerves themselves, like delicate wires, are charged with this life-giving, life-preserving current; that communication between the brain and the various parts of the body must be uninterrupted and at normal power in order that health be maintained.

Chiropractic recognizes all the known facts of cell life and the physiological processes of the body in their entirety. It teaches that vital force-energy-is responsible for their maintenance and continued development. It is easier to maintain these processes than to develop them. The body can adapt itself to a rather wide range of conditions, depending upon the ability of the nerves to respond appropriately to the various demands of environment. There are in general only two conditions responsible for illness, namely: a degree of abnormity in any vicissitude beyond the bodily powers of adaptation, or, second, a mechanical derangement of such nature as to interfere with the trans- [52]

FUNDAMENTALS 

mission of nervous energy from the tissues to the brain and from the brain to the organs and tissues. 

This energy is transmitted over pathways, traceable to certain points between the bony segments of the vertebral column, which, being flexible, under various conditions impinges upon or pinches the nerves, thus preventing the transmission of energy. In some instances the pressure is temporary and in others chronic as a result of fixation. 

Flexibility is due to the discs or elastic cartilages between the vertebrae, and the fixation or thinning of the discs causes impingement. 

When these conditions arise, instead of a disease having attacked the body, they amount to a purely mechanical derangement which requires adjustment before normal functioning and organic integrity can be established. A study of the structures of the body discloses the fact that pressure on the nerves cannot occur elsewhere except temporarily, where it may be corrected by the natural adaptative powers of the tissues. 

Nature being incapable of error, according to chiropractic, all that occurs in the body is natural. 

In addition to recognizing the chemical, mechanical and thermal elements involved in cell life and the human body, chiropractic in its corrective procedure views the human body as a machine [53]

already perfect, or, at least, once perfect but having developed physical defects. It does not seek to give the body something that it is not intended to possess, but to enable it to do the work it should do. It regards the human body as the most wonderful of all machines, in which operates every principle known to mechanics, chemistry and thermatology. Germ Theory Chiropractic takes issue with medical science on the ground that the latter has not disclosed fundamental facts, as witnessed by its theories on infiltration, degeneration and necrosis; in fact, chiropractic rejects the entire medical theory of disease. 

The first aim of prevention it considers to be the maintenance of the powers of adaptation at their maximum; the first aim of cure the release of vital force, which is competent to meet every emergency. Germs are found to be merely the product of certain environments, as natural to a given condition as coconuts in the tropics or white foxes in the arctic. Fishes do not produce the pond. The germ is no factor in a failure to function, a maxim which induced the chiropractic doctors during the epidemic of influenza in 1918 to lose no time pursuing the pneumococcus but rather to deprive the [54]

FUNDAMENTALS 

germ of its sustenance. Of 41,000 cases treated it is declared that only one death in a total of 1,800 was due to that condition termed pneumonia, despite the prevalence of the latter in a very large percentage of cases.

There are no biological grounds for assuming that a germ, say from a typhoid environment, upon entering a healthy body would dominate the new environment. Germs are produced by specific conditions, which when normal can resist invasion from a foreign abnormal source. The germ corresponds to the environment it feeds upon. 

The alleged existence of persons called "germ carriers," who infect others without themselves being affected, is held as a thoroughly unscientific superstition. Were it true, physicians going from patient to patient would be the chief culprits in transmitting disease.

Persons disinclined to accept the simpler theories of chiropractic are very naturally skeptical as to its efficacy in dangerous illnesses, even where medical science has proved unreliable. The layman at once accepts the fact that an adjustment at the spine may cause some changes in nearby tissues, but it is difficult for him to believe that changes can take place in remote organs and in the extremities. The principle is more easily grasped [55]

by visualizing the nervous system as an integral development. To the tiniest cells of the body lead nerves which supply the required energy. All come from the intervertebral foramen, through which the large nerve trunk passes from the spinal canal. The nerve branches do not lead directly to a particular region but are sent from the nerve trunks in various directions, sometimes meeting to feed the cells of the same tract, and becoming continually finer in their ramifications. No part of the body is entirely dependent upon one nerve branch for its innervation. 

Muscular contraction near the spine is capable of compressing the cartilage of the vertebral column, which causes a drawing together of the bony ridges of the opening through which the nerve trunk emerges. The latter consequently receives a pressure which reduces its working surface and lessens the energy transmitted to the tissues. The only places in the entire body where pressure on nerve trunks can occur are at these openings where they leave the spine.

So marvelously intricate is this'integral development of the nervous system that the remotest filaments, which terminate in the peripheral tissues, are invisible even under the microscope. When action in any part of this system is arrested a con- [56]

FUNDAMENTALS 

dition known as stasis occurs. A complete occlusion of the nervous impulses results in paralysis. 

When cells are unable to function congestion results through the retention of fluids, and stimulation of the surrounding cells produces inflammation, which attracts nervous energy to the area and may or may not restore action. Should this inflammatory process succeed in restoring action to the cells, it will induce movement of the retained fluids, reduce congestion, and restore normal tone to the congested area. If the inflammatory process should not be successful, the congested tissues will disintegrate, beginning at a central point, and forming conditions variously identified in symptomotology according to area involved as, for instance, a boil, abscess, tumor or cancer. No matter how the symptoms are named, the condition, according to chiropractic, is the same. The differences are of degree only. The greater the area of involved tissue the weaker the response to nervous stimulation, until in chronic cases occurs a generally high temperature called fever, in contradistinction to local high temperature which is called inflammation. Inflammation resulting from "mechanical" stimulation is more acute when in a restricted area, owing to the presence of a larger area of healthy tissue, which is capable of greater [57]

reaction. General inflammation or fever is therefore less acute.

Only One Abnormality 

Chiropractic recognizes but one abnormality: improper innervation. The many symptoms, known as diseases, represent differences of degree in this abnormality, due in every case to excessive or diminished activity in the tissue. Familiar identifications mean little or nothing. The appellations tumor and whooping-cough have no more significance to chiropractic than wild asparagus has to the principles of agriculture. Health is the condition of normal innervation, which is the sole function of chiropractic to restore or produce. 

Morbid conditions in tissue, which are due to abnormal supply of nerve energy, produce poisonous gases which may be carried through the vascular channels to manifest themselves in some distant part of the body. Gases passing through the circulation to the lungs leave deposits of carbon dioxide on the capillaries in the alveoli and carbonize the delicate tissues, thus preventing a proper oxygenation of the blood, which upon returning to the area of abnormality collects more poison and finds its way again to the alveoli of the lungs causing a congestion termed pneumonia. This latter condi- [58]

FUNDAMENTALS 

tion, therefore, is secondary, and permanent relief depends upon the restoration of normal innervation in the morbid area. 

Perfect health is present with normally functioning nerves and normally active tissues-muscular, mucous, fluid or osseous. The natural efforts of the body to eliminate waste tissues may eventually produce other subluxations and impingements, and the chiropractor is required to make palpations before adjustments in order to determine altered conditions. Increased stimulation and reflexes following adjustments must also be taken into account, but at all times the chiropractor is primarily concerned with nerve pressure.

It is maintained that medicinal remedies and external applications never cure morbid conditions, that in all cases of cure under such treatment the natural chemistry of the body has been responsible. 

Unless normal energizing has occurred relief will be only temporary and the symptoms will recur in the same or other tissues. The only exception is the immediate effect of wounds and other injuries caused by violence. Cancer

The claims of chiropractic with respect to cancer are sensational. Mystery and horror has en- [59]

veloped this condition since time immemorial. 

Cure has been declared impossible among the learned of the medical profession. The quest of the ravaging bacillus has constituted a leading epic in the history of pathology. No surer publicity stunt exists than an announcement that the germ or the cure has been discovered. Invariably the world hears no more of the discovery. Meanwhile the ineffectual butchery of sufferers continues. 

The cause of cancer has already been discussed in the foregoing paragraphs. Medicine has described cancer as a growth, which is alleged to be misleading. Chiropractic declares it to be a degeneration of tissue, the characteristics of which are determined by the location and degree of impairment of the parts involved. Chiropractic has offered a theory which has been generally disregarded in a long and futile research, and has presented emphatically its claim for recognition in the relief and actual cure of the dread malady. 

Dr. T. F. Ratledge, of Los Angeles, one of the most prominent chiropractors of the West, in the course of twenty years' study and research has sought governmental aid for the purpose of bringing to light additional facts concerning cancer. He considers that the entire truth about this abnormality is demonstrable. Discussing its characteris- [60]

FUNDAMENTALS 

tics he has pointed out that the usual sites of cancer are those parts of the body most subject to the stress of function, but that it may develop in any tissue as a result of almost any form of injury. 

Only improperly innervated tissue is susceptible. 

The belief that cancer is a growth due to harmful extraneous influences is declared by Dr. Ratledge to be a hypothetical error. He pronounces the swelling to be a certain stage in the degeneration of tissue, and its degree largely influenced by oxygen deficiency. 

It is easier to comprehend the importance of the nerves in maintaining vitality than to visualize their far-reaching influence. Various abnormalities in children are traceable to impingements on the nerves of their parents, as affecting cellular activity and predispositions to certain ailments.

Adenoids, affections of the ears and nose and general head disturbances are largely due to the unnecessary force used by persons assisting at birth. 

Jerking of children by the head frequently causes impingements, and this is given as a cause in many cases where one eye has become deflected, as may appear from one to five years after birth. It is claimed that chiropractic has considerably minimized labor pains, save at ejection and after-birth. 

Subsequent operations on the mother due to tear- [61]

ing of the tissues and what are called adhesions are said to have been obviated and vast economy in health and confinement costs made possible.

Chiropractic aid has been extended to many thousands of persons employed in sedentary occupations and unable to get sufficient exercise to keep up bodily tone. Nerve pressure of long duration acquired by habitually cramped positions during working hours has yielded to skilled adjustment. 

Constipation prevails in such environments and chronic intestinal trouble is exceedingly common. 

But the more outstanding achievements of the new science naturally lie in the wide category of serious illnesses. When it is realized that by far the majority of sufferers have sought chiropractic as a last resort, some adequate idea may be had of the significance of its claims and the scope of its The years of thorough preparation now required of the student in chiropractic and the extent of scientific research throughout the profession attest a vastly higher grade of service than has been commonly supposed. success.

Vivisection has been found unnecessary for experimental purposes and considerable opposition to it was voiced by chiropractic influences in 1918. 

Discovering that they were losing friends in a conflict that had little bearing on their work or future, [ 62 ]

FUNDAMENTALS 

chiropractors gradually withdrew from this slippery old arena of controversy. 

In numerous instances chiropractic has been applied to animals with notably satisfactory results. 

Dogs have been tied and muzzled and their subluxations corrected, giving relief in several types of indispositions. Dr. K. T. Shea of Los Angeles in 1921 made an adjustment on a goat which had ruptured a blood vessel, causing its milk to be streaked with blood. The animal was placed over a saw horse and responded favorably to the treatment. Similar success attended adjustments on dogs for experimental study. Dr. Clarence De

Marsa, of Los Angeles, tells of having given prompt relief to a dog whose hind legs had been paralyzed by a kick from a teamster which caused a spinal dislocation.* *The foregoing presentation of the fundamentals is derived from statements generously contributed by Drs. K. T. Shea, T. F. Ratledge and 

James Compton, of Los Angeles. [63]

CHAPTER V Chiropractic versus Medicine HE BONE OF CONTENTION between chiro practic and medicine has not changed materially since the first prosecutions at the Palmer school in 1903. "Practicing medicine without a license" has remained the obviously illogical basis of attack. Technicalities have secured judgments and penalties; equally they have provided defense. The contentions of chiropractic have taken root in "inalienable rights." In view of the constitutionally bestowed privileges of "life, liberty and the pursuit of happiness," the prosecuted continue to assert that the individual has too little to say as to how he or his next of kin shall be doctored-especially when life is at stake. They contend incidentally that medical laws are not vindicated by the fact of the ignorance of individuals as to their bodies and how properly to protect and preserve life. The appalling lack of information and a general credulity towards healing systems are deemed to have fostered the exploitation [64]

of the afflicted, to the end that scientific progress and health have been terribly handicapped. 

Chiropractors allude to the preternatural humbug of medieval alchemy, direct ancestor of allopathy, and declare it relatively not more preposterous than the empirical bigotry and abracadabra of medicine today. They cite the admission of medical doctors that there are less than ten curative specifics in all the pharmacopoeia, and maintain that the career of patent medicines and endless remedial impostures have engendered an increasing public distrust in the men and methods designated to guard human life.

Profiteering in birth and at death, and in the conduct of hospitals is pronounced a national stig- v ma. It is authoritatively said that an expectant mother of average means can travel to Europe, pass through her confinement and return with her child at less expense and with probably greater safety than if she is treated at the average hospital of her own city.

The wholly inadequate hospital accommodations for afflicted persons of modest or scanty means is referred to as distressing and disgraceful, and the refusal of some hospitals to admit emergency cases, especially where such refusal may result in death, is declared inhuman and at total [65]

variance with the medical gesture towards public health conservation. The recurring hospital scan dals, involving cases of alleged brutality on the part of internes and nurses, surgical incompetency and even unsanitary conditions, are classed with the sensational investigations and reports of prison conditions. 

With due credit to conscientious physicians, chiropractors point to such shortcomings and abuses as rendering the long attempted outlawry of chiropractors a preposterous spectacle, as unjust and misguided as it is un-American. It is contended that it would be far more appropriate, for example, to bar from practice the medical doctors responsible for the continued spread of the narcotic evil. Prosecution

The prosecution of chiropractors began with chiropractic and has lasted to the present time. 

Even though today, according to well-known chiropractic authorities, nearly one-half of the socalled members of the profession are either unqualified to practice because of inadequate study or are justly included in the category of quacks, chiropractic's achievement is proclaimed as more impressive than that of the medical profession, and at the present time less experimental. The per- [66]

centage of incompetents is held to be no greater than in any other profession. The history of its accomplishments in the face of severe opposition has proved impressive-sufficient to convert the great majority of legislatures before whom the rights of chiropractic have been presented. After relentless persecution for a generation, causing much imprisonment, actual martyrdom and impoverishment, the cause of chiropractic is deemed to be vindicated, as witnessed by thirty-nine states of the Union now according its representatives the right to practice.

The first to go to jail for the cause was its founder, Dr. D. D. Palmer. Dr. Willard Carver, of Oklahoma City, one of the leaders of the profession, also served time for his faith. The crusade against the pioneers began under allegations of illegal practice, and was continued through nonrecognition of the science of chiropractic or, in many cases, because of outright charlatanry, and generally with a complete disdain for any accruing benefits. Chiropractors have never practiced medicine and the conservatives are opposed to its use under any circumstances. They have never made concessions to medicine, nor conceded to medicine and surgery that value given by osteopaths. They have not professed even to be healers, [67]

for their fundamental theory is that only nature is competent to effect healing, and spinal adjustments are intended solely to release natural forces. 

They address themselves to the spine and not to the disease-a defense not usually convincing to the court, however. In any case the possibilities of harmful results are declared to be infinitely small in comparison with surgery and medicine. The fact that patients have increased rapidly in number testifies to successful and important service. But because allopathic standards have claimed preservation at all costs, and because chiropractic diploma mills have flourished and charlatans embraced the cause, ergo all of chiropractic must be condemned and destroyed. As prejudice lay at the bottom of the persecution, so lack of foresight in the job of prosecuting eventually tended to strengthen the chiropractic sinews.

High Cost of Health 

During the year 1930 the comment concerning medical fees and the high cost of health began to threaten a national scandal. It was taken up by many of the principal newspapers and periodicals of the country, voicing not merely the protest of chiropractic but that of the general public. 

Dr. Shirley W. Wynne, Health Commissioner 

[68]

of New York, is quoted as declaring in New York on November 12, 1930, in the presence of board members of the Public Health and Nursing Ser vice, representing six states: "The white collared class is receiving poor medical service at an exorbitant cost. . . . The solution of the problem lies either with the doctors organizing among themselves to supply qualified service at a fixed fee, or a public demand for state medicine."

However pertinent the criticism of medical service, the culminating suggestion of "state medicine" still more clearly sounded the tocsin so familiar to the chiropractic ear. Assuredly inconsistent, it seemed, that the shortcomings of medicine should particularly require its legal enthronement. 

Already the New York commissioner had become the target of the chiropractic press and his own medical colleagues as well, many of the latter taking exception on ethical grounds to a testimonial written for a toothpaste. His providing the New York 

Herald-Tribune with a "rogues gallery of quacks" seems to have been an effort to discountenance all drugless healers, and actuated by the same motive as his attempt to establish a radio censorship that would exclude all broadcasting that might run counter to medical policies. But the proposed alternative of state medicine reaching the press of [ 69 ]

the nation was the chief danger signal. The influence of the New York commissioner was considerable, however his ethics might be deplored by the New York County Medical Society. There was the precedent of former Health Commissioner

Royal S. Copeland, who had been elected to the 

United States Senate in 1922 and 1928. The bridge between medical and political authority has a short span, and Washington gives ready ear to New York sentiments. Indeed something of a crisis in the world of drugless healing is predicted in chiropractic journals. 

Medical issues so prominently aired in New York illustrate the general trend of conflict over the question of drugs. Materia medica had been placed on the defensive. Dr. Wynne's condemnation of high medical fees and his suggestion that physicians and surgeons advertise their prices, to the discouragement of fee-splitting and the disparagement of ethics, proved a bombshell for his constituents and perhaps a boon to chiropractic.

The latter, at least, had not reached that exalted professional plane where profiteering occurs. 

Vaccines and Serums

The political foothold gained by the medical fraternity lies in public health service. It has been [70]

helped by richly endowed medical foundations, and by the prestige of war surgery and universal vaccination. Chiropractic, opposed to the use of vaccines and serums, as well as to all medicine, centers its attack upon compulsory examination and treatment, invoking the constitutional rights of the individual. It views public health service, as advocated by the medical fraternity up to the present time, less of a humanitarian task than one calculated to perpetuate allopathy and to increase medical revenues by the general adoption of laws requiring allegedly specific preventive measures, such as for example inoculations. Its leaders have attributed the wide use of serums largely to the propaganda of their manufacturers, and to some extent the subsidizing of medical offices. Dr. 

Chiropractic has not rested its opinions regarding bacteria and serums solely upon the statements of its own people. Many prominent authorities have been quoted from time to time, including scientists and physicians of world renown. 

Antoine Bechamp, professor of medicine, chemistry and pharmacy at the University of Montpellier, France, declared that germs are the concomitant effect, not the pathogenic cause, of disease, and act as friendly scavengers. Other opponents of the 

Pasteur theory are said to include doctors Sir Henry [71]

Maudesley, Sir Lawson Tait, Henry Bastian, Charles Stirling Saunder, H. Fergie Woods, Reginald Aus tin and Herbert Snow. The chiropractor explains the general adherence to the theory that the bacterium is a disease-producing invader as due to a professional desire to further the use of preventive remedies. 

Dr. Philip Ricord told the French Academy, of which he was a member, that vaccine spread syphilis. Cowpox, the source of vaccine, is said to have been declared analagous to syphilis in man by such eminent English physicians as Sir Charles 

Creighton, Dr. Benjamin Ward Richardson, Dr. 

Edgar M. Crookshank, Sir William Collins, Dr.

James J. Garth Wilkinson, Dr. Robert Bell, Dr. 

Herbert Snow and Dr. William Scott Tebb. Dr. 

Creighton called vaccination "a grotesque superstition," and Dr. Wilkinson referred to the practice as "blood assassination." Dr. Caron, a prominent 

French physician, declined at all times to vaccinate. 

Herbert Spencer, John Stuart Mill and Alfred 

Russell Wallace denounced vaccination, the latter proclaiming it "a stupendous delusion and its enforcement a crime."

The origin of vaccination has been widely ridiculed as traced from the country pharmacist Jenner, who circulated the rumor that anyone who [72]

had taken cowpox could never have smallpox. "The solemn gesture of protecting public health" incited Sir William Arbuthnot Lane, physician to the King of England, to utterances, which, it is said, prevented his being invited to address the members of the American Society for Control of Cancer Propaganda. Among the startling statements of the distinguished visitor was the following: "We have been studying germs and testtubes when we should have been studying diet and drainage." This surgeon of London's best hospitals betrayed heresy over the germ doctrine by saying "There is but one cause of disease-poison, toxemia, most of which is created in the body by faulty living habits and faulty elimination." 

It was mainly his mention of carrots and cabbages as a preventive and possibly a cure for cancer which made him unpopular with the society for cancer control.

Chiropractors and other groups opposed to the employment of antitoxins have brought to light the numerous cases of anaphylaxis, or the dangerous reaction possible from toxin-antitoxin injections. Medical publications name other cases. 

Sixty-two deaths from this cause are reported in detail in the Journal of the American Medical Association, April 5, 1924, pages 1093-1098. In an [73]

issue of January, 1926, seven cases are discussed by

Dr. Chester A. Stewart. 

Voicing the suspicion that "the hand that rocks the vaccine factory is the hand that rocks the medical profession" on the part of Dr. J. F. Baldwin resulted in his ouster from the presidency of the Ohio State Medical Society.

The immunity chances indicated by the sponsors of serum treatment appear unimpressive, say the doctors of chiropractic. This particularly applies to diphtheria, considering the fact that the estimated expectancy of diphtheria per 1,000 inhabitants in the United States is only 1.3, according to the United States Public Health Reports of 

November 21, 1924.

The Neglected Spine 

Alfred Walton, M.D., graduate of Harvard in 

1879, former president and chief of the surgical division of Essex County Hospital, New Jersey, and corresponding fellow of the Maine Academy of Medicine, is quoted by James Compton, D.C., former secretary of the California State Board of Chiropractors, in The California Telegram, of 

April 27, 1922, as follows: "As incomprehensible as it may appear, the medical profession is woefully deficient in knowl- [74]

edge of the spine, and its importance in the treatment of disease. Men of scholarly learning, and of true scientific discernment, have investigated with great patience every conceivable avenue of research bearing upon the cause of disease, but the importance of vertebral displacement, as a causative factor, has been ignored or greatly underestimated. "The principal reason for this deplorable situation is to be found in the fact that anatomical instructors have discouraged dissection of the spine. 

Medical students have not only been permitted but have been required to make more or less careful dissection of other parts of the body-arms, legs, head, etc., but all the medical men I have ever talked with concerning the subject, without exception, acknowledged they were not required to dissect spines. دو Herein lies one of the many causes of resentment on the part of chiropractors, whose study of the vertebral column and nerves emanating therefrom covers a period of three or four years, in view of which it is regarded as preposterous that their credentials should be determined by doctors of medicine.

Dr. Compton also quotes Richard C. Cabot, 

M.D., head of the Massachusetts General Hospital and author of a well-known work on diagnosis, as [75]

saying publicly that but fifty-three per cent of the diagnoses at his hospital were correct. Dr. Compton raised the question as to the average of the ordinary physician, considering that so distinguished an institution erred in judging the nature of fortyseven per cent of its cases.

This chiropractor recalls the sensational decree of the Bavarian Royal College of Physicians while opposing the building of railroads, to the effect that rapid travel would give the people brain disease, and further alludes to the banishment of Harvey, physician extraordinary to the King of England, for saying that the blood did not stand still in the arteries. Dr. Compton reminds us that as late as 1842 bathing in bathtubs was condemned as injurious, that in that year in some European cities a physician's prescription was required.

Such is the caliber of facts brought to bear against the entrenchment of medicine in the citadels of state. The activities of the American Medical Association with headquarters in Chicago and powerful contacts at the national capital and in New York are met with counter-propaganda from those groups opposed to the use of drugs. The conflict is an old one, grown sharper in late years by the vast increase in the number of supporters of manipulative and mental healing methods. Con- [76]

siderably more than a century ago Dr. Benjamin 

Rush, signer of the Declaration of Independence, wrote as follows: "The Constitution of this Re public should make provision for medical freedom as well as religious freedom. To restrict the art of healing to one class of men and deny equal privileges to others will constitute the Bastile of medical science. All such laws are un-American and despotic. They are fragments of monarchy and have no place in a republic." The famous journalist, W. T. Stead, declared that of all individuals, the physician and the priest, should have no arbitrary control over the person, by law or otherwise, and that owing to the more intimate relationship between doctor and patient than between pastor and communicant the doctor's arbitrary power was the most to be feared.

Entrenchment of Medicine 

The passage by Congress in 1930 of the Ransdell Bill, appropriating $10,000,000 for medical purposes, the Jones-Parker Bill for co-ordinating the activities and enlarging the powers of the 

United States Public Health Service, and the Har ris Cancer Bill, occasioned regret and more or less consternation on the part of anti-medical forces, to whom this governmental recognition seemed an [77]

epochal advance in orthodox healing at a period when drugs had become widely unpopular. The 

Jones-Parker Bill had come before President Coolidge during his administration, and he had vetoed it on the ground that "it gave a military status to men supposed to be engaged in scientific research."
