President Hoover, although ordinarily somewhat less orthodox in his beliefs than his predecessor, took a different view, seeming in opinion of chiropractors to cement further the union between state and medical authorities.

In Europe as well as America it has been evident that courts and legislators, but for the intervention of medical authorities, in the vast majority of cases are disposed to favor chiropractic, and, indeed, despite this intervention, have very generally acceded to the claims of the latter. No more concrete evidence of this attitude exists than in the case of People vs. Love, 298 Illinois, 304, where the opinion of the Illinois Supreme Court is as follows: 

"It is not the province of the courts to extol or belittle chiropractic, osteopathy or medicine and surgery. They are now all established as useful professions, and as time has progressed it has been thoroughly demonstrated that all of them have accomplished, and all are daily accomplishing, the relief and cure of human ailments. [78]

"Constantly comes proof before the courts that chiropractic does enable the chiropractor to relieve and cure many of the ailments of human beings, and that the practice of this science is in no way deleterious to the human body. That is the proof in this record."

The decision, as further quoted by Norman E. 

Malcolm, former city attorney of Palo Alto and president of the Chiropractic Defenders' League of California, which battled for the successful chiropractic initiative in 1922, proceeds to declare it proper to consider chiropractic as a useful and lawful business, science or profession, and as such, "property," within the meaning of the constitutional provision as to due process of law, and included in the right of liberty and the pursuit of happiness. It pronounced as arbitrary and unreasonable the requirement that letters of recommendation from two medical men accompany application for a license on the part of a chiropractor, and declared the Illinois Medical Act unconstitutional. 

Medical Jurisdiction 

The right of medical boards to regulate chiropractic has been denied time and again by high legal opinion. Judge Lansden of the Supreme Court of Tennessee in 1920 handed down this decision: [79]

The Court thinks that Chiropractors cannot be classed along with charlatans and fakirs. It is a well-developed system of healing, recognized in many jurisdictions, and many believe in its efficacy.

It is not suggested on the record that the practice of Chiropractic is in any way deleterious to the human body. 

Our statutes undertake to provide that no one shall practice the healing art until he has been examined by our various Boards and duly licensed. As a condition to obtaining license, the applicant must pursue a course of study covering many subjects. Chiropractors have no occasion to apply much of this learning.

The Court is of the opinion that since their treatments are not shown to be injurious to anybody-they do not give medicine, operate, or subject the body to injurious manipulation-the requirement that they study and be examined on subjects in no way pertaining to their occupation is an arbitrary and unreasonable attempt to restrict their liberties and the liberty of the people who wish to patronize them.* 

Such regulation has no reasonable tendency to promote the public safety and welfare. The Court recognizes fully the power of the Legislature to regulate the practice of Chiropractic by appropriate legislation. A Board may be created to do this, or the present Board empowered to regulate this profession under suitable regulations. An innocent business, however, cannot be prohibited under the guise of regulation. 

Our statutes, therefore, if they may be said to prohibit the practice of Chiropractic, are invalid to this extent. 

The Journal of the American Medical Association and Hygeia have persistently attacked chiro- 

*Italics introduced. 

[80]

practic and osteopathy, along with various healing cults. Other monthly magazines with a national circulation have from time to time joined the onslaught, prompted to some extent by an organization known as Friends of Medical Progress.* 

In the case of chiropractic the prevailing criticism has been that the theory of spinal subluxations is incorrect and that adjustments, if actually accomplished, are of doubtful importance and seldom give lasting benefit to the patient. Considerably more heat is displayed on the subject of germs and serums, where the opposing views are irreconcilable. Much capital has been made of the horde of adventurers who hoisted the chiropractic shingle during the early years of professional effort. 

War on Cults

Dr. Morris Fishbein, editor of the two organs of the medical association above named, has been chief assailant of the curative systems not allied with allopathy. The publication of his Medical 

Follies, following the work of the Frothingham investigating committee in Boston in 1922, helped to foment the ill humors of the drugless healing issue, which for a generation had been developing *Formed in Boston, 1923. The name later became The American 

Association for Medical Progress. [81]

in every community of the United States and had become the chief note of alarm in the medical journals of the country. Notwithstanding a growing tolerance toward mental treatment and osteopathy on the part of medical doctors, Dr. Fishbein, as spokesman for the American Medical Association, has vehemently denounced all rival systems, categorically repudiating the principle of elective treatment. At the annual congress of the American 

Medical Association in February, 1930, he advocated combining the powers of the state medical examiners into a federal licensing board. 

In the midst of its long war on nostrums, the medical fraternity found itself faced with a serious defection caused by healing cults. Hundreds of thousands of persons of every age had adopted Christian Science, osteopathy, chiropractic, New Thought and other non-medical methods. Even psycho-analysis loomed as a possible adversary. The number of persons who had abandoned medicine was estimated at forty millions in an article appearing in the Journal of the Michigan State Medical Society, August, 1923. In December of that year 

Dr. E. C. Levy, director of public welfare at Richmond, Virginia, stated in an address printed in the 

American Journal of Public Health: "In spite of the fact that regular medical practice today is com- [82]

paratively superior to what it has ever been, nevertheless there has never been a time when the people had less confidence in it." Dr. George F. Butler, president of the Rockefeller Foundation, told the lay members of the American Association for Medical Progress on the occasion of its first meeting: "The world has been waiting for this society, and it must be backed to the limit, or the medical profession in this country will be swamped by the cults and societies ranged against it." 

More than a mere idle disaffection on the part of the public was apparent, for an active propaganda against medicine existed. Every utterance of physicians that could be construed as adversely critical to the medical profession seemed to be seized as evidence, to find publicity in Physical Culture, the journals of chiropractic and osteopathy and other printed mediums as well as through the spoken lectures of mental healers. When such eminent physicians as Dr. William Osler and Dr. Richard C. 

Cabot declared drugs for the most part worthless,* * "Most drugs have no curative effect whatever on the diseases for which they are administered."-Modern Medicine, by William Osler. Dr. 

Cabot of Harvard Medical School is accredited with the statement: "Every educated physician knows that most diseases are not appreciably helped by drugs." Dr. Osler further states: "We do not now feel under obligation to give any drugs at all, except where the patient's attitude, or the attitude of his family makes its expedient, in order to create in him the buoyant expectancy which is the real curative agent." [83]

the medical profession appeared to be threatened by disruption from within. "A rapidly growing recognition upon the part of physicians generally that it is a waste of effort to attempt to compel the people to accept 'regular' medical treatment if they regard some other form of treatment as more efficacious" is the cause given by the Citizens' Medical Reference Bureau of New 

York for the failure of the twenty years' "purely scientific program" forced on the people. The latter quote is from Dr. Irving Arthur, whose address at the instance of the Gibson County Medical Society was published in the Indiana State Medical 

Association Journal. 

"It is generally conceded that the medical profession is losing its grip upon the people," said Dr. 

Arthur. "Our medical laws are being repudiated by trial juries, and state legislators refuse to enact more stringent laws to regulate the healing art. 

The people of California by popular vote have refused to recognize the medical practice act of that state. ... "The people of this country are demanding of the medical profession something more than shaking up test tubes and looking through microscopes. 

The thing that they demand most of all when they [84]

are sick is service, and if they cannot get it from the medical profession they will get it somewhere else."* 

Other signs of dissension became evident with the increase of publicity about the perils of vaccines and tonsillar operations. The accusers were joined by many medical men. The anti-vivisectionists likewise found support among physicians and surgeons, among whom Dr. Forbes Winslow authoritatively denounced the wholesale killing and torture of animals in the laboratories as useless and barbarous.

In England the defection from medicine has rapidly increased. The Illustrated Daily News of 

Los Angeles, in its issue of December 31, 1930, published a significant letter from the 807-yearold Royal Hospital of St. Bartholomew, London, asking financial aid. The letterhead bore the leg end "Patron: H.M. The King. President: H.R.H. 

The Prince of Wales, K.G.," and, in part, stated: "This Christmas, our South Block with 210 beds will be empty. Closed for the first time in 

200 years, this block, long since obsolete and unsuited to modern medical requirements, cannot be again brought into service until funds are forth- * Niagara Falls (N. Y.) Gazette, of December 11, 1929. [85]

coming for its reconstruction. But, first, the burdensome debt of 90,000 pounds still remaining on our new surgical block, with its six modern major operation theatre suits, must be discharged. "Thus our mother hospital of the Englishspeaking nations, with its unparalleled record of pioneers in medical progress. . . is compelled for the first time in its 807 years' history, to make a universal call for help." Propaganda Who is the defender and who the aggressor is often a problem which entails a study of original rights. That the waxing efforts of the medical authorities to educate the public through health crusades were largely actuated by motives of defense is the opinion of the most impartial critics. Mrs. 

Annie Riley Hale, who is not at all impartial, in her denunciatory book, These Cults, refers to the American Medical Association as "the most colossal, the most all-inclusive publicity machine ever known." Apropos of this opinion appear reports by Dr. Ray G. Hulbert of Chicago, chairman of the Osteopathic Publicity Committee, in the Journal of the American Osteopathic Association, issues of January, February and March, 1925. These re- [86]

veal the scope of medical propaganda and echo the challenge so widely sounded among chiropractic and osteopathic periodicals. In part the statement is as follows:

Medical "education" of the public is progressing fast. 

The plans of the American Medical Association are growing more numerous, more complicated, more efficient. There is no use trying to stop them. We could adopt some of them to advantage. Some of the propaganda is misleading, false, sinister. To a certain extent, we can oppose and thwart these parts of it. No matter what we mean to do about the campaign as a whole, or its various details, we need to know the facts.

The A. M. A. maintains a far-flung battle line, fighting both offensively and defensively. State societies are supplementing its work. Health officers and health boards of states, counties, and cities are doing their part. Individual physicians are fighting in the ranks, in accordance with the plans of various organizations. Others are doing free lance work by supplying newspapers with syndicated "health" columns, writing books and pamphlets. 

And some are using guerilla tactics, writing and lecturing against "irregular" methods and practitioners. Most of these pay scant attention to facts or truth. The secretaries of the California and the Colorado medical examining boards have been urging for several years "a constructive, educational campaign" in self-defense against chiropractors and others who were encroaching too fast. At the 1921 Convention of the A. M. A., the speaker of the house of delegates made a strong plea for impersonal medi- [87]

cal publicity, and detailed several plans which he considered practical for the organization to undertake. The president of the A. M. A. spoke for the idea, as did other leaders. Many medical magazines published favorable editorials. By 1922, the speaker of the A. M. A. house of delegates advocated the appropriation of $75,000 to defray the expenses of an active and constructive plan of public health education. He urged action. He mentioned "the demand, the need, the urgency for the discontinuance of further temporizing methods," etc.... 

In another year Hygeia was launched and carrying the message of the allopaths into the homes and schools of the nation. Abstracts of articles in this magazine are supplied to newspapers all over the country, linking the thought of health with the thought of allopathic methods wherever possible. Every school superintendent in the United States has been or is to be circularized on Hygeia as a school proposition. School officials of New York City circulated 28,000 copies of one issue to their teachers. The A. M. A. took advantage of the recent flurry over physical examinations of school children in Chicago, to stimulate the circulation of Hygeia among people interested in schools everywhere. In at least one State, it is reported that Hygeia is being sent to all members of the Legislature. This is probably the case wherever legislative fights are in prospect. These are only samples of the ramifications of effort to advertise and boost the methods and theories of Allopathy. 

Opponents of medicine have been quick to declare such medical propaganda to be indirect advertising calculated to benefit medical doctors fi- [88]

nancially, and thus completely at variance with the medical code of ethics which forbids indirect advertising.*

A more drastic criticism of organized medicine appears in the C. C. A. Bulletin of March, 1930, under the title "The Medical Menace." This is the organ of the California Chiropractic Association.

In most every state and place where chiropractic has earned and secured legal recognition, it has been compelled to overcome the effect of organized medicine. In California, after many weary years of legislative effort, preceded by persecution that the founders of our democracy never anticipated, the pioneers were compelled to turn to the direct vote of the public for some semblance of justice and fair play, and thereby secured legal recognition that politicians are not always at liberty to grant. 

The cost in dollars actually spent was far in excess of a hundred thousand. The time and effort devoted to this stupendous task by Chiropractors and their friends can never be computed. Yet those fundamental and economic enemies of Chiropractic are not content to abide by the decision of the people, and are attempting in devious ways, to accomplish their desires for the extermination of Chiropractic. 

Many States have had Chiropractic laws that were equitable only in part for Chiropractors and humanity. And many of the youngest Chiropractors have seen those laws and the will of the people swept aside by clever political lobby- * "It is equally unprofessional to procure patients by indirection, by indirect advertising, or by furnishing or inspiring newspaper or magazine comments concerning cases in which the physician has been or is concerned."-Principles of Medical Ethics. [89]

ing of the medical interests, augmented by the enormous profits from the sale of dope, or medicine, whichever you choose to call it, and which is at this moment slaying over forty per cent of those who should be most conversant with its potency. 

When a direct attack on Chiropractic laws was interpreted and combated by public opinion, these medical Gauls instituted a basic science, a board of regents, board of educational standards or what have you, to becloud the issues in order to hide from the public gaze designs that are selfish and little less than murderous.

In California one of those monstrosities was provided, not to determine the essential value of Chiropractic or other healing methods to the citizens of our great commonwealth, but to try to accomplish what all the medical-political forces failed to do in November, 1922, namely a "Board of Vocational Standards." But sad to relate this even was not capable of reaching and strangling Chiropractic, because the State constitution provides that an initiative act by the vote of the people cannot be changed or altered by any legislative enactment. And what the medical forces failed to realize when they ordered their chore-boys to provide this "board of vocational standards" was that a legislative enactment could not change the intent of our law. Therefore, unlike our people, in that they are never tiring in their efforts to gain that to which they have no right, they set about pledging their hirelings to a revision of our State constitution. For by this means they are assured of success in reaching and accomplishing the nullification of the Chiropractic law. 

Certain it is that if the California Chiropractic initiative can be destroyed nothing is impossible to these traducers of right and justice. And certain it is that they will accom- [90]

plish their ultimate aim if their secret agents by trick or device can fill the mouths and minds of sufficient of our fellow Chiropractors with the propaganda that everything is well with Chiropractic in California. . . .

The foregoing is cited as an example of the many bitter denunciations directed at the medical profession. Between antagonistic groups devoted to the preservation of human life are thundered accusations of a virulence more political than professional. Scarcely an issue of any of the score of chiropractic journals but resounds with protest and censure. The year 1930, while bringing new hope to American chiropractors because of the amalgamation of large organizations and a general strengthening of the united front, nevertheless held in store sensational disappointments such as the passage of the before-mentioned Ransdell Bill, the Jones-Parker Bill and the Harris Cancer Bill. 

The Rockefeller contribution of a million dollars for negro hospitals, the appropriation of $15,980,- 000 for the United States Veterans' Hospital program, and state projects involving $8,000,000 for veterans' hospitals, while not one dollar was in evidence for the building of chiropractic sanitariums, occasioned scornful comment in the chiropractic press. [91]

Disintegration 

August Andrew Erz, in his forthright little book, The Verdict of Science on Chiropractic,* 

predicts the doom of official medicine. He declares the house of medicine is divided against itself and in the last stage of disintegration; that modern medicine amounts to a conglomeration of medicalized cults, each insisting upon special therapeutic claims. Among these he indicates "the drug and knife cult," the dietetists, vaccination, innoculation, serum, antitoxin, organo-therapy, vivisection, gland transplantation, hydro-, electro-, radio-, and mechano-therapy cults, forming minor divisions of the "main sects": the allopathic, homeopathic, eclectic and physio-medical. After referring to the medical trust engaged in the manufacture of drugs, serums, surgical instruments, etc., Dr. Erz observes, that when a profession enjoys the monopoly of the healing art, then feels authority dwindling, all sorts of despicable expedients are practiced. He declares the insincerity of official medicine betrayed by the tactics employed in opposition to chiropractic. "State medicine" he pronounces an outlandish institution as repugnant to the American mind as "state church." This critic 

*Bureau of Special Research, San Francisco, 1925. 

[92]

maintains that medical officials have no more right to examine the fitness of chiropractors than Catholic theologians would have over candidates for the Protestant ministry. He declares medical doctors unqualified to decide upon chiropractors, because in the first place they have only contempt for chiropractic, and second, they usually know nothing of the principles of chiropractic. He pronounces the examination by doctors of medicine "a farce and humbug," since the questions put to applicants have nothing to do with chiropractic but to general subjects unrelated thereto, and the certificates issued entitle the bearers to practice any method of drugless healing. Reference, of course, is made to conditions in states where no special board of chiropractic examiners exists. 

Chiropractors who have sought credentials at the hands of medical boards have met with severe condemnation by their colleagues, and, as a rule, wherever the profession is represented extensively by men and women who have thus received their licenses, organization has lacked cohesiveness and considerable friction has been in evidence. 

Against the medical charges of incompetency, illegitimacy and the employment of unscientific methods, chiropractic has imposed a formidable defense. In upholding the constitutional rights of [93]

the individual a direct challenge to the authority of medicine has been issued. The war has been waged over fundamentals. Chiropractic as invader and not secessionist, has strengthened its claim by raising a question of title. Without heritage of gain but with new weapons and an unknown standard, it has summoned all disbelievers to aid its cause on promise of a better wage of health. In eighteen years all states but a small group have admitted the newcomer and acknowledged in some degree his right to compete in the great arena of public recognition. The fact of traditional errors in medical practice, perennial contradictions, and the inherent uncertainties of all healing, but most of all, concretely, a widely growing prejudice against drugs, have accounted for much of the indulgence shown the chiropractor. Less amenable than the osteopath to medical authority, yet he has profited by the status of osteopathy, won mainly through concessions which chiropractors are now able to refuse. But the conflict between the extremely antagonistic theories of allopathy and chiropractic is far from being ended, since actual autonomy exists as yet in less than half of the states of the Union. [94]

CHAPTER VI How Chiropractic Stands in Various States of the Union HIROPRACTIC has attained a general varied recognition and is practiced throughout the United States. The first law giving recognition and autonomy to chiropractic was passed in Kansas in 1913. Its provisions did not become effective for many months, so that Arkansas, where subsequent legislation had occurred, was the first state actually to issue licenses to chiropractors. 

With respect to the similarity of their laws covering the practice of chiropractic, the states of the Union may be divided into five groups. In the following twenty-three states the chiropractic profession is permitted to govern its own affairs, although the laws differ in their requirements: Arizona, California, Florida, Georgia, Idaho, Iowa, Kansas, Kentucky, Maine, Maryland, Missouri, Montana, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Mexico, North 

Carolina, North Dakota, Oklahoma, Oregon, South 

Dakota, Tennessee, Vermont and Wyoming. [95]

In six states applicants for chiropractic licenses are required at the instance of medical boards to pass examinations in the "basic sciences," such as physiology, anatomy, pathology and chemistry, and in some instances bacteriology, diagnosis, toxicology, etc., as interpreted medically. This law has tended to exclude the majority of chiropractors, osteopaths, sanipractors and drugless physicians. 

The states where it is enforced are Arkansas, Connecticut, Minnesota, Nebraska, Washington and

Wisconsin. It obtains also in the District of Columbia. In eight states no recognition of chiropractic exists, namely: Alabama, Delaware, Massachusetts, Mississippi, New York, Pennsylvania,

South Carolina and Texas. In four of these states, Alabama, Delaware, Mississippi and South Carolina, chiropractors are allowed to practice by virtue of the fact that the medical laws contain no provisions by which the pursuit of their calling is construed as being the practice of medicine. Massachusetts, New York, Pennsylvania and Texas are considered as "open states," affording at least a theoretical immunity, but through court applications and technicalities a chiropractor is frequently adjudged to practice medicine.

Five states have mixed boards of examiners, the physicians outnumbering the chiropractors. Not [96]

only must the applicant prove conversant with the principles of chiropractic science, art and philosophy, as medically interpreted, but he must also pass an examination in some exclusively medical subjects. It is therefore difficult to get a license in these states: Indiana, New Jersey, Ohio, Rhode Island and West Virginia, and many chiropractors refuse to observe the requirements. New Jersey had a chiropractic examining board about 1922, but the law providing for it was repealed through the efforts of the medical organization. In Indiana a single chiropractor sits with the medical board and the requirements are sufficiently difficult to cause it to be regarded as a closed state. 

Four states, Colorado, Illinois, Michigan and Virginia, grant licenses through medical boards upon chiropractic credentials. In Colorado if an applicant proves graduation from a recognized, reputable, legally chartered college of chiropractic providing a course of not less than three years, a license is issued. There is co-operation between the medical boards and chiropractic associations in these states, the examining boards of the chiropractic associations making recommendations to the medical boards. However, notably in Illinois, evasion and outlawry exist, militating against chiropractic standards. [97]

Utah has a board of chiropractic examiners appointed by the governor and superintended by a state bureau of licensure. Examinations are held twice a year. Conditions are not deemed favorable and it is believed that there are not more than thirty chiropractors in the entire state. 

Louisiana Restrictions

In Louisiana so drastic are the restrictions that at the present time the profession is barely represented within its boundaries. The state constitution declares that any person who diagnoses or treats or attempts to cure any disease or deformity of any human being by any method or modality or other means shall be considered as practicing medicine and must secure a license from the state board of medical examiners in order to so practice. In addition, the medical law states that the court shall issue an injunction of both temporary and permanent nature in event of the violation of the act. The Supreme Court of Louisiana and the United States Supreme Court (State vs. Walter W. Fife, 1927) have held the practice of chiropractic to constitute the practice of medicine within the meaning of this act. 

If a chiropractor opens an office in Louisiana he is arrested and jailed without trial upon the com- [98]

plaint of any person to the court, parish or district, and the court is compelled under the decision of the two higher courts to issue a temporary injunction. If the chiropractor sees fit to adhere to the ruling a date is set for a hearing to determine if the injunction shall be made permanent. If the defendant persists in practicing he is imprisoned for contempt of court. The law has been rigidly enforced for many years. Dr. Harry Gallaher, secretary of the International Chiropractic Con gress, was arrested twenty-seven times between 1914 and 1919. He was the only chiropractor who succeeded in remaining in the state for such a period of time.

A survey of the five groups of states, which do not include the separately considered Utah and Louisiana, indicates that chiropractic is more strongly established in the West and South, although six of the central states give full recognition. In the East the position is weakest; however, four states, of which three are in New England, have accorded full recognition. Autonomy exists throughout the Pacific and Mountain states, with the exception of Washington, where the "basic science" law is in force.

The uphill fight of chiropractic-assailed continuously on all sides, weakened by disaffection, [99]

apathy and discord, betrayed by its mercenaries and almost overwhelmed by strongly entrenched opposition-from its earliest days has been one of the most extraordinary conflicts in the annals of science. Its victory is far from complete. Though its standards have been fixed on many an eminence, the battle still rages on far-distant fronts. New 

York, Ohio and Texas continue to reveal a perennial battle-small groups, half-organized and poorly equipped being engaged in a guerilla warfare against a mighty legion.

The conflict throughout America has been much the same. To glimpse the career of chiropractic in a few representative states is to understand its life struggle. 

The Oklahoma Contest 

Oklahoma presents a chiropractic autonomy and freedom from interference unexceeded by any of the twenty-three states mentioned in the same category. Recognition was won in 1921 after fourteen years of effort, much of which was directed against discriminative legislation that encouraged outlawry.

In 1907 Oklahoma was admitted to the Union and in the autumn of that year Dr. Willard Car- [100]

ver, present head of the Carver Chiropractic Col lege at Oklahoma City, with Dr. T. F. Ratledge and others, prepared and submitted to the legislature a bill designed to regulate the practice of chiropractic. The previous year a similar measure had passed the legislature of Minnesota but had been vetoed by the governor. No previous chiropractic law had ever been proposed anywhere.

This was still the infant period of the profession, for the Palmer school had been actively in existence only four years and the Carver-Denny 

Chiropractic College barely two.

This bill was placed in the hands of the judiciary committee, which examined many lay witnesses who testified to cures through chiropractic. 

The evidence pertained to the most serious diseases. The bill reached the Senate, which expressed approval by a considerable majority, but upon reconsideration defeated it by a single vote. The measure came before a Senate committee again, following its passage in the House. In the meantime a medical bill, known as Senate Bill No. 189, appeared, requiring the attention of Dr. Carver and his colleagues. The former lawyer succeeded in having certain clauses detrimental to chiropractic altered, but shortly after the adjournment of the legislature in May, 1908, Dr. Carver discovered [101]

that Bill No. 189 as printed did not contain his amendments. When the clerk in the office of the 

Secretary of State was unable to find the original bill, the chiropractor asked permission to join the search. He discovered the document among some waste paper consigned to the janitor, and after having it photographed returned it to the capitol to be engrossed correctly. 

The approval of Bill No. 189 by Governor 

Charles N. Haskell spelled immunity in some degree for chiropractors and compensated partly for the failure of the chiropractic bill of the previous year. That Governor Haskell was favorable toward chiropractic was evident by the fact that he and members of his family had received treatments by Dr. Ratledge, who was on the governor's staff at that time and held the honorary rank of colonel. During the latter part of his activities in 

Oklahoma, Dr. Ratledge was the only chiropractor permitted to enter the Senate after numerous signers of an allegedly scurrilous petition circulated by Dr. Carver were excluded from the Chamber.

Upon evidence that Dr. Ratledge's name had been affixed without his knowledge it was expunged from the record. Thus he was enabled to continue contact work in the legislature in behalf of the cause he served. [102]

A half year's attendance upon the Oklahoma legislature by Dr. Carver, Colonel George M. 

Masterson, manager of the chiropractic campaign for recognition; Captain F. M. Posegate, writer, and others, was not without favorable results. But chiropractic needed organization. Rivalries threatened to disrupt the legislative program and complete accord on policies was far from being realized. L. L. Denny, the associate of Dr. Carver for two years, joined with him in an unremitting expenditure of time and money, prior to the former's resignation in 1908 from the college which he had raised money to establish. The previous year he had been chosen first president of the Oklahoma Chiropractic Association, which took up the fight for legal protection. Between 1912 and 1918 it was known after reorganization as the Oklahoma 

State Association of Doctors of Chiropractic. Later it readopted the original name. 

Toward the end of this period there came up for discussion Senate Bill No. 111, devised to check chiropractors by compulsory examination in various medical subjects, at the hands of the medical board, at the same time restricting their practice to drugless methods. Despite all efforts the bill passed both houses and was signed by the governor, but it was destined to emancipate rather than abolish [103]

chiropractic. Taking advantage of the ninety days which must elapse before the law would be in effect, the Oklahoma Chiropractic Association, led by its president, Dr. W. R. Gorby, Dr. Carver, Dr.

John C. Hubbard and others circulated a referendum petition asking that the law be not approved by the voters of the state. The required number of signatures were received, whereupon the medical association demanded of the Secretary of State that the citizenship credentials of all signers be examined. This was done and the validity of the petition assured. The election of 1918 passed without the question coming before the people, but Governor Robertson acknowledged the petition in 1920 and it appeared on the November ballot. 

Campaigning developed tensely in the camps of the medical and chiropractic associations. Both sides appealed to the public through printed advertisements and such other publicity as they could command. Mass meetings were held in several cities at which the case of chiropractic was made clear. 

It was a protest against a law which would require chiropractors to be examined in medical subjects, since they not only had nothing in common with medicine but were opposed to its use. The long and impressive record of benefited cases was put forth; the very fact of the petition cited as proof [104]

of public gratitude. Should the multitudes who had placed their faith in chiropractic and had been restored to health be now compelled to renounce chiropractic? Was it consistent with American freedom that an individual be prevented from choosing such means as he thought best in order to save his life?

This strange contest between rival professions over the right to administer to the sick resulted in a victory for the chiropractors by a majority of 46,000 votes. A signal success capable of influencing the cause in other states, nevertheless it was defensive and not aggressive-a final stand to retain a precarious position. Yet in this resistance the new science had been made a public issue and many thousands of citizens had become informed as to its merits. Unquestionably a greater following had been created.

The next year the legislative committee of the Oklahoma Chiropractic Association introduced a bill to establish the right to practice independently of medical control. On this committee were Drs. 

W. R. Gorby, H. B. Hoppe, E. M. Hurry, Harry 

Gallaher, and Judge George S. Evans. Dr. Gallaher, who had waged a lone battle for five years against the Louisiana law, with Dr. P. E. Courtney of Enid was delegated to the capitol. [105]

The Senate approved the chiropractic bill by a considerable majority. There was delay in having its duplicate submitted on the floor of the House, whose committee chairman was allied with the medical profession, and the House had to call for the bill. Its passage and approval by Governor 

Robertson ended the fourteen years of struggle for the recognition of chiropractic. The board of chiropractic examiners, forthwith appointed by the governor, comprised Dr. C. Sterling Cooley, of Tulsa, president; Dr. Harry Gallaher, of Guthrie, secretary, and Dr. Curtis J. Lee, of Oklahoma 

City, treasurer.

As the new law was retroactive, compelling all chiropractors to take an examination and not merely applying to a new generation of practitioners, it resulted in ridding the profession of a large number of imposters. Prior to this any person with a few months or weeks of training in chiropractic could open an office and accept patients. 

In 1921 there were 553 practitioners in the state. 

When it became rumored that they were to be examined, about one-fifth promptly migrated. Of the survivors 327 appeared at the Irving School in 

Oklahoma City on August 29, 1921, and spent six days writing on thirteen subjects. The board of examiners after a six-weeks' session devoted to [106]

reading papers found 286 of the applicants qualified to continue in their profession. By 1930 approximately 700 licenses had been issued in the state of Oklahoma.

The status of the chiropractor in Oklahoma now so notably secure was achieved only after years of contest, during which time repeated obstacles, apparently insuperable at times, were reared against the cause. In the retrospect it furnishes many a lively episode in legislative and professional records.

Not the least sensational was the arrest and imprisonment of Dr. Willard Carver. This chiropractic pioneer in 1917 wrote an article published in a small daily paper declaring that the physicians, druggists and osteopaths of the state had been assessed to carry on the campaign against chiropractic. He raised the question whether members of the Senate had been bribed and was accordingly brought before that body and charged with contempt. Pronounced guilty, ten days in the county jail and a fine of $500 was the judgment. An order for his release was obtained before the expiration of the sentence and the fine was permitted to go unpaid. [107]

"Basic Sciences"

Concerning the second group of states where the "basic science" law is in force, conditions have thereby become exceedingly difficult for the chiropractor. Although the subjects required under the examinations are now included in the courses of the chiropractic schools, the medical interpretations of these subjects exclude a very large percentage of chiropractic applicants.

The "basic science" law is generally regarded in the chiropractic profession as an expedential enactment due to the difficulty in construing the practice of chiropractic as that of medicine. It is viewed as a roundabout attempt to accomplish the exclusion of chiropractors.

In Nebraska and Washington, where the "basic science" law went into effect in 1926, no licenses have since been issued to chiropractors. The first chiropractic law in Nebraska was passed in 1915, and until January 1, 1916, the doctors of chiropractic then practicing in the state were licensed by exemption. A course of three years of nine months each was required until 1924 when the time requirement was dispensed with and qualifications of applicants rested solely with the examining board. Conditions in this state were gener- [108]

ally regarded as favorable to chiropractic until the passage of the "basic science" law two years later, and Nebraska is now typical of the states where this law is in force, the chiropractor's chances of qualifying in all the required subjects of two professions being exceedingly unfavorable. All entrants for examinations must now present a certificate showing a completed course in chiropractic of three years of nine months each.

Between 1916 and 1926 the average number of licenses issued yearly was about thirty. At present there are probably not more than 325 chiropractors practicing in Nebraska, and it is estimated that approximately 100 who hold Nebraska licenses carry on their work in other states where public and official attitudes are more favorable. 

The resident practitioners are hopeful of having the "basic science" law repealed within a year or two. The Nebraska Chiropractic Association has been an active force in advancing the interests of the profession and in 1930 was considerably heartened by a decision of the state attorney general by which recognition to chiropractic was accorded on the part of the industrial insurance department. 

In the state of Washington a contrary ruling occurred. 

The fact of a provision for licensing chiro- [109]

practors and osteopaths under the "basic science" law is regarded as amounting to a recognition of the two professions as health agencies despite the barrier imposed by the difficult examinations, and accordingly has given rise to a continual protest on the grounds of prejudice and unfair discrimination, notably in view of public moneys being expended for medical care but not for chiropractic or osteopathic facilities. 

New York 

Among the "open states," New York and Texas represent particularly hard-fought battlegrounds. Recognition and compromise up to the present time have not been gained, and the chiropractor remains outside the pale of legal protection. Not included by law among medical practitioners, yet as such he is susceptible to arrest and penalties through technicalities. The charge of "practicing medicine without a license" is as familiar in New York state, consequently, as in the states where medical boards exercise authority over chiropractic. 

Considerable effort to influence public opinion in behalf of chiropractic has been organized during the last five years, especially under the guidance of the American Bureau of Chiropractic, the head- [110]

quarters of which are at 100 Fifth Avenue, New

York. Represented in forty-two states and in six foreign countries, it has put forth a protest against alleged medical monopoly and persecution and declared the right of any individual to select the doctor of his choice. Through Chiropractic News, published weekly at the address named, a large number of testimonials have appeared in connection with reports of meetings of auxiliaries in various parts of the country.

That chiropractic is gaining attention in New York as a public issue is indicated by the publicity given to the arrest of Dr. J. Acman Holland, of 

2331 Seventh Avenue, in the summer of 1930.

Protest meetings of a large number of patients occurred, at which the science of chiropractic was pronounced one of the greatest blessings to humanity and the work of Dr. Holland upheld. One speaker said he would travel from Maine to California to tell the truth about chiropractic. A front page article of two columns appeared in a local newspaper reporting the protest and including a resolution by Auxiliary No. 22 of the American 

Bureau of Chiropractic, which in part stated: "This persecution of chiropractors by the medical profession under the disguise of the State Educational Law, should be stopped. . How can [111]

anybody conceive the idea that a person applying bare hands to the spinal column can be charged with practicing medicine? . . . Such a charge does not tend to benefit the community, it is detrimental and destructive; its purpose is to keep the world in ignorance of the merits of chiropractic.” 

The arrest of Dr. C. P. Eifertsen, vice president of the American Bureau of Chiropractic, proved the signal for additional protest that reached the utmost boundaries of the chiropractic world. This imprisonment reacted immeasurably to the fame of the imprisoned, as Dr. Eifertsen was declared to have relieved one of his keepers of blindness in one eye.

Simultaneously with this report the Chiropractic News mentioned in its issue of September 11, 1930, the testimonials of cures of tuberculosis, sleeping sickness, psoriasis, the restoration of the voice of a well-known Metropolitan Opera star, the cure of chronic bronchitis of a priest and of the notable case of Henry Wuchner, said to be the fourth man to survive tubercular spinal meningitis. Cases of cancer of the liver and quinsy, and sworn testimony from many parts of the world, were submitted, all a fragment of the data employed in a direct mail campaign to acquaint the public with the achievements of chiropractic. [112]

The American Bureau of Chiropractic, largely composed of laymen beneficiaries, has concentrated upon New York in the wide distribution of its appeal. Its editorial forces have bitterly attacked the position of the medical authorities, as witnessed by an article in the Chiropractic News, October 9, 1930, of which the introductory paragraph reads in part: "Persons no longer submit humbly, meekly and entirely to the hocus-pocus of needless operations and the drugging of their bodies with apothecaries' mixtures. That awful record of nineteen million deaths a year from disease, not from old age, nor accident, is confronting the medical profession with the most serious indictment, that, as far as the health of the world is concerned, medicine . . . is a flat failure."

This is illustrative, not only of the attitude of this organ of publicity, but of practically all chiropractic publications and organizations. Encouraged by the loyal adherence of patients, by a gradually improved status, despite thirty years of excommunication, the leaders of chiropractic, conscious of a complete divergence of belief, declared their independence of medical authority. Claiming to have saved countless lives, to have brought relief and happiness to millions of homes, the or- [113]

ganized strength of chiropractic has sought to reach the legislator through his constituents and finally to bring about a universal recognition commensurate with the dignity of science. 

In no state have practitioners been more continuously harassed by investigators than in New York. A nightmare of insecurity and suspicion has prevailed since the first arrests more than twenty years ago, when sleuths masquerading as patients began to seek evidence of illegal practice. 

This came at the time when a crusade had been launched against palmists, clairvoyants and masseurs, and Sixth Avenue, between Twenty-third and Fifty-ninth streets, witnessed the extermination of scores of objectionable establishments bordering on the elevated railroad route. Current opinion was justly aroused against charlatanry, and the apprehension of some chiropractors, many indeed, unworthy of the name, served to class all chiropractors with the disreputable element. The 

New York newspapers, containing frequent items about arrests, and circulating throughout America and abroad, were responsible for much of the early prejudice against chiropractic. It had also inherited the skepticism shown towards osteopathy. [114]

A Philadelphia Skirmish 

The attack of the Philadelphia Record in 1930 reflected this old stigma, and the chiropractors of Pennsylvania, lodged under the same liabilities as those of New York, rose to protest with the cry of "Health freedom."

Following the investigations by two young reporters and two girls, representing themselves as married couples, The Record published an alleged canard denouncing all unlicensed healers as quacks.

The Chiropractic News responded, showing the circulation of The Record to be only 68,360, and reprinting a card sent to the physicians of Philadelphia presumably for the purpose of increasing the sale of the paper. The card contained an exposure based on "months of exhaustive investigation by a corps of investigators" and was entitled "Bootlegging the Healing Arts." It declared the authorities powerless to check the evil under the Medical Practice Act and advised the doctors to purchase The Record to learn the whole story. 

The Philadelphia chiropractors acclaimed this newspaper attack as a stupid attempt at persecution. Attention was called to the fact that medical quacks had not been included in the affront, which was dubbed hypocritical and destined to act as a [115]

boomerang. Certainly it furnished a theme for chiropractors' meetings in many parts of America and swelled the chorus against "medical monopoly." Of the hundreds of chiropractors in Philadelphia only a few were arrested, and much difficulty ensued in trying to prove they had treated merely mythical ailments. 

The conflict in Texas has been perpetual, despite the increasing recognition in nearly all of the neighboring states. Organization of chiropractors, seen as the only means of gaining legislation, has proceeded slowly under the difficulties incidental to a lack of funds, yet a persistent effort has been made to find friends in the legislature. Ladies' auxiliary associations have been specially active, as in Minnesota and Kentucky.

Scarcely more favorable are conditions in the states having examining boards composed both of doctors of medicine and chiropractic, for the burden of mastering medical subjects in addition to the three or four-year chiropractic courses suffices to bar the great majority of applicants. Ohio, where early sounded the slogan "Go to jail for chiropractic," typifies a condition where the chiropractor must choose between the almost futile quest of a license and virtual outlawry. He is eternally faced with the possibility of being obliged to [116]

prove that he has not been practicing medicine.

The situation in the five states of this group is far from conducive to betterment of chiropractic standards, even though the law is ostensibly created for that purpose.

Vastly different are the conditions in Alabama and Mississippi, where no more recognition exists than in New York or Texas, but where, nevertheless, chiropractors conduct their work without molestation. 

In the fifth group of states, Colorado, Illinois, Michigan, and Virginia, where licenses are granted through medical boards upon adequate chiropractic credentials, and more or less co-operation between medical and chiropractic authorities exists, it may be serviceable to glimpse the record and present status of Michigan. Michigan

Here re-echoed as in practically every state the denunciation of chiropractic as a fraud upon the public. Recognition, or compromise, was ignored by the medical board or state authorities. The only hope of acquiring recognition lay in organization, which had already come into existence in other states by 1913. Then an amendment to the medical law in Michigan was introduced. [117]

By this measure all drugless healers were brought under medical supervision, but those who had been in practice for two years prior to September 1 of that year were rendered exempt from the provisions of the act and accorded a license. 

By this exemption clause 135 practitioners received recognition. The board of medical examiners, however, for ten years refused to permit any chiropractor to be examined, during which period there was presented biennially to the legislature a bill asking for recognition.

The number of chiropractors in the state had considerably increased despite the discrimination against the profession, and a strengthened state organization in 1921 and 1923 instituted vigorous campaigns to secure the passage of the bill. It was defeated in the legislature on each occasion by a narrow margin.

Foreseeing an eventual recognition of chiropractic the medical authorities realized that only the weakening of the organized ranks could check progress. Prosecutions on the score of "practicing medicine without a license" were not very effective in the courts.

There appeared in Detroit the so-called Alma 

College of Chiropractic. It supplied a four-year course of 160 hours, embracing high school sub- [118]

jects and purporting to qualify students to take a medical examination. Graduates holding the equivalent of a high school diploma were permitted to undertake the medical board's test, and a considerable number of licenses to practice chiropractic were issued. The holders of these credentials were organized into the Michigan Registered Chiropractors Association. In the meantime it was discovered that the head of Alma College of Chiropractic was a doctor of medicine.

Many chiropractors, who had had no affiliation with Alma College, had been induced to lend support to the organized activities of the Michigan Registered Chiropractors Association, and unwittingly contributed funds for propaganda tending to strengthen medical control. Believing themselves duped, they formed in 1928 an organization called the Chiropractors of Michigan. A new campaign was fostered, but its control passed into the hands of the presidents of the association and the college, with the result that the career of chiropractic in Michigan nearly foundered on the "basic science" laws. This might have happened but for a small group of the old line chiropractors who desperately fought the proposed legislation. 

The situation in Michigan differs from Colorado in that credentials in the former state are less [119]

indicative of a purely chiropractic rating. It is typical of those states where, it is charged, licenses have been gotten at the price of subversion. Unrepresentative authority has sprung up time after time in the profession of chiropractic, whose antagonists have allied themselves with disaffected elements as a means of ultimately discountenancing the entire practice. [120]

CHAPTER VII 

The Struggle in California 

IN CALIFORNIA are approximately one-fifth of the licensed chiropractors of the United

States, and in Southern California chiropractors are more numerous than in any other part of the world. In Los Angeles County alone are more than 1,200 members of the profession. The fight for recognition in this state covered a period of eleven years, from 1911 to 1922. Nowhere was the conflict with medical authorities more intense, nor the suppression of chiropractors pursued with a more relentless policy. Until the Chiropractic Act became a reality in 1922 and provision was made for a board of chiropractic examiners, a perpetual battle was under way, with apparently little chance of victory for the outnumbered crusaders. It is a tale of stratagems and spoils, of political intrigue and reprisal. Attacked on all sides, weakened by defection within the ranks and hundreds sent to prison, only an appeal to the public through the launching of an initiative petition finally won success. Then began a new battle [121]

to hold the position gained-to function according to law.

In 1908 there were probably less than ten chiropractors in the state. Exponents of various healing arts, including some of these chiropractors, organized the Naturopathic Association of California, and in 1909 sought legislation to effect the establishment of a licensing board. An amendment to the medical law, having approval of the state medical authorities, was passed, by which anyone showing a certificate of membership in the association might receive the seal of the medical board, and this constituted a license. Educational and professional qualifications were not investigated. Two years later, at the next session of the legislature, this absurd law was repealed, but in the meantime, along with many indiscriminate drugless practitioners a number of chiropractors had gained credentials. The latter eventually became at odds with the majority of chiropractors, who under agreements of organization refused to acknowledge medical authority. The dissension which developed has lasted until the present time, and since 1922 has been a fight for the control of the chiropractic examining board. The first practitioners to receive credentials under the naturopathic law, and others who embraced the opportunity to se- [122]

cure licenses under the drugless practice provisions of the medical law, as amended in 1915,* were less disposed to resist medical authority, and thus originated in California the opposing policies of the "mixers" and the "straights." This will be discussed later in this chapter, and in Chapters X and XI. This was a conflict between practitioners who relied more or less upon mechanical accessories, and in some cases even recommended drugs, and those who adhered strictly to chiro-praktikos (done by hand). It became more accentuated in California than in any other part of the United States. 

The lot of the chiropractor had grown more difficult in 1913 when arrests for "practicing medicine without a license" began to take place, and in the next two years about forty practitioners were convicted. By 1915 there were about 300 chiropractors in California, most of whom were graduates of schools that had sprung up in the larger centers of population. 

Danger of Absorption

For chiropractors to lose identity among the various drugless practitioners was seen by the *Drugless practitioners' licenses are still available in California upon presentation before the medical board of evidence of 2,000 hours of study in the healing arts. The original requirement was 1,000 hours and an oral examination. [123]

"straights" as playing directly into the hands of the medical board. The first organized resistance against what was viewed as a policy of absorption began with the Federated Chiropractors of California, formed in 1912, which undertook a program of public education. A licensing system under proper authority had long been viewed as the only means of attaining a secure professional status, and this could be realized only through a state board of chiropractic examiners. 

From 1913 to 1921 bills to establish such a board were regularly presented at Sacramento, the state capital, only to suffer defeat. Funds for campaigning were scanty and organization far from perfect, and even the support of rapidly increasing sympathizers, patients who had been restored to health through chiropractic, proved inadequate. 

Encouragement, however, came from the defeat of Initiative No. 46 at the fall election of 1914. This measure would have doomed chiropractic to oblivion among heterogeneous drugless healers. The opposition was directed by Dr. T. F. Ratledge, present head of the Ratledge System of Chiropractic Schools, who had been active for three years (1908-1910) in legislative work in Oklahoma and later in Kansas, where the first of all chiropractic laws was passed in 1913. The securing of legal [124]

THE STRUGGLE IN CALIFOR CALIFORNIA 

recognition in Kansas was in no small measure due to the courageous efforts of Dr. Ratledge, Dr. 

Anna M. Foy, of Topeka, and Dr. Willard Carver of Oklahoma City.

Impetus to legislation in California was given by Hon. Alfred L. Bartlett of Los Angeles, a former member of the state legislature, and it was under his guidance that the chiropractic bill of 1915 was launched and won a favorable vote in the assembly. There were but thirteen adverse votes. However, it was beaten by a narrow margin in the Senate. Mr. Bartlett was a believer in the principle that an individual has the right to elect such treatment against illness as he deems best. 

Furthermore, only upon legal recognition of chiropractic could standards be realized, and without standards the elimination of unqualified practitioners was impossible. Captain W. T. Helms of the same city was an ardent sympathizer, who, while chief deputy district attorney, publicly declared the medical law unjust. This was at a time when dope venders, abortionists and even thugs were said to be safer from arrest than chiropractors, against whom mere technicalities were sufficient grounds for jail sentences.

With arrests multiplying throughout the country, California chiropractors especially were har- [125]

assed for a decade prior to 1922. In a single year occurred 450 arrests. The "Go to jail for chiropractic" slogan is said to have been originated by the Federated Chiropractors of California. It infused the practitioners in all sections of America with a fresh zeal and a sense of heroism, and they pursued their livelihood with a diminished dread of the courts of law.

The Alameda County Chiropractors' Associa tion, organized in 1917 by Dr. James Compton, adopted a constitution by which its members were required to go to jail rather than pay a fine. The association was advised by Tom Morris, chief counsel of the Universal Chiropractors Association, and former lieutenant governor of Wisconsin, that it was illegal to maintain a constitutional decree against paying a fine, but the officers of the Alameda association would not revise the ruling. This organization became the hotbed of agitation, and many chiropractors from distant towns applied for membership, the actual strength of which was kept secret.

Dr. Ratledge's Arrest 

Meanwhile the round-up of men and women practicing without licenses continued. Dr. Ratledge who attended every legislative session at Sac- [126]

ramento between 1913 and 1921, was arrested in 1916, causing a newspaper sensation. He had been apprehended two years before under the Drugless Practitioners' Act, and acquitted. When declared guilty of practicing without a license, Louis Ward, later a judge in San Francisco, told him that if he would apply to the State Board of Medical Examiners for a license he would be granted a pardon. 

He was given the alternative of discontinuing practice until after the next legislative session. Dr. 

Ratledge declined, saying that he considered this a conspiracy against the people, inasmuch as a license signifies competency and nothing else. He insisted that the members of the medical board, being uneducated in chiropractic, were incompetent to pass upon his qualifications.

Governor Hiram Johnson pleaded with Dr. 

Ratledge to reconsider his stand. His going to prison would bring shame to his wife and boy.

He bade him to think of his son in particular. The chiropractor outlaw replied: "And if I don't go to jail he won't respect me when he gets old enough to understand." 

The governor put his arm about Dr. Ratledge's shoulder, and tried again to dissuade him adding, "Think it over." "I've been thinking it over for many years," [ 127 ]

was the reply. "You've been considering the matter for thirty minutes. Think it over, Governor." 

Perplexity stared from Governor Johnson's eyes as they parted. That there was such a thing as philosophy in chiropractic seemed incredible. 

The following day, May 5, 1916, Dr. T. F. 

Ratledge went to jail for ninety days. At this time there was no alternative of paying a fine. The widely published story of this incarceration stirred up considerable sentiment, and more than one editor alluded to the "spirit of '76." 

The "Round-up” 

Dr. Charles C. Lyon, aged seventy-four, was the first of the Alameda County chiropractors to be arrested and sent to jail. He served fifty days.

Quickly followed the seizure of the three organizers, Dr. Compton, Dr. John A. McCarl and Dr. 

Linden L. D. McCash, the latter being imprisoned on two occasions. Dr. Minnie Leach collapsed in jail after a ten-day hunger strike. Dr. Hazel E.

Harper and Dr. Shahzada were arrested and found guilty. Compton and McCarl were acquited on jury verdicts. Dr. Moses J. Holt, who succeeded 

Dr. Shahzada, was also taken into custody. Luke- Resistance doubled as arrests went on. warm practitioners became alive to their peril and [128]

compromise was seen as fatal to their future. To duplicate the error of the osteopaths by embracing medicine and surgery was regarded as a desperate expedient and equivalent to a complete surrender. 

In 1920 another initiative measure, No. 5, appeared, its purport being to license all drugless practitioners under the name of chiropractors. It met the same fate as Initiative No. 46 in 1914, for the organizations of chiropractors were gaining solidarity. 

As arrests continued fighting spirit arose. 

Among the more sensational cases was that of Doctors J. E. and Reba I. Willis, of Porterville. On

December 8, 1920, their office was ransacked by two investigators, their patients insulted, it was alleged, and they themselves placed under arrest. 

The local magistrate released them under bond to appear in the superior court, the arraignment occurring January 24, when they were sentenced to 

100 days in jail or the payment of $200. They chose imprisonment and bade their three-year-old twin girls good-bye at the jail door. It was the first time a mother had been incarcerated and much indignant comment was voiced. Letters of condolence came to the jail from foreign countries, and all told, more than 100,000 pieces of mail are said to have been received by the imprisoned [129]

couple. Mrs. Willis was released after sixty-five days to attend her children who had become seriously ill, and her husband was freed for three days to aid in their restoration. Dr. Willis served his time, but was arrested and jailed twice in 1922.

In the interim the chiropractic initiative measure passed and his case was taken to the Appellate 

Court. Governor Friend W. Richardson, shortly after his inauguration in 1923, pardoned Dr. Willis and all other chiropractors then in jail and under 

Governor Richardson in his address to the legislature stated in substance that he had pardoned these chiropractors because in his opinion they had been unjustly accused. sentence.

Dr. H. Health Engmark of Los Angeles was tried for the second time on January 21, 1921, found guilty of practicing medicine without a license and required to serve three months. He had a wife and two children as dependents. Others of professional repute had shared the same experience in growing number. In the late fall at Long 

Beach five chiropractors had been arrested in one day: Doctors P. F. Brandt, O. W. Halverson, J. W. 

Helms, Jeannette Habich and T. P. Overton. So aroused were citizens of the community that 1,200 assembled at a mass meeting. Dr. Ratledge and 

Secretary Brockman of the Chamber of Com- [130]

merce officiated, and Dr. E. C. Fortin of Los Angeles made clear the injustice of applying medical law to chiropractic.

In other parts of the state the round-up continued. Fresno and Bakersfield were scenes of numerous arrests. Judge Bunnell of Bakersfield refused to issue a warrant authorizing the search of chiropractors' offices in Taft. Nevertheless, in 

1922, Doctors C. De Marsa, J. F. White, L. John son and E. M. Johnson were taken into custody in 

Taft and arraigned at Bakersfield. When the judge asked the sheriff why there were so few witnesses, in view of the many subpoenas issued, the sheriff said: "Your Honor, the sheriff's office has been unable to catch the witnesses. They hide under beds and run out the back doors. They won't testify against these chiropractors. The sheriff's office has a lot of important business, so if you want these witnesses you'll have to catch them yourself."

The case was dismissed for lack of evidence. 

Despite the many tragic circumstances these trials sometimes afforded amusement.

When the same group of chiropractors was arrested in Taft in 1921, the special agent asked that they be placed under a bond of $1,000 each. Judge 

J. L. Reed, in his court at Fellows, replied: [131]

"I'm very sorry, but a goat got in last night and ate up all the bonds for $1,000, so we'll make it $100."

At the subsequent trial, the judge said that he could not see where the chiropractors had done anything but good and dismissed the case. Then he told how his little girl had been cured through chiropractic, and reminded his hearers that 2,000 years ago a Man had been crucified for doing similar work.

Decoy Evidence 

The Reverend Emil Meyer of Sacramento wrote in The California Telegram of April 27, 1922: 

"To our knowledge not one accusation, as a matter of record in California, has been brought against any chiropractor by any patient. Charges and convictions were based upon decoy evidence. "The Medical Practice Law is the only law, we believe, on the California statute books that requires hired, coached, trained spies to detect violators of the law. . . . No robber, murderer or other criminal is tempted by hired spies to commit crime, to 'get a case against him.' It is a crime to incite to crime. If chiropractic is criminal is it not a crime to incite the practice of chiropractic? "We cannot expect that every chiropractic adjustment will cure, no more than we must fear [132]

that every medical pill will kill. Many surgical operations are successful -to the undertaker; many poisonous drugs act likewise. Many good people, including many physicians, shudder with horror while they remember what poisonous medicines were administered only ten years ago. Why this great change is prescribing medicines? What transformations will another decade bring?"

The success of the initiative of 1922 was principally due to the intensive work of the California 

Chiropractic Campaign Committee, formed in 1921 by the Federated Chiropractors of California and the California State Chiropractic Society, which had come into being a few years after the former organization. The state was divided into two campaign districts, each having its own committee, and the work of raising funds and influencing public opinion went into the hands of hundreds of volunteers, both professionals and lay supporters. 

State Funds for Propaganda It

Campaign ammunition included criticism of the alleged expenditure of money for propaganda by the State Board of Medical Examiners. stirred up attack entirely outside of the battle zone. 

The Sacramento Union in an editorial of March 

28, 1922, sounded this echo: [133]

State moneys are supposed to be for the purpose of maintaining state departments and enforcing state laws. At least that is what the taxpayer is led to believe, and what the statutes provide. As far as can be ascertained, there is no statute permitting the use of public funds for propaganda purposes. Yet one department is deliberately using its state office and apparently a portion of its state funds for purely propaganda purposes.

The "Bureau of Information" of the State Board of Examiners has recently been deluging the press of the state with bold propaganda against the chiropractors and other drugless practitioners, to further their plans to compel such practitioners to study medicine and pass a regular state medical examination before being permitted to practice. Sometimes the two-page circular sent out will contain a modicum of news at the end in regard to examinations; others are but thinly veiled tirades against chiropractors.

The merits or demerits of the chiropractors do not enter into the question. Every state body is supposed to be unprejudiced and to be free from dominance by any cult, organization, association, school of learning or practice, . . . Supposedly unbiased, it is deliberately going out of its way and using its state office and apparently state funds—to attack a certain curative school. 

We believe that such practice is entirely without the jurisdiction of the State Board of Medical Examiners, and that its news letters should contain news only and not rabid propaganda.* 

A modern Jeanne d'Arc appeared in the person of Mrs. Jessie White, who organized the Chiro- *Reprinted in The California Telegram of April 27, 1922. [134]

practic Defenders' League and went from town to town in the north, pleading the cause and establishing fighting units to secure passage of Bill Number 16. In Los Angeles Dr. Ratledge offered to divide the tuition income of his school with the campaign committee. The Chiropractic Initiative 

Bill was carried by an emphatic majority of 153,- 751 votes. This vindication by the people of California marked the turning point for chiropractic in this state. The right of chiropractors to be examined and licensed by competent authorities, representatives of their own profession, was now a matter of law, a privilege that could not be revoked, and which assured recognition to every worthy individual.

Linden L. D. McCash, in charge of the campaign in the northern counties, testified that $18,- 085.07 had been spent in his district. In the southern part of the state $46,129.24 had been the cost of the campaign, according to Frank Pyott, committee chairman.

While the campaign was at its height considerable comment arose through the preferring of charges by the Campaign Committee against Dr. 

David P. Barrows, president of the University of

California, Berkeley, because of the public utterances of Dr. Barrows and campaigning on the part [135]

of students. Complaint was filed with the Board of Regents asking for the removal of Dr. Barrows for entering politics while acting as president of the State University. Although the charges tended to check the activities of the students, no penalty could be exacted, for the matter came under the political code.* At Stanford University the stu dents actively contended against the chiropractic bill, reflecting the prejudice expressed by their president, Dr. Ray Lyman Wilbur, who was also *The specific accusation brought against Dr. Barrows by the California Chiropractic Campaign Committee was that the university president had violated Article 9, Section IX, of the Political Code by issuing a statement to the press antagonistic to the chiropractic bill. The committee, in the name of 108,000 voters who had signed the initiative petition, demanded a retraction of Dr. Barrows' statement. The latter, in declining to accept a second invitation to debate the merits of Bill Number 16, wrote the campaign committee: "The only point of difference between us, as I understand it, is your proposal that the chiropractic profession have its own board of examiners, while my feeling is that all medical practice should be subject to one standard system of examination and certification by the state." 

This elicited the following statement from the chiropractic campaign headquarters in Oakland: "The chiropractic campaign committee concurs with the statement of Dr. Barrows that all medical practice should be subject to one standard system of regulation. But it also contends that all drugless practice should be subject to an entirely different system of regulation. The medical and drugless sciences are distinctly separate. Standard chiropractic institutions require at least a three years' course of study before graduation. Medical men, never having studied chiropractic, are therefore totally unqualified to either regulate or examine applicants to practice chiropractic. In fact the present obsolete medical law does not even provide for an examination in chiropractic. No examination in chiropractic has ever been given in 

California-and cannot be given under the present medical law, passed 20 years before chiropractic was discovered, (1876). Hence the imperative need for chiropractic regulation by chiropractic authorities, not prejudiced, competitive medical, interested only in their own profession." [136]

president of the American Medical Association, later becoming Secretary of the Interior. Another active foe of chiropractic was Chester Rowell,* editor of the Fresno Republican, whose syndicated articles were widely read on the Pacific Coast. 

The New Law 

The chiropractic bill provided for a self-sustaining board of examiners; a test of the qualifications of every chiropractor in the state as a basis for issuing licenses, a high school education or "education equivalent in training power" being prerequisite; a course of study covering 2,400 hours in a chiropractic school; the observance of all state and municipal health regulations and the non-use of drugs by chiropractors. The number of study hours required was 400 more than that specified for drugless practitioners in the medical enactment which now no longer might apply to chiropractic. It was estimated that by the passage of the chiropractic initiative $125,000 of taxpayers' money would be saved annually, as this was said to be the average amount expended by the medical board to prosecute chiropractors. *A challenge to debate was sent from the northern and southern campaign divisions to Chester H. Rowell or any proponent of the medical forces, with an offer to pay all expenses involved, but the challenge went unaccepted, chiropractic philosophy being considered a hard nut to crack. [137]

The first board of chiropractic examiners in 

California as appointed by Governor Friend W. 

Richardson, consisted of Dr. James Compton, of

Oakland, and Dr. J. B. Edgerton, of Banning, for three-year terms; Dr. Ray S. La Barre, of San 

Francisco, and Dr. W. A. Messick, of Los Angeles, for two-year terms, and Dr. Albin Peterson, of 

Los Angeles, for one year.

Dr. Compton was then president of the Federated Chiropractors of California, the Alameda County Chiropractors' Association, which he had organized, and vice-chairman of the Northern 

California Chiropractic Campaign Committee, of whose executive and editorial committees he was chairman.

The task of appointing the members of the new board had been passed on to Governor Richardson by his predecessor, Governor William D. Stephens, after six weeks' baffling investigation of the merits of fifty-four applicants, representing many schools and organizations. The initiative law required that no two applicants should come from the same school and no more than two from any county. 

Governor Stephens received a 200-word telegram from Dr. Ratledge which declared that if men not truly representative of the profession were appointed, then the responsibility would be upon the [138]

governor's shoulders. The matter of public health was to be weighed in the balance. Governor Richardson's statement on February 10, 1923, concerning his appointments included the remark: "The appointment of the chiropractic board is the most difficult job I have undertaken since assuming office." He added: "If the files of letters I have received showing the opinion of the chiropractors of each other should be published the legal fraternity would have a busy time with libel suits. The fight between the chiropractic schools has been intense, and as there are only five appointments to be made and at least a dozen leading schools, all could not be satisfied."
