Another initiative created a state board of osteopathy. There were approximately thirty-five classifications of drugless healers not affected by the new laws. Nearly 200 cases against chiropractors and osteopaths* under the state medical act were 

*The Medical Practice Act, found in Chapter 212 of the statutes of 

1907, in that year had repealed the existing medical and osteopathic laws, the latter in 1901 having provided an examining board of five osteopaths. (Chapter 99 of the statutes of 1901.) Thus after an interim of fifteen years osteopathy again acquired autonomy. During that period the eleven members of the state examining board included two osteopaths. 

In 1925, three years after the passage of the chiropractic act, there were reported to be approximately 700 more chiropractors than osteopaths in California. In these three years 1,142 licenses were issued to chiropractors, while in twenty-five years 1,160 osteopathic licenses were issued. Many of the osteopaths had gone to other states. [139]

dropped, according to a report from Harry A. Encell, attorney for the State Medical Board. 

Of the 900 chiropractors in California in December, 1922, it was estimated that less than fifteen per cent held certificates from the medical board. The forming of new boards, it was said by Attorney Encell, reduced the legal work of the medical board by seventy per cent. The principal effect of the new law, however, was to raise the standard of chiropractic through the elimination of unqualified practitioners and, ultimately, to advance the morale of the profession. 

Fresh Obstacles 

But the eleven-year fight for autonomy was not destined to bring peace to chiropractic in California, for no sooner had the new State Board of Chiropractic Examiners taken office than obstructionists appeared to demand their removal. These were drugless practitioners who were eager to gain control of the new board. Ouster proceedings were filed February 26 after the board had been in existence only sixteen days, on the ground that its members could offer no evidence of the three years' practice required by the new law.

This quo warranto action was started by Dr. 

Joseph Sanford, of the Western College of Chiro- 

[140]

practic in San Francisco, who had been denounced by the campaign committee in 1922 following his advertising the fact that his school had been investigated and approved by the medical board. Dr. 

Sanford was declared by the campaign committee to be anti-chiropractic. 

When the chiropractic examiners met in Sacramento on February 27 and read in the morning paper that quo warranto proceedings had been instituted, they took a room in the capitol, secured a typewriter, locked the door and issued five licenses typed on plain paper to themselves. A box of ordinary red seals had been bought at a stationer's. It was evident to all that if their right to office was denied by the court the chiropractic law would be in extreme jeopardy. Much depended upon the single point of the proper origin of authority in the profession. Holders of certificates under the naturopathic law bearing the medical seal and possessors of licenses under the Drugless Practitioners' Act obviously occupied a strategic advantage.

At this juncture Dr. Ratledge went to Sacramento and secured the names of all applicants for licenses. He asked for twenty-five dollars from each individual and from the proceeds was formed the Chiropractic Defense Committee, whose object [141]

was to defend the examining board. It was composed of committees from the Federated Chiropractors of California and the California State 

Chiropractic Society. But some of the "mixers" on the defense committee, being alarmed at a ruling by Attorney General U. S. Webb that chiropractors using hydro-electric or any other adjunctive modalities would be construed as practicing medicine and hence could not legally employ such modalities under the Chiropractic Act, decided that salvation lay in separation and forthwith withdrew their support. The recalcitrants then (1927) joined the newly organized Progressive Chiropractic Association, whose purpose was to combat the attorney general's ruling. The remaining members of the defense committee adjourned sine die and a new defense committee was immediately organized.* The "Progressives" 

That which is known as the "progressive movement" in chiropractic is chiefly confined to California and Oregon, but claims a following in Arizona, New Mexico, Texas and Oklahoma. Its adherents on an average are said to make spinal ad- *This committee was incorporated in 1926 as a state association. The following year it became merged with other organizations into the Cali fornia Chiropractic Association. 

[142]

justments in approximately eighty per cent of cases, but openly allege that chiropractic cannot cure all diseases. Unlike the more strictly orthodox practitioner, who depends upon manipulation for all treatment, the "progressive" concedes the necessity for surgery and even medicine in certain cases. He employs mechanical devices for examinations and treatment, as, in fact, do many of the more conservative chiropractors. 

In California, where the "progressives" attained recognition by securing places on the examining board in 1928, much friction has occurred between the California Chiropractic Association and the Progressive Chiropractic Association of 

California. An effort on the part of the latter organization to increase the number of hours in the study course from the present legally required 2,400 to 3,600, thereby including electro-therapy, hydro-therapy, biology, physics, minor surgery, optometry, obstetrics (including twenty-five bedside deliveries) and general hospital work, was regarded by the conservatives as a surrender of fundamental principles such as might result in eventual domination by the medical boards of the country. 

California represents something in the nature of a testing ground for chiropractic, in the opinion of its oldest exponents. While there are slight [143]

prospects of an eventual amalgamation between the opposing factions, the more extreme views involved appear irreconcilable. What constitutes chiropractic is a pivotal question, upon which much depends. Orthodoxy declares that the inclusion of medical theories destroys the very foundation of the science, particularly in that the "progressives" recognize bacteria as potential origins of some diseases and indicate surgery and medicine to relieve abnormalities which orthodoxy pronounces curable through vertebral adjustment. The "progressives," professing themselves equally qualified as chiropractors, declare it to be for the best interests of the profession and of humanity to admit the validity of other theories and methods. 

Whatever they deem necessary to effect cure they regard as legitimate and not incompatible with chiropractic. 

The two opposing organizations not only fail to include many doctors of chiropractic who lean this way or that, and are allied with neither of the associations, but neither is made up of practitioners all employing identical methods. When so fundamental an institution as the Carver Chiropractic College instructs its students in the use of instruments for diagnosis and treatment, it is not strange that among the nominal "straights" are [144]

many doctors of chiropractic who do not rely entirely upon hand palpation. In short, the alignments represented by the associations fail to indicate the degree of catholicism within the organizations, which renders possible an ultimate amalgamation and the enrollment of some 35 per cent of practitioners now unorganized. To what extent liberalism may weaken or strengthen chiropractic is a subject evoking general controversy and is of serious concern to the national organizations. 

The plan of the "progressives" for a 3,600hour course, embracing the new subjects already mentioned, was proposed at a joint meeting at Los 

Angeles, in August, 1929. The "straights" present refused to endorse the program. The study of bacteriology in particular was pronounced inconsistent with chiropractic. Dr. Otis M. McMurtrey, newly elected president of the Progressive Chiropractic Association, declared that his organization, nevertheless, would proceed alone to secure passage of such an amendment to the chiropractic act. 

Opposition to the plan was voiced by the California Chiropractic Association, whose officers viewed it as further evidence of collusion, and a publicity campaign to defeat the petition was un- [145]

dertaken. The required 92,800 signatures were not secured.

That the status of the "progressive" chiropractor was fast becoming indeterminable as well as immune from legal penalties was suggested at about this time by the case of Dr. B. D. Wilcox, of San

Diego. The law forbids the chiropractor to practice that which may be included in materia medica. 

Two expert witnesses tried for an entire day to define materia medica to the satisfaction of the court, whereupon the fatigued judge declared that he found the defendant not guilty.

Licensed In Secret The State Board of Chiropractic Examiners found its hands tied and was confronted with the likelihood of being unable to hold examinations or issue licenses. It was decided by the members, however, in any case to hold an examination in May. Hearing of this plan the attorney general gave instructions that no examinations could be held by the board while the court proceedings were pending. After considering this notification and realizing that some properly accredited chiropractors must somehow come into existence, else the years of effort toward recognition might prove to be in vain, the board resolved to hold two secret [146]

meetings. Accordingly on the 14th and 21st of

April, 1923, a month earlier than the time originally set, secret examinations were held in Los Angeles and Oakland and licenses issued to 220 chiropractors.

When news of this reached Attorney General Webb an injunction was secured to prevent further examinations. The supreme court held that it could not take cognizance of illegal practice and the five members of the board were ousted. 

This occurred early in 1924. On July 10 Governor Richardson named four new members, reappointing Dr. Compton, who was made secretary and executive officer the same day.

Immediately quo warranto proceedings were begun again, this time at the instance of Dr. George 

D. Gillespie, of San Francisco, holder of a drugless practitioner's license. Among the five Dr. 

Compton was doubly involved, having been ousted by the supreme court and reappointed by the governor. Charges of contempt were offset, however, since the secretary of the board could now prove his qualifications, thanks to the five licenses issued by the original board members to themselves on the occasion of their first meeting. This, as a matter of fact, was mandatory under the Chiropractic 

Act. 

[147]

For four years the second quo warranto action turned on the question of a jury trial, which was demanded at the outset by the Defense Committee. The attorney general denied the request as incompatible with proceedings of this nature. Two cases where jury trials had occurred in quo warranto were cited, yet the request was not granted. 

While this controversy lingered in court more than 2,000 chiropractors were licensed. The case fizzled out, and by 1928 it made little difference whether the chiropractic examiners were deemed lawfully chosen or otherwise.

The irreconcilables were not done with harassing tactics, however, and, prompted by Dr. Sanford, filed against 1,100 active chiropractors charges of fraud and deception in connection with their applications for licenses. The board ignored the charges and proceeded to hold examinations of these chiropractors. Dr. Sanford secured an injunction to prevent the board from holding further examinations until the charges were heard. At considerable expense the 1,100 cases were of necessity tried by the board, which found none of the applicants guilty of fraud and deception and filed its decision with the attorney general. The latter obtained a court release and an order by which the board became empowered to confer licenses upon [148]

the 1,chiropractors. This is one of many episodes in the long struggle against obstruction. 

During 1925 and 1926 the rivalry between the opposing factions of chiropractors continued. 

Every obstacle possible was placed in the way of the examiners. Opinion among conservatives was that the program betrayed a conspiracy to ruin the profession. In the former year at one period as many as three temporary injunctions were in force, and it was not until 1927 that routine work on the part of the chiropractic board was smoothly under way. During that year and the early part of 1928 the general situation improved. All eligible chiropractors received licenses and an extensive co-operation developed between the practitioners and educators and the examining board. Under the plan of the Defense Committee an especially effective liaison was maintained by which the policies of the board and the desires of the profession were co-ordinated. The expense was borne by the profession, but amounted to little more than a nominal defrayment. The monthly California Chiropractic Bulletin, official organ of the Joint Defense Committee, with James Compton, D.C., as editor, performed yeoman service in securing funds for the committee and inducing lax chiropractors to pay their license renewal fees, the neglect of [149]

which in numerous cases caused the revoking of licenses. It functioned primarily, however, as a news medium. 

A reciprocity agreement with the State of Nevada by which credentials were honored in either state was signed in 1924, but was rescinded at a meeting of the board in Los Angeles, January 1113, 1926, and thereafter all chiropractors seeking to practice in California were required to pass the regular examinations provided by section 5 of the 

Chiropractic Act. 

"Progressives" Gain Board

After five years of self-government and steady improvement chiropractic in California was beset with fresh difficulties arising from the appointment of examiners by Governor C. C. Young who were declared to represent but a small minority of the profession.

A statement by Governor Young followed his appointment of the new members. It read, in part: "I am trusting that this will prove a harmonious board, and that it will lead to harmony in the profession." This was greeted with ridicule. 

Harmony was not destined to prevail. The warring camps became more hostile than ever. In vain peacemakers sought in behalf of chiropractic to [150]

cement the differences between the California Chiropractic Association and the "progressives," but the differences were too marked and retaliations had been heaped too high. Represented by only two members on the examining board, and some of the time one, the leaders of the California Chiropractic Association declared the board failed to represent chiropractic. Governor Young's ap pointees, on the other hand, deploring the failure to amalgamate the rival organizations, dubbed their more conservative rivals as radicals, and adopted, it was alleged, a policy of retardation toward their opponents and their activities in the "C.C.A."

The issue reached a climax in a libel suit for $110,000 brought against the California Chiropractic Association, based upon four articles published in the California Chiropractic Association

Bulletin in May and September, 1929, which set forth among other allegations that the board of examiners, with its majority of "progressives," discriminated against certain schools and conducted its work in a way detrimental to chiropractic. The plaintiff was a former investigator for the board. 

Superior Court Judge Leon R. Yankwich, a recognized authority on libel, gave judgment for the defendants in January, 1931, holding that although the articles were libelous per se public officials are [151]

properly a target for criticism given in good faith.

(Civil Code, Article 47, sub-division 3.) Sensa tional testimony was introduced at the trial relative to alleged invisible influence over the board and the asserted "framing" of opposing chiropractors through women of the underworld. [152]

CHAPTER VIII Status in Various Countries HE PROGRESS of chiropractic abroad has been beset very naturally by prejudices similar to those which have existed in the United States. Advancement, however, is shown in many countries. In New Zealand the profession enjoys a general recognition comparable with that of the most liberal communities of western America. In some European countries it has attained a kind of esoteric importance, it being reported that the royal families of Italy, Spain, Belgium, Greece and Jugoslavia have been attended by chiropractors during recent years. King Humbert of Italy is said to be a strong advocate of chiropractic. Conditions in these countries and in the Balkans were reported by Dr. K. Ligeros at the American Bureau of Chiropractic in New York in the summer of 1930.

Dr. Ligeros completed a course in medicine at the 

University of Athens, and is a graduate of Montana 

University and the Palmer School of Chiropractic. 

He served a seven-months' prison sentence in Athens, Greece, for employing an assistant who held no diploma from a medical college. On the occasion [153]

of his visit to New York he stated that the Grecian government had named a committee to consider the establishment of a chair of chiropractic in the medical college of the University of Athens. 

Chiropractic attained favorable publicity in Greece two years before, owing to cures reported during the Dengue epidemic, notably those attributed to 

Dr. Ligeros. 

An account of the address of Dr. Ligeros at the headquarters of the American Bureau of Chiropractic is published in the Chiropractic News of 

August 28, 1930. He is quoted as saying that chiropractic or something similar to it was practiced among the ancient Greeks before medicine came into general use. "This is shown even in Greek sculpture," Dr. 

Ligeros says. "While the ancients did not reproduce the spine alone, because of its ugliness, they represented it as a serpent. Such is the case in the statues of Athena, Apollo and Hippocrates. "In ancient Greek statuary the serpent is shown drinking milk from a ball. This is taken to represent the spine 'drinking' the life fluid through the nerves. This 'life fluid' now is known as mental impulses, or nervous energy. "The old Greeks kept their bodies in shape by spinal adjustments. The treatment was adminis- [154]

tered by having the patient lean forward while the 'chiropractor' used the flat of his foot instead of his hand for the adjustment. "Writers on gymnastics, like Orevasius, Etios,

Galenos, Roufos and Antylicus, mentioned this method. Had not the library of Alexandria burned 

I believe we would find many volumes on this subject."

There is much favorable sentiment toward chiropractic in Greece at the present time, according to Dr. Ligeros, who speaks of the successful treatment of sleeping sickness, meningitis, tuberculosis and even blindness.

In that exceedingly conservative yet sanely progressive country, Switzerland, a view of the situation recalls the early attacks upon chiropractic pioneers in America. Although the Lucerne courts have finally bestowed recognition of the right to practice (the case of Simon Mueller, D.C., Ph.C.), the medical fraternity has been greatly aroused. 

However, there is scarcely more than one chiropractor to each million of the 4,000,000 people. 

Every possible obstacle has been placed in the way of the practitioners in Lucerne, Zurich and Berne.

It is believed that there is a single chiropractor in 

Basle. Through the instrumentality of Dr. Muel ler and his few colleagues a chiropractic society of [155]

approximately 700 members, the patients of the chiropractors, has been formed in the cantons of 

Lucerne, Zurich and Berne.

In England and Ireland chiropractors enjoy comparative immunity by virtue of the policy of authorities to ignore their existence. In Switzerland the little handful, who are identified as "straight chiropractors," are under constant surveillance. A considerably more lenient attitude is shown in Germany and Russia, where, after the Great War, many chiropractors trained in America took up their practice. Members of the profession who had served on the various fronts gained an influence which won thereafter more or less civilian respect. In France exists a continual battle against absorption by medicine. In Germany there is a tendency to view chiropractic as a branch of surgery. In China, Japan and Hawaii legislation has rendered secure the position of the chiropractor.

A Swiss Pioneer The case of Dr. Simon Mueller is notable. Gradu ated from the Palmer school in 1917, twice arrested in San Francisco, he decided to establish in his native city, Lucerne, Switzerland, in 1927. At this time there was at least one chiropractor in the country, a lady practicing in Berne, the capital, [156]

who had graduated from an eastern American school. His application for a permit to practice was refused. Shortly after undertaking work, despite the lack of this permit, he was haled into court and ordered to stop "practicing medicine."

Dr. Mueller explained that he had nothing to do with medicine and Judge Amtstatthalter discharged him with fatherly advice.

The subsequent encounters with the Swiss authorities are told in a letter from Dr. Mueller to 

Dr. B. J. Palmer dated May 24, 1930, which was published in the Fountain Head News, of October, 1930, dated "October, A.C. 35," to signify the thirty-fifth year of chiropractic.

Within a fortnight after his release, through the instrumentality of the secretary of the medical board, Dr. Mueller was brought to court again and fined fifty francs and legal costs. He appealed to the district court (Amstgericht), where the six judges found him not guilty of attempting medical practice. Right to a title, however, was denied because satisfactory degrees were not offered in evidence. Mueller was given ten days to produce the documents, but he failed to attend to this in his elation over the partial victory. His medical opponents appealed to the superior court (Obergericht), and again without counsel the chiropractor [157]

found himself in court, this time facing a severe tribunal composed of five judges. 

With nearly fifty of his patients in court Dr. 

Mueller submitted his diploma, a California license and other papers. The staatsanwalt, or state's lawyer, urged that the defendant was merely practicing massage and hypnotism, the latter a criminal offense. After heated arguments Dr. Mueller asked to give a demonstration of chiropractic in the courtroom. In response to his 

In response to his request for a volunteer every one of the assembled patients raised his hand, then an elderly Swiss was called. Bared to the waist he was placed on a long seat known as the convicts' bench. After explaining impingements as the cause of disease, Dr. Mueller made an adjustment on the witness' spine. As the customary snap sounded the jurists looked at one another mistrustfully. The staatsanwalt admitted there was nothing hypnotic about the treatment and the court decided that it was not tantamount to practicing medicine or massage, whereupon the verdict of the lower court was sustained. The fact that chiropractic was represented in the United States by a large following, impressive schools, photographs of which were exhibited, and by properly engraved diplomas, carried weight with the superior court, but the privilege of using a degree un- [158]

recognized by Swiss universities was denied as before.

This occurred May 1, 1928. On July 4 Dr. 

Mueller was called before six bearded members of the medical board. The president announced that they had been advised by the court to grant a license to practice chiropractic in the canton of Lucerne. He added: "As we do not know what you really do practice we would kindly ask you to give us an explanation of this American health science, as you call it." 

Avoiding medical terms, Dr. Mueller set forth the disease theory according to D. D. Palmer. When he said that the germ was the effect and never the cause of illness, he was asked what he thought of 

Koch and Pasteur. He replied that the world would have been better off had they confined their efforts to the plow. His inquisitors desired to know if he would be willing to attempt to cure cancer.

Dr. Mueller parried by declaring that no practitioner, medical or otherwise, ever cured anything, but that this was brought about by the recuperative forces of nature, which when properly set in motion performed the actual cure. He hazarded the additional statement that to accept a cancer patient in Switzerland would be especially risky owing to the extent to which serums were em- [159]

ployed, requiring six months or more for elimination from the system. Something of the record of cancer cases under chiropractic treatment in the 

United States was told.

The physicians of Lucerne instead of issuing a license to Dr. Mueller, three months later turned his case over to the police and military department, which sent an imposing order forbidding practice at once, since he was unable to diagnose. Dr. Mueller wrote back that chiropractors did not diagnose and continued his practice. Patients grew in number very rapidly. Newspapers, denying space for advertising, ridiculed chiropractic as wild and woolly art from the far West, comparable with powwow and voodoo. Whereupon, the solitary champion informed the travel bureau he would communicate this slander of American institutions to the American press unless it were checked. Tourist business is paramount in Helvetia. As a result the Elbert Hubbards of Lucerne withdrew their barbs, not knowing that the real Elbert Hubbard had upheld chiropractic in his immortal writings. 

Several times thereafter Dr. Mueller was obliged to appear in the lower court, through the diligent pursuit of the medical doctors, who shared none of the lenience of the legal authorities. Leaving his practice in the charge of a brother, Dr. [160]

Mueller opened an office in Zurich. First he organized a society of sixty patients in that city, and submitted their request for his services in Zurich, various credentials and the decision of the superior court of Lucerne to the regierungsrat, the highest authority in Zurich. He was granted ten days to practice, pending a review of the credentials and data submitted. The time expired and the dictum of the regierungsrat was not forthcoming, so Dr. 

Mueller continued with his work. He was subpoenaed again to appear at the district court in Lucerne, where medical representatives alleged that he had not obeyed court orders and was subject to a fine of 300 francs. Once more diplomas and degrees were put in evidence and through a lawyer effort was made to induce his prosecutors to seek information as to the status of chiropractic through the Swiss legation at Washington, D. C.

The outcome, however encouraging from a legal standpoint, has borne no promise of immunity from medical assault. Meanwhile the Chiropractic 

Society in Switzerland holds an evidently enthusiastic membership and issues an official magazine. 

An Egyptian Story 

A lone battle unlike that of Dr.

Mueller was waged by Dr. Makine B. Maroon, of Bab-El-Hadid 

[161]

Square, Cairo, Egypt, who was arrested in 1925.

In this case, however, the court upon finding the defendant not guilty advised the medical authorities to refrain from molesting the chiropractor. 

The career of Dr. Maroon has the flavor of some old Oriental narrative. The son of an influential family in upper Egypt, he journeyed to America about 1918 with two other young Egyptians to study medicine. When they reached the 

United States they chanced to see the prospectus of a chiropractic college. Maroon's companions ridiculed the system and went to the University of Chicago for their medical course, but both died of pneumonia. Maroon, impressed with the chiropractic theory, undertook its study at a prominent school. He practiced in the United States for a time and returned to Egypt in 1923. His father censured him for not adopting medicine in fulfillment of his hopes and said that his son had squandered his time and money.

An aged paralytic, bedridden for years and regarded as incurable, gained Dr. Maroon's attention. He told his father he would prove the worth of chiropractic by treating the old invalid. Fearing further humiliation, the father sought to dissuade him from the experiment, but the son had the paralytic carried to Cairo and lodged near his [162]

office. Daily for months he patiently manipulated and adjusted the spine of the sick man. Many residents heard of the treatment and a widespread interest developed over the case. When at the end of three months the invalid was able to stand with crutches the partial recovery was hailed as a miracle, and thereafter Dr. Maroon's office became the 

Mecca of ailing hundreds. Followed then the charges of the medical rivals, but the court, as already stated, bade the prosecutors to let the chiropractor alone.

The story of Dr. Maroon was published in The 

Life Line, by the American Chiropractic Associa tion, in Syracuse, N. Y., October, 1930. 

Evasion in Denmark

In Denmark chiropractic has encountered ultra conservative prejudices and has gained almost no prestige whatever. A law dating from 1794 is in force and purports to prevent the healing efforts of quacks. That all but doctors of medicine are thus indicated is apparent in that nobody but a physician graduated from Copenhagen University may treat the sick. Any other kind of healer is presumed by law to be preventing medical aid in a given case, whether or no the treatment is believed to be dangerous. [163]

Due to a provision that a vertebral adjustment or other treatment may be administered by some one outside the medical profession upon the written prescription of a physician, a sort of chiropractic bootlegger has come into existence. At 

Nykobing it is said that three physicians, who are not members of the medical association, devote their time largely to prescribing vertebral adjustments, for which the chiropractors pay ten per cent of their fees from each case thus provided. 

These men make a tour of the chiropractors' offices, appearing about once every fortnight. They participate in X-ray fees as well as in the customary three dollars charged for each treatment. 

This method of evasion exists throughout Denmark. The medical men contend that chiropractors are unqualified to diagnose and insist that the actual vertebrae requiring adjustment be designated before the chiropractor receives the patient. 

The supreme court in 1929, however, found chiropractors not guilty in cases where such designation had not been given. Spinal adjustment was adjudged not dangerous. Following this decree the medical association undertook without apparent success to have the licenses of the three accommodating physicians revoked. [164]

CHAPTER IX 

Organizations 

HE Universal Chiropractors Association, formed in 1906, was a protective organization by which legal assistance was afforded chiropractors especially in states where laws were inadequate for protection. It offered aid in malpractice suits. The work was maintained for twenty-four years, when, with the American

Chiropractic Association, it was merged into the National Chiropractic Association, in the late autumn of 1930. 

The American Chiropractic Association, organ ized a few years prior to this merger, functioned as a competitor of the Universal Chiropractors 

Association. Its membership never attained 2,000, considerable sympathy being alienated by its resolution to recognize physio-therapy and the modalities as pertaining to chiropractic. The members of both these organizations were included in the National Chiropractic Association. 

A part of a general movement toward centralization and co-ordination, this latter association became especially concerned with matters of [165]

protection.

It will be discussed further in this chapter. With headquarters in New York City its first elected officers were Dr. Lillard T. Marshall, Lexington, Kentucky, president; Dr. C. E. 

Foster, Jacksonville, Florida, vice-president; Dr. B. 

A. Sauer, Syracuse, New York, executive secretary; Dr. Lee Fuller, Terre Haute, Indiana, treasurer. 

The International Chiropractic Congress con stitutes a central advisory power for dealing with the general problems of chiropractic throughout the world. Its scope is international; its government democratic. Through the congress help has been extended in various states requiring campaigns of publicity. It plans to develop general, constructive propaganda of an educational nature and to support a research department. Eventually it may be empowered to decide upon the validity of proposed modalities or technics, whose yearly advocacy has produced much vain discussion without revealing notable advantages over established methods.

The International Chiropractic Congress aims to co-ordinate the large number of chiropractic organizations, which for many years under their own leaderships and groups continually labored at cross purposes. Conflict of effort was apparent in [166]

ORGANIZATIONS 

state, district and county associations, being heightened by antagonistic modes and professional rivalries.

The congress is composed of three divisions: the International Congress of Examining Boards, the International Congress of Officers of State Chi ropractic Associations and the International Congress of Educational Institutions. As such it constitutes a federation whose individual organizations preserve their identity of effort in a work that covers the entire field of chiropractic. Under a board of governors elected from the three subsidiary organizations an executive committee chosen by the board has active charge of all business. Uniform Education Barely a decade after the establishment of the first chiropractic schools it was perceived as a necessity that fundamentals must be safeguarded through uniform courses of instruction. Peril had arisen with the correspondence courses and "diploma mills," by which unrepresentative agencies offered to train and qualify anyone as a doctor of chiropractic in a few months or even weeks. Some of these courses could be acquired on payment of fifty dollars, which sum likewise included a certificate or diploma. Another danger lay in the di- [167]

vergent views of the better schools, threatening an eventual cleavage or undermining of the fundamentals. 

As early as August, 1917, the International

Association of Chiropractic Schools and Colleges was organized at Davenport, Iowa. It was an attempt to reconcile the educational policies of all schools of recognized standing, presumably in accordance with the regime of the "Fountain Head," the Palmer School of Chiropractic. A close affiliation, however, proved difficult to bring about, although some advance was made towards unifying educational methods in several important schools. 

Even less actual success attended the initial task of combining state organizations. This was projected at a meeting in Kansas City, Missouri, October 1, 1921, but proved almost barren of results.

The same year an attempt was made to establish a national board of examiners at Palmer school, during the annual lyceum, or homecoming of graduates. J. Ralph John, D.C., was elected president of the new board, and for two years examinations were held, then the undertaking was abandoned. 

From these beginnings, nevertheless, sentiment was stirred in chiropractic journals and the urgent need of a general amalgamation of forces became apparent to the majority of educators and practi- [168]

ORGANIZATIONS 

tioners. The early efforts were not in vain, as was demonstrated in 1926 when the more auspiciously launched International Congress of Chiropractic

Examining Boards found potential allies from schools and state associations. By September of the following year, when a convention was held at Memphis, Tennessee, there were, in addition to eighteen examining boards, seven colleges and schools represented.

The first officers of the International Congress of Chiropractic Examining Boards, elected in Kansas City, Missouri, in 1926, were Dr. Eugene Cox, of North Carolina, president; Dr. Anna Foy, of 

Kansas City, first vice-president; Dr. R. C. Ellsworth, of Oregon, second vice-president; Dr. Sylvia L. Ashworth, of Nebraska, third vice-president; 

Dr. E. J. Bullock, of New Hampshire, fourth vicepresident; Dr. Harry Gallaher, of Oklahoma, secretary-treasurer. The board of directors included Dr. J. Ralph John, of Maryland; Dr. Maud 

Hastings, of Tennessee; Dr. C. Sterling Cooley, of 

Oklahoma; Dr. Myrtle Long, of Iowa, and Dr. 

W. J. Robb, of Kansas.

The inclusion of strong working units from the three classes of organizations was effected at the annual meeting in Chicago, September 3, 4 and 5, 1928, when the International Chiropractic Con- [169]

gress was created. Of the three branches of the congress the officers of the state associations shoulder most of the work, operating through the district and county associations to the individual practitioner and the lay public. Constitutional provision for membership affiliation on the part of all individuals in state associations is designed to engender a still greater cohesiveness in the profession. The congress is represented in thirty-four states and is designed to become the co-ordinating power in the chiropractic world.

More than 200 delegates were present at the 

Omaha convention in 1929, where enthusiasm and co-operative spirit were demonstrated on every hand among the representatives of state associations, state boards of examiners and educational institutions. All groups and recognized schools within the profession were represented at the five-day assemblage. Interfering in no way with the autonomy of the individual divisions, the congress proved inspiring by its tolerance and by its program of development for the interests of all constituents.

The evolution of the International Chiroprac tic Congress, whose influence and activities are of such recent origin, is notable as one of the outstanding achievements in the history of chiroprac- [170]

ORGANIZATIONS 

tic. So innumerable were the conflicts of creed and leadership in the majority of states of the 

Union that many predicted failure for this farreaching merger of interests. The Omaha convention surprised the most sanguine delegates by the complete harmony and purposeful co-operation that prevailed. The leaders of chiropractic had been brought together, and their deliberations, based upon nearly three decades of continuous battle for existence, revealed a practically unanimous point of view and a comprehensive vision of future requirements. 

Program of the Congress

As far as finances might permit a tentative program was outlined. Briefly, it included a plan for an international public educational department, the endowment and proper maintenance of chiropractic schools and the advancement of chiropractic science; a policy of resistance to adverse propaganda from the medical profession and the securing of favorable legislation; legal representation and publicity; scientific research and the promotion of public health, the establishment of chiropractic sanitariums; the organization and unified support of the lay friends of chiropractic; the standardization of methods of instruction in chiropractic [171]

schools and colleges, through meetings of chiropractic educators and the investigation and supervision of schools; the codification of laws governing professional practice, with a view to ameliorating the conflicting state laws and engendering an eventually complete reciprocity among states as concerning licensure; the publication of a first class professional journal as a medium of communication calculated to bring all members of the profession into closer relationship, also to serve as an educational instrument; the establishment of a legal and legislative department operative wherever the congress is represented, whose functions will include the maintenance of present adequate chiropractic laws and, the promotion of legislation where inadequate or non-existent; the securing of a status of equality for the chiropractor among the professions and the right to enter all hospitals, thereby vindicating his position in the service of health. 

This last resolution touches upon a subject which in recent years has especially agitated chiropractors. In line with their contention that the individual is constitutionally entitled to select such treatment against disease as he deems best, stands the impeachment of the present hospital system. 

The American Bureau of Chiropractic is an organization of laymen and practitioners formed in [172]

ORGANIZATIONS 

1925 for the purpose of advancing the science. It disseminates the evidence of recoveries through chiropractic and is engaged in a continual educational campaign, being at present represented in forty-two states and in England, Canada, France, Belgium, Greece and New Zealand. The membership is largely made up of men and women who have been chiropractic patients. For the first time a convention was held jointly with the annual lyceum of the Palmer School of Chiropractic, Au gust 24 to August 30, 1930, and 2,500 delegates were reported in attendance. A parade of 250 automobiles preceded the opening session, enlivening not only Davenport but the adjoining cities of 

Rock Island and Moline, Illinois. 

Work of the "A.B.C.”

The platform of this laymen's organization, commonly referred to as the "A.B.C.," is as follows: "Chiropractic is a philosophy, science and art of things natural, a system of adjusting the articulations of the spinal column, by hand only, for the correction of the cause of disease. "The purpose of the American Bureau of Chiropractic is: to organize laymen to secure and maintain legal recognition of the science of chiropractic and its practitioners, to undertake a broad [173]

and intensive campaign to present chiropractic to the people, to gather and disseminate authentic information about the accomplishments of chiropractic, and to maintain a publicity bureau for the benefit of its members."

Many testimonies were given at the sessions held at the auditorium of the Palmer school, not only personally but by graphophone, which included statements of cases on tuberculosis, cancer, sleeping sickness, spinal meningitis, arthritis and other diseases. 

Throughout the United States exist 145 auxiliaries of the A.B.C., each having from eight or ten members to several hundred. It is not uncommon to find present at meetings of some of the auxiliarises as many as 500 persons, evidencing the enthusiasm of a membership composed of patients or former patients and their families. This zealous activity is an almost cult-like florescence such as might have sprung from the seeds of philosophy sown by D. D. Palmer. His teachings of the innate and educated intelligences, of vital energytone-and that both ancient and modern metaphysical argument woven about the theory, remain among this far-scattered but closely organized membership. 

The fourth annual "get-together" in New [174]

ORGANIZATIONS 

York, February 21, 22 and 23, 1931, drew as many as 7,000 friends of chiropractic. It was decided to hold the next anniversary in the spacious Madison Square Garden. A thousand persons had been unaccommodated in the generous ballroom of the St. 

George Hotel, Brooklyn.

The American Bureau of Chiropractic is said to have been born from the single idea of having bail bonds ready in event of arrest. Dr. William H. 

Werner, founder, was a step ahead of his colleagues in preparedness, owing to the rigors of professional life in New York. Other chiropractors came to him for advice, until a small nucleus for a protective association was formed, eventually becoming known primarily as a layman's organization.

This lusty child of chiropractic came at a time when prophecies from the medical camp were calculated to inspire dread. One official of the American Medical Association had said that chiropractic could not survive more than two years at the most. 

It was indeed but a year since catastrophe had fallen upon the Universal Chiropractors Association, and affairs at Palmer School were in jeopardy. 

Many young students refrained from continuing their courses; not a few practitioners began to look toward other means of livelihood. 

Dr. Werner established radio station WMCA, [175]

and rallied the field with encouraging addresses. 

These had considerable effect and the infant organization began to grow rapidly. Following the nucleus formed at Woodhaven, Long Island, auxiliaries sprang up at Staten Island, Brooklyn, then in Illinois, New Jersey, Rhode Island and elsewhere. On March 17, 1927, the American Bureau of Chiropractic opened an office at 100 Fifth Avenue, New York. There it is now maintained as a 

New York State corporation limited to state activities, but its charter denotes it as a membership corporation with international scope. None of its officers receive a salary or any money from the organization. There is an international board functioning as a governing body, being composed of nine laymen and chiropractors. The international officers are Dr. William H. Werner, president; Dr. 

C. P. Eifertsen, vice-president; Dr. F. C. Zinke, treasurer; Dr. G. Vernon James, secretary; Dr. 

Anthony Cuffe, E. Gottfried Stenberg, Joseph H. 

Dunn, Michael Hogan and Herbert Foster.

The publicity program of the A.B.C. has become extensive, conveying the chiropractic message through various channels to the public. Dr. James 

Workman, of Santa Ana, California, president of the California unit, reports that radio lectures of fifteen minutes will be given daily at stations in San [176]

ORGANIZATIONS 

Diego, Los Angeles and San Francisco. Acclaimed by many individuals as the most outstanding publicity yet achieved for chiropractic was the float which won a special prize at the Pasadena tournament of roses on New Year's Day, 1931. Several hundred thousand persons watched the flowered displays representative of towns, industries, education and many prominent institutions. The chiropractic float, carrying the legend "Dawn," was artistically symbolical of chiropractic. The project was planned and the design executed under the direc tion of Dr. Workman. 

Years of Reorganization 

To gain something of a picture of the present National Chiropractic Association, which embodies much of the professional strength of chiropractic, it is necessary to review a little of the history of the two organizations which formed it. The A.C.A. was comparatively young at the time of the merger, but is said to have the larger representation in the membership. Many of the U.C.A.* members *The Universal Chiropractors Association was organized in 1906 in the basement of Palmer School, 828 Brady Street, Davenport, a year after the school had been moved to that location. The project was conceived by 

Hod Norton, Ernest Erz, B. J. Palmer and about sixteen others. A chiropractor had been arrested in La Crosse, Wisconsin, "for practicing without a license," and there was no money for his defense. It was realized that the danger was common to all. Tom Morris, of La Crosse, was engaged as counsel and Dr. Palmer as secretary, and both continued in office uninterruptedly until the tempestuous convention in Chicago, 1925. [177]

dropped out during the last five years of its existence, 300 in a single year. Though the career of the U.C.A. had been very notable, though it had handled 3,300 court cases prior to the year 1927, and under the guidance of Dr. B. J. Palmer and 

Tom Morris, national counsel, had prospered financially, it could not withstand the conflict between school and field.

The first shaft that nearly shattered the Universal Chiropractors Association fell in 1924. It was loosed from that mysterious but alluring object, the neurocalometer, which, like Pandora's box, seemed to hold a cloud of mischievous spirits. 

But a single touch by the tutelary genius, "B.J.,” was needed to shower the chiropractic realm with darts of controversy. Scarcely a leader but felt the sting. The intimate friendship between Tom 

Morris, counsel of the association, and Dr. Palmer, which had existed for twenty years, was broken and the association split into factions. It never regained its support or prestige. Presently Palmer School itself received a body blow through faculty disagreement, and, as already narrated, was thereafter saved from impending ruin by the appointment of a board of advisers. [178]

ORGANIZATIONS Doom of the U.C.A. 

The story of this disruption within the U.C.A. is intimately connected with the facts set forth in 

Chapter III. Dr. Palmer, recognizing the serious ness of the situation throughout the profession, brought about by the leasing offer for the neurocalometer, sought to have the device endorsed by his co-workers. One after another declined to lend his name to the product. Doctors Harry Vedder, James Firth and Steve Burich, now of Lincoln College, were then on the faculty of Palmer Schoolon Dr. Palmer's pay-roll. But they refused to give their endorsement, as did such other intimate associates as Doctors C. M. Guyselman, Lee Fuller, Elmer Schillig and Black, of Texas.

Dr. Palmer made his final appeal to Tom Morris, the lawyer. Together they had planned the policies of chiropractic since the earliest days. Together they had formulated that tenet which they knew to be paramount and irrevocable: the right of the sick to get well. Dr. Palmer made it clear that the situation was desperate and that Morris alone could extricate him.

Aware that refusal meant the severance of friendship, Morris nevertheless declared that their policy faced the crucial test. As they had always upheld the right of the sick, so must they uphold [179]

the right of the chiropractor, for the cause was common to both. A neurocalometer, which Tom 

Morris heard could be made for thirty dollars, could not be forced upon the profession at a figure so exorbitant as to be commensurate with an interest of 7,000 per cent.

This private appeal by Dr. Palmer occurred at the Chicago Convention of 1925. Morris addressed the delegates, enunciated again the principles which condemned the nerve-tester, and tendered his resignation as chief counsel of the association. Dr. Palmer followed with his resignation as secretary.

Stormy scenes occurred. The convention was asked to decide which of the two leaders should be retained. Dr. Heinrich Derringer, of New York, one of the most influential chiropractors of the country, flayed Morris in an indignant speech. 

Three years later on his death bed, it is said, he called for Morris to come and forgive him—an example of that spirit which prompted Dr. Palmer and his wife to send a floral tribute to the funeral of Tom Morris in New York in September, 1928.

The outcome of the vote on the convention floor was that Morris was reinstated. The office of the association was moved from Palmer School to the suite occupied by the attorney in La Crosse, Wisconsin. [180]

ORGANIZATIONS 

This was the climax of events preceding the loss of control by Dr. Palmer. He had returned from a world tour to discover the Palmer supremacy all but overthrown. That autocracy, which the leader himself had often confidentially discussed with his immediate associates, but which he had found could not be alluded to on the lecture platform, was plainly doomed. The era of democracy for chiropractic apparently had begun. But the membership of the old organization was hopelessly split.

The Democratic Urge 

The undercurrent of activity in chiropractic organization for many years has tended to displace the authority of the educators. The dream of farseeing leaders has been to establish a government for the profession that would give full expression to the chiropractic field at large. Time and again the rivalries of schools, which have controlled their graduates to an astonishing degree, have developed political leaders, whose service has been partisan, tending to prevent that solidarity which is of especially vital importance to chiropractic. Often young leadership has been blamed for the outcroppings of inherited conditions, and the vaunted need of more dominating leadership has but confused the issue. The hopes of the more democratic leaders [181]

have been crystallized in the forming of the International Congress and the new National Chiropractic Association.

The Chiropractic Health Bureau, organized in 

Davenport, Iowa, by Dr. Palmer, September 4, 1926, has gained strength as a protective association, having taken up insurance after the methods of the Universal Chiropractors Association though charging somewhat higher rates. As a non-profit organization it undertakes to insure chiropractors against malpractice liabilities and the losses incidental to prosecution. The object of the bureau is broadly stated in its constitution as follows: 

The organization shall aim by research, publicity, combative and defensive legislation, lawful legal protection, cooperation, and in every legitimate and ethical way, to promote and advance the Philosophy, Science and Art of Chiropractic and the professional welfare of its members, to the end that every locality shall have knowledge of Chiropractic and have the unhampered right and opportunity of obtaining the services of chiropractors of unquestionable standing and ability; this organization without reservation affirming its belief in the justice of the principle of allowing the sick to seek and obtain the services of practitioners of their own choice, of whatever calling, or school; and this Bureau to undertake and attempt everything that it can legally and lawfully do in the defense of this principle. 

The officers consist of a president, who holds office for five years, and three vice-presidents, who [182]

ORGANIZATIONS 

have three-year terms, also a secretary and treasurer each having terms of one year. All officers are elected. Members pay dues of ten dollars yearly and are subject to assessments of an equal amount, when in the opinion of the officers more funds are necessary to carry on the business of the bureau. 

The association undertakes to defend members who may be wrongfully accused.of practicing medicine, surgery or osteopathy without a license, and also members sued for malpractice, provided always no other means or agency besides chiropractic have been employed. Any other criminal charge deemed unjust by the bureau, preferred against a member, calls for its service. The prosecuted member is expected to employ his own counsel, who looks after the case under the direction of the general counsel of the bureau. If the defendant and his lawyer comply with the provisions of the constitution of the bureau, the latter reimburses the defendant for the fees paid to the local counsel, not exceeding fifty dollars, and also for taxable costs and penalties. 

Insurance 

An announcement from the Chiropractic 

Health Bureau, dated July 28, 1930, showed a total membership of 625 chiropractors, living in fortyfour states, six Canadian provinces and in foreign [183]

countries.

The total receipts from all sources from March 1, 1930, to May 31, 1930, were reported as $9,335.31, and the total bank balances of the bureau in all funds, as of February 28 of that year, $15,372.99. Disbursements of three months, to May 31, amounted to $6,519.30. Between February 28 and May 31 cash reserves gained $2,- 816.01. 

Some additional idea of the sums previously expended for protection within the profession is afforded by a statement on the part of Dr. Waldo G. 

Poehner, printed in the U.C.A. News, of October 

15, 1927, to the effect that the cost of administration of the Universal Chiropractors Association in the recent past had reached the sum of $122,000 a year, the membership being 1,450. In 1927 the receipts and a small balance carried from the previous year totaled $73,877.72, of which $71,856.66 was disbursed. The total disbursements of the previous year had been nearly double that sum. 

Receipts from assessments during the fiscal year ending July 31, 1924, were $208,341, the total receipts, $235,297.46. The disbursements exceeded the receipts by $41,734.12. Receipts in 1925 and 

1926 fell to $172,000 and $106,000 respectively, the disbursements each year slightly exceeding the income. [184]

ORGANIZATIONS 

The large protective associations have operated in a manner similar to those beneficiary organizations devoted primarily to insurance. Profits and losses have been shared by the members. Surplus has been used against litigation and assessments have been based on the requirements of defense. Premiums have ranged from one dollar a year, for state affiliations, to the present average of sixty dollars paid by members of the Chiropractic Health Bureau. 

In the conduct of cases the national counsel, as a rule, went to the scene of the action, although a local attorney was engaged. Experts, medical as well as chiropractic, were hired and witnesses subpoenaed. Thus the expense of cases ran from $1,500 to $5,000; in a single instance, $10,000.* 

In 1926 the board of directors and management of the U.C.A. received criticism over the handling of affairs. A petition was circulated among the members for the purpose of having the records of the association investigated. Further dissatisfaction was voiced by those who objected to the salary paid the general counsel. A reply of protest was received from the association, which had moved from the Palmer School of Chiropractic in Davenport to the Linker Building, La Crosse, Wisconsin. The *From a statement by Dr. B. J. Palmer. 

[185]

attacks were alleged to come from the promoters of other defense organizations and, it was declared, were formed of misstatements and half truths. 

It was pointed out that in Texas the national counsel had won in forty-five criminal cases and lost to local counsel in only four cases during the period between January 20, 1925, and February 15, 1926.

The total records showed that the U.C.A. had won every malpractice case except one in twenty-two months.

Before the petition to the members had gained the required signatures, the officers of the association voluntarily conducted an investigation of the records and a report was sent to all members. The publication of figures came too late, however, to offset the growing disaffection in the membership, whose confidence had been jarred by the criticism and strife. From this year to the final toppling of this pillar of chiropractic, support waned and it was a considerably diminished group that finally entered the merger with the American Chiropractic Association under the new name of the National 

Chiropractic Association. 

Signs of an approaching end to a long era of rivalries and antagonisms within the profession appeared in 1930 with the strengthening of the 

International Congress and the amalgamation 

[186]

ORGANIZATIONS 

which brought the National Chiropractic Association into being. The feudal age of chiropractic indeed seemed to be passing. The seriousness of the strife had come to be understood in every corner of the entire field. Less patience was being shown toward constantly rising new leadership of an egotistical stripe, grotesquely patterned after the pioneers. On the other hand more indulgence was developing toward sincere, self-effacing effort, for co-operation was seen as the prime need. Thus the fighting spirit, that inherent radicalism inherited by the sons and daughters of chiropractic, began to be tempered with a sense of calm maturity and the firmer dignity of recognition. The portals had finally opened, Law had smiled upon the newcomer and now Science, wondering, had ceased to sneer.

Before the end of 1931, it is said, the International Congress of Chiropractic and the National Chiropractic Association may unite their forces. 

Now working in harmony, this merger, embracing as it would practically all reputable elements, would constitute the greatest co-ordinative move in chiropractic history. No rivalry exists between these organizations and the American Bureau of 

Chiropractic. The latter with its lay membership [187]

stands closer to the general public and is primarily intent upon legislation.

The difficulties that obtain in any kind of professional organization, where intense individualization operates against co-operation, have been greatly multiplied in chiropractic. Its exponents have been hard to lead and as addicted to dispute as were the first few, who fought at every fleck and flicker in the chiropractic kaleidoscope. Continually working with the hands tends to make a man or woman self-sufficient, persecution breeds independence, and so nothing short of an extreme abiding danger common to all would have called these professionals to the colors and imbued them with an organization spirit. 

Today's leaders in these national and international organizations are aware of the problems and pitfalls within and without the profession. Some of the leaders are astute politicians, who have had to cope with the medical adversary in the campaigns of years. They have seen pretentious organizations come and go. They will avoid, for example, the errors that doomed the National

Federation of State Chiropractic Associations. 

Here was a body that struggled for eight years before it collapsed for lack of support. Begun in 

1912 and sponsored by the heads of the largest [188]

ORGANIZATIONS 

schools, dissension quickly deprived it of the vital force necessary to chiropractic. Dr. B. J. Palmer, disapproving of the management, lost interest and penned a diatribe from Davenport which apparently sealed the doom of the Federation, which, however, survived at least in name until 1917, when reorganization occurred. But even with new blood the sinews failed to respond. The far-flung, heterogeneous associations failed to line up, and the fault lay mainly with the individual. Group consciousness had not yet been forged from the raw metal of insurrectionism. By 1920 the National 

Federation of State Chiropractic Associations had ceased to open its few dust-laden books. It had never gained strength, nor had it accomplished any memorable benefit, although a journal-The Backbone-was published from Chicago, where the headquarters were situated. The federation merely added to the inauspicious precedent which threatened to becloud forever any attempt to organize chiropractic on an adequate scale. That some genius for organization has been born out of past experience seems evident from the more recent successes and the scope of activity. With the waning domination of the schools, doctors in the field have come to guide the destiny of the profes- [189]

sion, helping to render it immune from pedagogic strife.

The comparative strength of the National Association and the International Congress is variously estimated at present. On one hand, recognized cohesive elements, which have survived many years of changes and disruptions, denote solidarity. They represent a surviving old guard inured to long campaigns and the hardest winters of chiropractic. On the other hand strong factors from the state associations and educational forces of the country are combined in a mighty constructive effort. A new idealism has sprung up on all sides. Many of the members of one national association are members of the other. The present keynote is Co-operation. 

Less tolerance than ever before exists for selfish motives and local aggrandizement, so that the plans for a still greater legion born of these far-flung forces may indeed be fulfilled in the near future. [190]

CHAPTER X Democracy or Autocracy 

HERE seems to be no immediate likelihood of an alliance between the National Chiropractic Association and the International

Congress with the Chiropractic Health Bureau. 

The stumbling block is the mechanical accessory, in spite of present policies which aim to circumvent it. Not that the former young organizations have embraced the philosophy of the physio-therapists. Such is far from the case. It is that their leaders believe that any commandment against the use of modalities will do more harm than good. 

That any present organization or individual has enough power to induce all practitioners especially all "mixers"-to confine their service to manipulation, is deemed preposterous. It is strongly felt that the more rigorous the policy the wider will become the cleavage between "straights" and "mixers," and to prevent this, vigorous efforts are now being made. It is believed that the integrity of chiropractic can be maintained only by a gen- [191]

eral professional recognition of the sufficiency of its theories, and in this are agreed all leaders of the national organizations, in and out of Davenport. 

The respective policies of the N.C.A. and Congress and those of Dr. Palmer, as controlling the health bureau, are as follows: The advisory groups of the two first named are disposed, for the present at least, to let the status of chiropractic be determined by a campaign of education throughout the lay and professional spheres. By unremitting emphasis, the superior advantages of chiropractic care and service will become established more firmly. 

The prestige of the theory will be enhanced in public opinion; the chiropractor relying too much upon modalities will be inspired to adhere to first principles. Of more value to the practitioner than side money from modalities is a new and greater recognition of chiropractic. But if chiropractic is to become but a single mode among many, the adjusting table but a dingy relic among the brave accoutrements of a modern drugless healing establishment, then the very foundation will have crumbled, and "straight" and "mixer" alike will be adrift in the fickle waters of an uncharted sea. 

While fully comprehending that the proof of the pudding is in the eating and that the public, in any event, will decide whether to have its chiro- [192]

practic "straight" or "mixed," Dr. Palmer is at present diametrically opposed to concessions. He sees chiropractic as a delicate prodigy requiring a firm hand, its inherited noncompliance as something to be deplored but conquered. As this book goes to press news comes of his refusal to adopt the policies of the N.C.A. and Congress, which he labels "straw organizations." The following are excerpts from an article by Dr. B. J. Palmer in 

Fountain Head News, of February, A.C., 35 (alias,

February, 1931). The article is entitled "The ACA-UCA Union-What Does It Mean?" Pur porting to give primarily an analysis of the new 

National Chiropractic Association, Dr. Palmer takes the opportunity of stating his own position in unmistakable terms. This has created a sensation among the leaders of the organizations named, and much uncertain weather is prognosticated. 

Like a presidential message in parts, it lays bare the vital needs of chiropractic, constituting an important record at an epochal period. After discussing the careers of the merged associations, he writes: 

Dr. Palmer's Criticism 

"The UCA now passed into the hands of the wrecking crew-those who thought more of their own pleasures than a sacrificing service to a cause; .. [ 193 ]

"Naturally, and as is to be expected, the profession gravitated into two groups those who were for a firm controlling hand, and those who wanted to run wild without restraint. Those who were for that sort of thing joined the ACA and helped on its objectives, . 

"The ACA, therefore, was born of opposition to the UCA and all it stood for. It was a playground for mixers who wanted the fruit of Chiropractic without earning the right to Chiropractic by helping to sustain it. . . ... "Both organizations . . . were granting everything medical any Chiropractor wanted. . . 

"When any organization ceases to legitimately and objectively serve those who pay its dues, they drop out, and it drops down. Both organizations were existing in name. ... "Overtures were made to me, when UCA Secretary, to unite them. It was impossible. The UCA was (then) serving Chiropractic, the ACA was serving Chiropractors. When I passed out of the picture, overtures were made to Tom Morris. It was still impossible. The same difference in objectives and business policies existed in Tom Morris' mind as in mine, therefore the same barrier. When 

Tom Morris passed out of the picture, and the UCA passed into weaker hands, overtures were [194]

again made. It now became a possibility. Were not both serving the same master? One thing positive faced them both-obliteration-or amalgamation. . . "The strength of any 'Chiropractic' organization rests solely and alone in its Chiropractic objectives.... .. · 

ee I restate now what I have told the officers of that International Congress from the beginning, and annually ever since... that The International Congress would live if it set forth, adhered to and deliberately maintained Chiropractic objectives; ..." 

Dr. Palmer follows with a prediction that the

National Chiropractic Association and the Inter national Congress will combine, but he doubts that the united protective associations will accomplish anything, because of their predisposition to compromise. ". . . give every Chiropractor anything he wants in Chiropractic and thus the more quickly force him into his own downfall and ruinlegally, legislatively, commercially, as well as economically."

He most emphatically declares the need for strong, experienced, centralized leadership. The 

A.C.A. and U.C.A., he says, suffered because of too many leaders. The International Congress was con- [195]

ceived of a desire to "get the leaders together." He adds: "They have. Now none follow; all direct. 

Reminds me of a Mexican or Chinese revolutionall generals and no privates. "There are enough men on each side to block any definite program that anybody on either side might propose. . . . "There are some Chiropractors in that present united group [presumably N.C.A.] that are so prejudiced personally, and antagonistic Chiropractically, because of the years past having developed it into them, that they will continue to sacrifice everything Chiropractic, rather than yield now to its better interests, . . . There are other Chiropractors in that combined group who are so interested in the 'peace-at-any-cost, pacifistic, giving preference to amalgamation glory,' that they will sacrifice Chiropractic to attain any or all those ends. There are some mighty fine, high-principled Chiropractors, also, in that present officership of the united associations, who, having had no experience, think that certain ways are the correct ways to attain certain ends. "I know well the man or men who have engineered this amalgamation. For them, their motives and sincerity, I have only the highest admiration. .. The vital question yet remains-what can be [ 196]

the possible program that can spring from such a mixed conglomeration of men, . . .? Outside of those groups... there is and has been existing one group of Chiropractors who have builded an organization that thot more of Chiropractic than it did anything else; who are opposed to a 'peace-at-anyprice' program if Chiropractic is to be sacrificed; will not endorse any amalgamation where Chiropractic comes off second best. If it can be shown that Chiropractic is the first, great, and only objective of amalgamation, it might change the complexion of much. Can this be done, other than with high-sounding titles, words, and promises, evangelical enthusiasm, of which our profession has had too much? Time will tell! "If human nature will reverse itself on one side of the equation, and every man lay down his cherished theories of self-independence, and all resign themselves to do only that which is best for Chiropractic then and not until then-will this organization be of value to Chiropractic. If that were done, the problem is solved; .. ... "In rebuttal to some of these statements, any one of these organizations will say that they have been serving Chiropractic. They use the name 'Chiropractic' for their organization. But, when any organization will declare itself for everything [197]

medical, including tonsillectomy by surgery, and deny the efficacy of Chiropractic adjustments, is that serving Chiropractic? When another organization will take page ads of medical apparatus and declare itself in favor of medical methods in substitution for Chiropractic methods, is that serving 

Chiropractic? When another organization will meet year after year and its delegates are cautioned about discussing Chiropractic, for fear it will cause a rumpus in the path of a serene and happy meeting, is that serving Chiropractic? Citations could be given without end. . . . "This new organization can be made to be a dominant, strong factor in the future of Chiropractic and be of incalculable value to Chiropractors." 

Dr. Palmer's Platform 

Here Dr. Palmer suggests strict adherence to chiropractic as defined, a fuller study of the field of service, a "no pleading guilty" policy at trials of malpractice suits and not seeking to get out of each suit in the cheapest way possible; that each case be carried through and tried on its merits, regardless of cost; no settling cases out of court or pleading guilty to save expense; a prompt and honest settlement of legitimate claims on the part of members; [198]

a definite inter-state legislative policy and provision for defeating adverse legislation. 

He recommends a definite program among affiliated state organizations for the development of a fair and reasonable chiropractic educational standard, providing for "new chiropractic growth ... to take the place of those who numerically are rapidly dying out."

On this score Dr. Palmer condemns the late 

American Chiropractic Association for having in instances encouraged "the raising of all standards" to equal those of medical education. 

The importance of an encouraging attitude toward affiliated state organizations is stressed. 

Annual programs should include only chiropractic speakers, and only chiropractic subjects and exhibits should be permitted on the floor of the convention rooms. Dr. Palmer's tenth suggestion is "A definite and understandable practice program encouraging and urging its members to confine their practice to that which everybody knows is Chiropractic." 

He recalls the fact that at the Louisville convention the A.C.A. chairman outraged sensibilities by introducing him with "Give him thirty minutes to tell us what chiropractic is." "Which he knew [199]

as well as everybody else in the profession," adds 

Dr. Palmer. 

He advocates a more far-seeing co-operation with chiropractic state boards, and the building of a safe financial structure for the national organization, proof against the misuse of funds. 

Dr. Palmer makes it clear that he does not challenge the honesty of anyone on the staff of the united organization, but merely recognizes a ubiquitous tendency to spend organization money with a free hand. "I am going to be asked to unite the CHB with this new organization. When the new organization has built itself up to a program platform equal to that of the CHB, then one or the other will automatically cease to exist; for two associations with the same objectives cannot live in the same profession. The reverse of that was what happened to the ACA and the UCA. When the UCA got down to the ACA, non-Chiropractical level, then they were two bodies trying to occupy the same space. One had to cease to exist; it had no cause for existence, and was absorbed. It took 6 years for the UCA to come down. How long will it take the new organization to come up?... [200]

A Plea for Leadership 

"I was asked to assume leadership of this CHB.

I refused to have anything to do with it unless they placed Chiropractic first and adopted that policy program in spirit and in toto. . . . "Meanwhile, the CHB stands on a sound financial business basis. No officer in it, no member in its ranks gets any salary, regardless of how much or how little work he does for its objectives. It has a cash reserve practically equal to that which the 

UCA had in its heyday of its biggest membership.

Those employees who do receive salary get a modest stipend. Every dollar of income is being legitimately expended, working for Chiropractic. . . "I am not opposed to the new organization. . . .

It possesses tremendous potential possibilities. If it rises up to them, no one will rejoice more than I, because it will be serving Chiropractic-and that's all I am interested in, or ever have been. "It is too soon yet to either condemn, condone, or endorse. There are many entanglements in the way of immediate accomplishment. It is going to take at least five years for a sincere evolution to take place in many in its ranks. . . . ... "There is room for a dozen 'Chiropractic' organizations when each has a divided objective; but [201]

there is room for only one Chiropractic organization when there is a Chiropractic objective. "I am just as set in these convictions as Chiropractic is right. I am just as 'stubborn' as Chiropractic being right dictates. I have no other course. 

"Ever since 1924, the profession, its state associations, its national protective associations, its International Congress, has been seeking a leader. Is he hiding in that new united group? Is he submerged in that International Congress? If he is, drag him out, put him in control against his will, make him work and save Chiropractic from its traducers." 

Whether chiropractic needs dictatorship or whether it needs democracy, whether it will be made or broken by the modality wheels that now vibrate torture to the hopes of "straights," time will tell. How far the field will be guided by the forum, to what extent pedagogic influence may still be necessary to preserve theory intact amid vagaries of practice, are problems more fundamental than those of polity. Involved in their solution is the eternal question of leadership, upon which all else depends. [202]

CHAPTER XI Modalities 

HE invasion of machinery, which for some years has been increasing the clinical appearance of physicians' offices, has likewise altered the offices of many chiropractors. The conservatives view the adoption of mechanical accessories with apprehension, since "done by hand" may cease to characterize chiropractic and the fundamentals of the system be buried under a regalia of wheels, lenses and meters.

Not only to aid diagnosis but to afford special treatment have the appliances been installed. The majority of chiropractors employ some form of mechanical accessory. There is a wide range of opinions and prejudices respecting the value of these adjuncts, a rigid alignment of the more extreme views existing in California. Here, however, as already stated, efforts are being made to reconcile the "straights" and "mixers." 

The adoption of the "modalities," or mechanical devices, began on an extensive scale in 1924 and [203]

was largely caused by a period of depression which prompted many chiropractors in their uneasiness to annex specialties for the purpose of strengthening their clientele.
