The office of the "straight" chiropractor, to whom the philosophy of chiropractic is as broad as the entire subject of health and disease, is equipped with an adjusting table, rest cots and a few chairs. 

His "mixer" colleague's, however, glistens with

X-ray apparatus, heat lamps, vibrators, or possibly spinal meter instruments, "radionics" and hydrotherapy equipment, clinical thermometers and other devices. These are regarded as merely accessory to the main work of vertebral adjustment, yet much more time is necessarily required for the tests and treatments through the use of the accessories. 

There is the necessitated extra space; extra assistants and elaborate records are required. From an economic point of view the question has frequently been raised whether the chiropractor employing the "modalities" to any extent actually increases his revenue, considering the added overhead expense, installment payments and extra time devoted to the patient. Often occurs the criticism that greater reliance should be placed upon pure chiropractic, that its scientific findings are by no means completed, and, consequently, that time for im- [204]

MODALITIES 

portant experiment is sacrificed for the more perfunctory modes of treatment represented in the various appliances. It is maintained by such critics that the "mixer" is prone to content himself with the routine and rudiments of his school instead of analyzing each case according to the more profound teachings of chiropractic. On the other hand "straights" are dubbed reactionists.

It is chiefly with respect to their use of the modalities that chiropractors are divided into different classes. These classes have not become numerous, for the science and profession are too young, and the need of standardization too urgent. Whereas the "straight" chiropractor is readily identified and his beliefs are promptly understood, such is not the case with the "mixer," who ranges all the way from the practitioner who relies somewhat upon one or two mechanical devices, to one who employs all known paraphernalia and maintains chiropractic merely as a single department. Thus, while by far the larger percentage of "mixers" adhere to certain Palmer fundamentals, many nevertheless are as close to the medical camp as osteopaths, even in some places working in conjunction with physicians and surgeons. "Mixers" have been actuated by contrasting motives: here, a conscientious liber- [205]

alism; there, a desire for credentials and clientele at any price.

There seems to be little evidence that "mixers" object to this appellation, and the more dignified term "physio-therapists" being not generally applicable, is not acceptable. Some users of accessories have gone so far as to devise the title "chiropractic physician," but this combination is condemned for obvious reasons. 

Where no chiropractic law exists the "mixers" are few, since employing modalities renders the user subject to the medical law which specifies that that which "stimulates or inhibits" is to be construed as of medical treatment. In states having chiropractic examining boards, however, "mixers" abound, because there, it is said, medical politicians are ever occupied in trying to divide the chiropractic profession.

The X-ray is dubbed the original sin the "mixer's" first drink. A few chiropractors, however, had acquired Abram's "radionics." Then the neurocalometer let down the bars for the inroad of appliances. Dr. Palmer insisted that this machine was compatible with chiro-praktikos-in fact was an aid to "straight" chiropractic. Many disagreed with him. [206]

MODALITIES 

Had the terms for leasing neurocalometers been less rigorous, Dr. Palmer might have persuaded the profession that the "nerve-tester" was in no sense alien to chiropractic science but a corroborative adjunct capable of demonstrating the infallibility of hand palpation. But as an improvement on the latter, it was sure to invite dissension, whatever the cost of using it.

The great majority of the users of modalities regard themselves as chiropractors in the fullest sense, so it appears as another chiropractic tragedy that factionalism has arisen, or has been produced during legislative campaigns, reacting upon the entire profession. The need of cooperation has become thoroughly apparent to all, and the last two or three years have witnessed an unprecedented centralization through the merging of organizations.

That the issue already raised on the Pacific Coast, however, may become of universal concern is suggested by the fundamental question involved, and since chiropractic is virtually a religion among a majority of its sponsors, the articles of faith are likely to engender schismatic differences comparable with those of religious groups. While against this contingency state and national organizations are laboring for harmony, the fight of the older [207]

generation of chiropractors has been directed against a loss of individuality and absorption by medical interests. If the absolute adequacy of vertebral adjustment is contradicted by the "modalities," then according to the old guard of chiropractors, much that has been gained in the way of recognition will be dissipated and irrevocably lost. 

Light therapy and hydro-therapy have a substantial following among the practitioners of the country, as have dietary and diagnostic methods, massage and foot correction. It is estimated that approximately a third of the chiropractors in the 

United States employ electro-therapy.

The trend of chiropractic in the use of methods and accessories was sought by a survey published in 

1930 by the Lincoln School of Chiropractic at In dianapolis. As the "mixers" of the country have been under fire, it is probable that they have more generally responded to the questionnaire than the "straights" and some allowance for this should be made. Thus, a somewhat greater acceptance of alien uses and theories may appear in the painstaking questionnaire of this prominent Indiana school than that which actually occurs. It is instructive, however, to note the following replies from more than 1,800 chiropractors: [208]

The subject of "spinography" has attained much importance, involving the study and use of X-ray. Nerve testing by mechanical means is also an accredited study subject in some schools. Leading institutions recognize to a greater or less degree various practices that have come to be adopted in the field, although many of the latter have nothing essential to chiropractic. The X-ray is in general [211]

favor because of its accurate showing of lateral, superior and inferior subluxations, despite its limitations in listing posterior and anterior vertebrae. 

It is primarily intended to serve as a means of verification of the findings deduced through palpation and major analysis, and as such saves the time of both chiropractor and patient, besides obviating possible mistakes. [212]

CHAPTER XII Chiropractic Training 

HERE ARE approximately 300 schools which teach chiropractic, including about seven colleges of outstanding importance. 

Many of the smaller schools have more or less extraneous subjects in their courses, some even teaching chiropractic from a rather medical point of view. 

Before the coming of chiropractic legislation the natural tendency among the schools of the profession was to set their own standards. There was comparatively little agreement as to what constituted the essentials beyond the rudiments as originally taught. "Diploma mills" flourished for a time in nearly every large city, casting discredit upon chiropractic and releasing a horde of incompetents whose chief ambition was to get rich quickly. 

Among them were a few more conscientious spirits who succeeded in learning something as they went along experimenting, and eventually some of these earned a measure of recognition in the field. [213]

The very first laws demanded at least a high school education before one might study chiropractic, and patients soon acquired the art of discriminating between the high school product and the less erudite pretender. The fact that chiropractic gained its following in spite of charlatanry, disrepute and open attack is frequently given as one of the outstanding proofs of its merits. large percentage of its adherents sought out the chiropractor as a last resort after all other treatment had failed.

As the donations of patients saved the Palmer school in its early days, similarly grateful friends of chiropractic came to the relief of many struggling schools. But for the most part funds for education were woefully lacking. Never have endowments been forthcoming for chiropractic schools and hospitals. It has never been the simple expedient of naming an institution after some saint, then asking religious persons to lend support. 

Medical hospitals and many schools have had churches behind them. Chiropractic has been compelled to battle alone and against mighty opposition, and certain forces that normally might have been counted upon to uplift its standards assailed it. Yet in some ways obstruction brought hardier advancement. [214]

CHIROPRACTIC TRAINING 

With the gaining of recognition came the power to drive out imposters. Gradually the correspondence courses and night schools began to disappear. Legislation and organization were erecting a stronger shaft for the new column, which,

America was beginning to realize, was not merely an unstable replica of osteopathy but something able to stand on its own base. 

Carver Chiropractic College in Oklahoma City, which was the second large institution to come into being (1906), after about two years of instruction lengthened its course from six to nine months. By 1910 it had been extended to eighteen months, which remained as the minimum until 1927, when it became twenty months. In 1930 the course was changed to three years of ten months each, thus covering thirty months. 

Standardization 

The first efforts to standardize courses of instruction in various schools of the country occurred at about the year 1917 and were practically fruitless. Further than fulfilling the immediate state requirements and some disposition to pave the way toward reciprocal privileges among states, no extensive correlation of schedules has since taken place. The International Congress of Chiropractic 

[215]

plans to investigate all institutions teaching chiropractic and to maintain supervision over their work. Since many of the leading educators are active in the congress and its subsidiary organizations, this standardizing experiment is expected to bring notable results.

Among the varied curricula appear courses in embryology, orthopedy, pediatrics, dissection and first aid. The X-ray, discovered the same year as chiropractic, has become important in diagnosis, giving birth to the subject of "spinography."

Jurisprudence has received considerable attention, due to the many state and local laws and regulations, without knowledge of which the young chiropractor is likely to encounter serious difficulties. Carver College in 1929 found it feasible to establish a department of physio-therapy. 

The following three-year course reveals the degree to which a leading chiropractic school has followed the trend of legislation and the adoption of the modalities, while adhering nevertheless to the fundamentals of chiropractic.

Business English Orthopedia Public Speaking Personal Efficiency The International Congress of Chiropractic has found it expedient to give temporary recognition to all schools having adequate courses, pending personal inspection by representatives of the congress. 

The obvious difficulty of holding together the school constituents in the face of a regulative program affecting their destinies for better or worse has proved another argument for catholicity in the control of the organization. This openmindedness has been demonstrated in numerous ways, particularly in extending membership to the 

National School of Chiropractic of Chicago despite the fact of its doctors being admitted to the county hospital under the medical banner. [217]

As already stated in Chapter IX relating to organizations, the International Congress is partly composed of members of state boards of chiropractic examiners. Depending upon the enduring strength of the congress and the scope of co-ordination through state boards, educational standards in the various states will continue to be more definitely realized and upheld. The control of curricula up to the present time has reflected legislation rather than professional organization and actually dates from the passage of the first chiropractic law in Kansas in 1913. Standardization has been proportionate with increasing recognition, thereby causing advocates of chiropractic to affirm repeatedly that most of the incompetence in the field is directly traceable to discriminative laws and persecution. In those states where favorable chiropractic laws came into being and chiropractic leaders stepped into authority, spurious instruction found it more and more difficult to function. 

Three years after the passage of the Oklahoma law in 1921, the chiropractic examiners ruled that no college or school would be deemed reputable if it maintained home extension, correspondence or evening courses. Examinations and licenses became necessary, so study must be thorough and certificates authentic and adequate. Thus the chiro- [218]

CHIROPRACTIC TRAINING 

practic institution was given less leeway than the academic institution, as is proper where technical training is involved.

Efforts to standardize instruction have evolved such suggested courses as the following for a chiropractic college of the first rank. The number of study hours, which is somewhat in excess of those commonly required, here serves merely to indicate the comparative importance of the subjects. Obstetrics is not taught in the majority of schools, since it involves surgery. Pediatrics, not included below, is taught in some schools. Numerous other variations occur. 

Anatomy, including gross anatomy, embryology, neurology, histology and orthopedy, 955 hours; physiology and chemistry, including physiology, biological and physiological chemistry, toxicology, urinalysis and dietetics, 380 hours; pathology, including gross pathology, pathological histology and bacteriology, 300 hours; hygiene, including public health and public health service, 100 hours; diagnosis or analysis, including spinography (X-ray), 430 hours; obstetrics and gynecology, 100 hours; chiropractic, including chiropractic symptomatology, chiropractic principles and practice, chiropractic palpation and adjusting, chiropractic jurisprudence, contagious and infectious diseases, [219]

eye, ear, nose and throat and dermatology, 1,147 hours; elective subject or subjects, 116 hours. 

This total of 3,528 hours is only seventy-two hours less than the minimum total required in medical courses. Chiropractors receive 307 hours more than medical students in the subject of anatomy. None of the time is spent studying the lower animals. Forty-eight percent, or 1,728 hours of the medical curriculum, is devoted to medicine and surgery, which are not included in the chiropractic course. Chemistry in the medical school relates to about 27,000 drugs and chemicals, whereas in the chiropractic school it applies solely to the human body. It is indicated, furthermore, that chiropractors spend 247 hours more on the study of the science of chiropractic than the medical doctors spend on the subject of medicine, including pediatrics, nervous and mental diseases, dermatology and syphilis and medical jurisprudence. As chiropractors are not permitted to attend in cases of childbirth, during actual labor, or to practice surgery, their study of obstetrics and gynecology covers only 100 hours, as compared with the 252 hours required of medical students. It is observed that the 3,528 hours of subjects, as here set forth, are devoted entirely to the human body in health and disease, while only 1,872 hours of subjects in [220]

CHIROPRACTIC TRAINING 

medical study pertain to the human body in health and disease, if one excludes the subjects bearing on drugs and surgery.

In most of the states considerably fewer study hours than those in the above suggested course are sufficient to secure full credentials. The California law, for example, demands only 2,400 hours, although some of the "progressives" are seeking to include subjects that will bring the total to 3,600 hours or possibly 4,000 hours. The present 2,400 hours are assigned as follows: Anatomy, 600; histology, 100; elementary chemistry and toxicology, 100; physiology, 200; bacteriology, 100; hygiene and sanitation, 100; pathology, 200; diagnosis or analysis, 400; chiropractic theory and practice, 500; obstetrics and gynecology, 100. 

That numerous practitioners with only an original six-months training eventually gained state credentials, and various schools once regarded as purely commercial enterprises finally became accredited institutions with full three-year terms, should properly be regarded as of the same phenomena of development seen in other drugless healing systems and in the ancient days of medicine itself. Qualifications came apace with recognition, capability with qualifications, public service with capability. That which brought recognition to [221]

chiropractic, changing the character of its schools and its service, was the proven testimony of cured patients which finally converted newspapers, courts and legislatures. In the same ratio that honor attaches itself to public office, superior men come into public service, and this rule has long applied to every profession.

The Humble Environment 

When chiropractic teaching was in its first formative years it was realized that a generation of students might come and go before the profession would attract a large proportion of young men and women holding college degrees. No profession ever had a humbler beginning, and as one looks today at the great Davenport institution it is difficult to imagine the simple efforts that marked its origin. It seems almost incredible that one individual's initiative could have brought about so vast an establishment in so short a time. It is more understandable to those who saw B. J. Palmer emerge from poverty through sheer persistencewho since have sensed the inspiration of Mrs. 

Palmer, known to the chiropractic world as "Mabel." It is still bewildering to the few who know that B. J. Palmer began his teaching at a time when he was actually destitute, and a piano [222]

CHIROPRACTIC TRAINING 

box was his sleeping place. His companions were as poor as himself. Among the young laborers and loungers of Davenport were some sufficiently curious to listen to the theory of chiropractic as passed on to Bartlett Joshua by his father. Even after the fruits of his teachings began to be seen and Palmer School of Chiropractic was a reality, this keenly thinking youth realized that it would be time wasted to try to interest cultured persons in chiropractic. In fact, in the struggle for expression, the only hope lay among the less profound individuals to whom he was accustomed and who would give readier ear. So B. J. Palmer would tell some truck driver or barber of the secret of disease as due to impingements at the spine. A demonstration would follow. Possibly the volunteer pupil would be relieved of a headache or lameness. From this to a lesson on vertebrae was but a little step. Thus the carefully guarded knowledge of D. D. Palmer began to percolate through an ever widening circle of amazed neighbors, who began to bring their relatives and friends so that young Palmer might make an adjustment and explain the mystery. 

It is said that D. D. Palmer remonstrated with his son because of the latter's premature activities as a healer. The young man, however, was head- [223]

strong and greatly absorbed in experiment. The very fact that one did not have to be educated in order to help suffering was in itself significant. 

Some of these crude disciples would never comprehend anatomy or histology, nor would it greatly matter. It was sufficient to locate the impingement, then automatically, without all the bother and uncertainty of diagnosis, nature would do the rest. Assuredly much education was unnecessary, even possibly undesirable. In any event chiropractic could not wait for the embrace of culture; it would languish in outer offices before recognition would be given.

The first arrest of a graduate was that of a highly educated Japanese, Shegetaro Morikubo, of La Crosse, Wisconsin, in 1906. He was charged with "practicing medicine without a license." Dr.

B. J. Palmer's attention had been attracted to the name of Tom Morris, former lieutenant governor of Wisconsin, as a friend of drugless healers. Morris was engaged to defend the case and won it. The defendant later became prominent in Minneapolis. 

Before the Universal Chiropractic Association came into being that year Morris had become a confidant of Dr. Palmer, and together they planned much of the future of the science and profession which today has claimed world notice. Morris was en- [224]

CHIROPRACTIC TRAINING 

thused over the possibilities in chiropractic and bade good-bye to politics.

Strangely simple were the ingredients that went to establish the first school of chiropractic. The hardships and uncertainties already have been mentioned. It was fortunate that among the early students were those who had enough initiative to start schools of their own in other cities, for thus the work progressed and widened. During the early years the claims for chiropractic were extravagant; as experience ripened, these claims were tempered to a more professional attitude. The schools sought attention by means of startling statements. Since advertising space was generally denied, word-of-mouth information was especially relied upon. The schools were open to all, a policy necessary for self-preservation and the perpetuation of chiropractic.

Between 1913 and 1922 educational requirements for those applying to study chiropractic became established over a large part of the United States. The caliber of students has changed accordingly. Today the leaders of the profession are discussing the problem of attracting the best minds to a fuller study of chiropractic, as a preliminary to which recognized prejudices must be overcome. [225]

The schools and colleges of chiropractic now seem to be reacting to the program of the national organizations, by which the influence of mechanical accessories is to be offset by renewed emphasis upon first principles. With the more complete vindication of these principles will come the type of patronage necessary to enhance education-to support its activities on a plane similar to that enjoyed by other professional schools. [226]

CHAPTER XIII 

Ethics O SUCH DEFINITE code of ethics as that of the medical or legal profession obtains in chiropractic. The schools, however, seek to establish ideals of service, honor and discretion among their students. Efforts have been made in various organizations to decide upon precise rules of professional conduct, but rivalries in the field and perpetual disagreement as to proprieties have prevented the general adoption of any code. The fact that advertising seemed an unavoidable necessity from the earliest years and had the sanction of the founders tended to make the chiropractor less conservative than other professionals. Furthermore, with the many years of sensational publicity attending court proceedings and legislation in every state of the Union, the chiropractor acquired some of the characteristics of the free-lance, matters of ethics being for the concern of the individual rather than the group.

The more carefully taught young chiropractor [227]

is enabled to perceive that his first duty is always to his patient, that he assumes responsibility for the health and happiness of all who seek treatment at his hands. He is bidden to recognize his limitations, to refrain from criticising other doctors, irrespective of their school; to grant at all times the right of the patient to be treated by the practitioner of his choice. The chiropractor is encouraged to consider his work as of foremost humanitarian importance and his services available to the community. Monetary considerations must not interfere with zealous devotion to practice. He is warned against seeking political positions, asking favors, compromising his patient or himself or mixing in the personal affairs of his clients. Like the young physician he is sometimes bidden to learn the oath of Hippocrates, a part of which, after 2,200 years, still remains the logos of professional behavior, notably the lines: "I will give no deadly medicine to anyone if asked. . . . Into whatever houses I enter I will go into them for the benefit of the sick. . . . Whatever in connection with my professional practice, or not in connection with it, I see or hear in the life of men which ought not to be spoken of abroad I will not divulge, as reckoning that all such should be kept secret. . . ." Το offer or to accept a split fee is held to be inimical [228]

ETHICS 

to high standards. This principle has been widely disregarded, as in other professions, but a strong sentiment has arisen for its observance. Advertising As in the medical profession, and to a somewhat less degree among lawyers, architects and the more conservative clergymen, advertising has been an outstanding problem in the consideration of an ethical code, and rendered especially difficult in the case of chiropractic by its early struggle for a place in the sun. Organizations acquired certain rights denied ordinarily to individual practitioners. For instance, under the classification of constructive educational publicity continually arose most scathing denunciation of medicine and medical doctors. Irrespective of who was really the invader and who the defender in the field of public health the group voiced sentiments, which expressed by the unorganized individual would not have been tolerated. Alone, the chiropractor in the considerable majority of cases possesses those instincts necessary to ethical behavior. Organized, as a part of a protective and promotive system, his sensibilities dissolve into group consciousness, which on a large scale is capable of finding expression on a par with the most self-righteous nationalism. [229]

Strife and persecution have not made barren the chiropractor's sense of propriety, which is ultimately fostered and not stifled by the group. He is aware that misstatements in advertising will do him more harm than good in the long run. Exaggerated or fabricated stories of cures he knows will have a distasteful aftermath. The chiropractor is ordinarily required to promise, as a condition to receiving a license, to abstain from false statements in his advertising.

Dress Dr. J. R. Drain, in his stimulating book, Chiropractic Thoughts, discourses briefly upon ethics and observes incidentally that the matter of dress has been generally slighted. As a prelude to the former theme he advises chiropractors to adopt a conservative appearance—that of the average business man or business woman. Dr. Drain regards such as the most desirable, if uniform dress is to be considered. 

Chiropractors who array themselves like Dunkards, with Ben Franklin hats and long whiskers are a hindrance to the cause, he believes, since freakish appearance hints at freakish acts. This Texas edu cator has noted in the profession a long-haired type, a long-tailed coat type and a celluloid collar type. 

Because the Palmers let their hair grow does not [230]

ETHICS 

exonerate other chiropractors from the consequences of following suit. "I do not believe that the chiropractor should wear the cowboy hat, boots, or galluses," adds Dr. Drain, and he condemns the celluloid collar as unprofessional. 

Loyalty to the Profession 

Ethics is deemed to involve not only the attitude and behavior of the practitioner toward his client but also toward the science of chiropractic and the fellow chiropractor. Emphasis is often placed upon the obligation to aid in the support of the chiropractic cause. Here the responsibility of the chiropractor is unique among professions, for the long defensive battle against established curative systems has given birth to a code full of the consciousness of protective zeal. Organization leaders have rounded up the disinterested and delinquent during campaigns in a way suggestive of ward politics. For the chiropractor to be so immersed in his immediate duties as to forget his responsibilities toward the profession is held unethical, and the criticism of colleagues is sure to follow. In spite of this, however, a very large percentage of practitioners are induced only with greatest difficulty to take part in organized activities and many remain entirely aloof. The actual [231]

number of organization members in various states ranges from thirty to fifty per cent of the total number of chiropractors. Wrapped up in the loyalty of graduates of some of the older schools is much of the esoteric, shown in an almost religious fervor, and that strong group consciousness which is characteristic of oppressed minorities. Former 

Palmer students have been said to evince a cult-like attitude, due to the personal domination of Dr. 

B. J. Palmer and the almost symbolic influence of school traditions and associations. 

The young chiropractor, especially from the Davenport institution, is inclined to feel that he has become privileged to share in some special knowledge-a science as yet but illy comprehended by the modern, materialistic world—a treasure envied by learned physicians misunderstood and undervalued by contemporary science. His obligation toward his alma mater does not end with the payment of tuition. The "Fountain Head" holds an abiding interest in the absent sons and daughters, and as faces turn toward Mecca at the muezzin's call, so the Palmer graduates faithfully retrace their steps yearly to the great assembly known as the Lyceum, a gala event for the Iowan city. There the full significance of the common cause is renewed, fresh objectives are clarified, old loyalties dramatized. [232]

ETHICS 

To have fought the fight of chiropractic, to have endured imprisonment―ignominy-this breathes vitality into organization. Here is no musty chronicle of annual meetings, but a vast modern epic, whose stage has been set and whose drama has been lived in every clime under the sun. 

Chiropractic ethics reflect a sense of martyrdom and not even the sharp divisions of sentiment and technical disagreement are sufficient to nullify the cohesive spirit of the larger organizations. The duty of the individual toward the group, therefore, is clear-cut, for every chiropractor knows that only through the combined strength of the many can safety be assured. The advantages gained through legislation may be swept away if organization grows lax, and the only guarantee lies in the loyalty of the individual.

In order to understand the attitude of the chiropractor toward other chiropractors it is only necessary to realize the measure of difference between schools and the fact that not so many years have passed since degrees were obtainable upon an exceedingly short course of instruction. However, criticism of other chiropractors is held to be unethical. Much distrust abounds as to the methods of the rival practitioner, yet, as a matter of ethics, a chiropractor who would accept treatment at the [233]

hands of a medical doctor in preference to a chiropractor of any kind, would be deemed to have violated a most sacred code. This view, of course, would scarcely occur among the small minority, who occasionally recommend medicine and cooperate with physicians and surgeons.

The subject of ethics is included in the usual chiropractic course, despite the absence of a generally approved code. By training as well as environment professional attitude and conduct are shaped, and as obtains in every walk of life, the individual is finally left to follow the dictates of conscience and at least to keep within the law. Personality Realization of the importance of an abiding recognition of ethical standards under the aegis of organized chiropractic has developed considerably during recent years, and at the present time finds expression in the numerous journals of the profession. The perennial persecution and the stigma earned by early charlatans have made imperative more closely drawn rules of conduct, especially in view of the responsibilities of those entrusted with health and life. To dignify a science, a profession, a doctor's calling-in the face of widespread contempt and ridicule has been a heartfelt problem [234]

ETHICS 

for the leaders of chiropractic, the solution of which, however, has been aided by the accruing favorable legislation. But serious handicaps have existed in the tendency of the rank and file of practitioners to take lightly the subject of personal appearance and manner. A large percentage of the doctors of chiropractic have come from unpretentious walks of life, where dignity is easily confused with deception. Many habitually discard professional deportment, believing it less advantageous than a frank and sociable manner. These men and women, few of whom have had contact with cosmopolitan customs, have dreaded the appearance of artificiality, and seem to have been more or less unaware that success usually depends ´upon more than an ability to adjust vertebrae. The comparison between one of these homely-mannered chiropractors and the polished European practitioner is extraordinary, yet the discriminating patient may not appraise ability according to dress, manners or even general educational attainments. Tact 

The younger generation of chiropractors take more seriously matters of appearance and behavior. 

They are taught that duty to themselves and their profession demands an upstanding dignity, an [235]

agreeable and sympathetic attitude. Confidence on the part of the patient is vitally important. It is part of the work of the chiropractor to inspire the sufferer with the hope and expectation of recovery, and this is not accomplished by irrelevancies and lax behavior. Affectation is undesirable, and far more effectual is a sincere concentration upon the case itself. Direct simplicity is better than abstruseness, but tact is even more necessary to the doctor than to the diplomat, for the doctor cannot by contradiction cover his mistakes. 

Judicious selection of employees contributes to the confidence of clients. Nothing is more damaging to the repute of an office than an unbusinesslike or too businesslike atmosphere, unless it be the ill behavior of attendants. Courtesy is the first sign of intelligence.

The chiropractic student is advised against alarming his patients with technical phrases difficult to understand. If it is necessary to use them it is well to explain their meaning. Care and precision in all conversation with patients are to be observed. In money matters the simple rule of encouraging clear understanding about costs holds good, when the patient desires early to know the possible expense involved. Much disillusionment and discontent can be avoided by a pleasantly defi- [236]

ETHICS 

nite arrangement early in a course of treatments.

In an editorial in the Bulletin of the California 

Chiropractic Association appears the following counsel to the chiropractor: "You are the ambassador of your profession. The future of chiropractic is in your hands to mould into the profession of the future or to let die by neglect of the simple things. Guard well the treasure that has been placed in your hands for safe keeping." [237]

come 

CHAPTER XIV Public Health 

EALTH propaganda, once the bon marché of the patent medicines, in late years has bea national movement. Magazine pages are thronged with advice on the care of the digestion, teeth, hair, complexion and much that pertains to physical fitness. Governmental aid in health protection is traced from ancient days when populations waged a common battle against plague, and in America we have witnessed its co-operation in the wars on cholera, smallpox, yellow fever, diphtheria, malaria, typhoid, modern influenza and other scourges. But today preventive measures are by no means confined to the more dreaded contagious diseases, and the crusades of health authorities at present, according to chiropractic journals, are more or less of a medical ballyhooexploitation calculated to strengthen the hold of materia medica at a time when drugs are becoming unpopular. To the chiropractic point of view on this score reference has been made in Chapter 

V, Chiropractic versus Medicine. 

[238]

PUBLIC HEALTH 

Very naturally the scope of health propaganda undertaken thus far by chiropractic covers the field where it has deemed medicine to have proved especially incompetent. It has considered the victory over smallpox due to cleaner living, not to vaccine. It is skeptical of the mosquito's exclusive culpability and power to destroy healthy human kind. Medical efficiency in influenza has yet to be shown, declares chiropractic. The latter casts a fresh limelight of publicity upon increasing insanity and the narcotic evil, and thereby finds the medical profession guilty of still greater wrongs. 

As fiercely as it has attacked the use of smallpox vaccine, so it has assailed the methods of treating the insane and the encouragement of drug habits. 

That the number of inmates in state institutions for the insane in the United States increased 

460 per cent in forty years, as against an increase of population amounting to 110 per cent, is set forth as evidence of failure or neglect on on the part of medical guardians to conserve public health. 

Because the treatment of insanity so largely involves the nerves, chiropractors have found a fertile field for criticism in the methods employed under medical auspices, which they pronounce as antiquated and inept. The Chiropractic News of 

September 18, 1930, rates the cures of the insane [239]

in one chiropractic sanitarium at sixty-five per cent, and those in state institutions at only five per cent. The Chiropractic Review (Vol. 1, No. 

1) cites the growing burden of expense to care for the insane in the state of Illinois already amounting to approximately one dollar a year per capita of the entire population, and the fact of 500 patients being committed each month on an average from the city of Chicago. From a chiropractic sanitarium in Iowa, which is supervised by the 

Board of Control of State Institutions, comes the testimony of treatment of more than 1,000 cases in six years, including mental, nervous and epileptic subjects, and the offer to submit for inspection a record of cures of insanity greatly exceeding that of any state institution.

Dr. D. D. Palmer, discoverer of chiropractic, wrote in 1910, with reference to insanity: "It is usual to find occlusion of the third, sixth, seventh, eleventh and twelfth dorsal nerves. Why not locate the vertebra which causes the impingement, the cause of mental derangement?" (Page 49,

The Chiropractor's Adjuster.) Again (page 622) 

he writes: "All abnormal functions, either vital or intellectual, depend upon structural change of tissue. . . . As the vegetative function is subservient to the vital so the cumulative is subordinate to [240]

PUBLIC HEALTH 

the mental. Normal tonicity of tissue performs absorption, assimilation and excretion in normal amounts, an equilibrium we name health. Likewise, the cumulative will accumulate normal thots. 

If the solidity and tension of nerves and muscles are not normal, too lax or compact, their intellectual and vegetative functions will be abnormal.” 

In much the same vein as more recent defenders of the chiropractic faith, he declared (page 

868) in answer to an article in Everybody's Magazine by a physician, that the medical profession had based its conclusions regarding insanity upon post mortem examinations, whereas the chiropractors were depending upon findings made during life. "The medical fraternity continue to change their pathology," he writes, "each time apparently proving their diagnosis.' 

Much more severe is the censure visited upon doctors of medicine because of the increase in the number of narcotic addicts. The confessions of the latter, according to chiropractors, are said in nearly every case to reveal the habit as developed from authoritatively prescribed treatment in drugs or hypodermic injections; not from original transactions with dope peddlers, as is sometimes supposed. Illuminating facts on the situation in the 

United States were printed in The California [241]

Chiropractor, issue of November, 1928. This Pasadena publication states as follows: 

"Federal records show that narcotic violators in 1927 represent the largest number of Federal prisoners ever incarcerated for violating any one 

Federal law.

Addicts Multiply 

"Since 1919 there has been a 900 per cent increase in the number of addicts committed to Sing 

Sing, 500 per cent to Leavenworth, 3,000 per cent increase to Auburn Prison, New York. Forty per cent of the men prisoners going to Federal prisons are addicts; 60 per cent of the inmates in New 

York's jails and correctional institutions are addicts. 

"The Report of the U. S. Narcotic Division shows that apprehended narcotic violators rose from 1060 in 1917 to 10,297 in 1925; and the

Federal law aims only at the seller, not the user.' 

The Philadelphia Grand Jury was informed by Judge McDavitt in 1925 that 1,000 peddlers supplied 30,000 addicts in that city. Narcotic offenders sent to McNeil Island in six years numbered 998, as compared with only 58 bootleggers. 

In Kansas City, Missouri, 1,200 narcotic arrests were made in 1923. [242]

PUBLIC HEALTH 

A report from Washington, D. C., dated August 12, 1930, and carried in press dispatches of the following day, cites Dr. Charles E. Terry of New 

York, who made an address at the conference on narcotic control at the Public Health Bureau, held on the former date. Dr. Terry is declared to have said that surveys of scattered cities representing 2,200,000 of the country's population, showed that opium had gained in usage about 7.94 grains per capita, exclusive of that illicitly distributed. This represents a national consumption of about 140,- 000 pounds, which is nearly the total amount imported. It is announced that medical science is seeking substitutes for narcotics. 

This is but a mere fragment of the evidence similar to that presented at the opium conferences at Geneva in recent years, describing the spread of the dope evil throughout the world. 

A writer in the California Backbone, of November, 1922, observed, regarding the habit-forming drug: "It becomes apparent, then, that sickness in which there is distress is the usual condition preceding its initial use, and for the relief of which it is administered. The only answer to the 'narcotic evil' is to place a ban on its use in the practice of 'scientific' medicine, where it is freely prescribed in all sorts of inconsequential or mild [243]

'attacks' of disease, just to afford a temporary unconsciousness of the distress to make it look right to interested observers who are distressed by the suffering of a loved one. . . . "It becomes evident that narcotics should not be used in cases where there is hope for the recovery of the sick, and that this terrible, monstrous habit is chiefly forced upon the unsuspecting victim and his solicitous and unsuspecting loved ones without their knowledge or consent. You will not find two percent of the drug users who acquired the habit knowingly. . . ." 

"Life Extension” 

Chiropractic health propaganda bestows its share of advice on the prevention of disease. The early detection of subluxations means forestalling illness. Temperance, moderation, proper diet are persistently commended. The layman is advised to scrutinize mortality tables closely enough to discover their deceptions, since much that is claimed for life-extension work is more apparent than real. On this matter, it is pointed out in 

The Life Line of October, 1930, that a reduction in infant mortality and a lowering of the death rate under middle age have given the appearance of an expectancy of longer life. The average span [244]

PUBLIC HEALTH 

has been increased mainly by better care of infants, but statistics fail to prove that man over fifty-two years of age has any greater chance for longevity than he had in the year 1789. "The expectation of life has increased, but the span of life has remained stationary," said Dr. 

Louis I. Dublin, insurance statistician of New

York City, addressing the American Public Health 

Association at Minneapolis on October 3, 1929. He added that the cutting down of preventable deaths among young people has increased the average length of life, whereas improvement after the age of forty has been slight. Discouraging possibly from a medical point of view is his statement: "Such campaigns as center around the annual physical examination are very desirable, but their value, so far as they go to extending the life span, is questionable." 

Other chiropractic prevention advocates greater care in bodily postures as a means of checking subluxations, and one practitioner in San Diego, California, is undertaking a scientific study of attitudes as influencing health and disease. 

Physical education has long taken cognizance of the ill effects of incorrect posture.

Possessed with the ardor of a young science many chiropractors continue to uphold the philos- [245]

ophy originally expounded by D. D. Palmer. His theories of "innate intelligence," the "educated intelligence" and vital force are widely retained with a religiousness that brooks little contradiction. Some have averred that the mental hygiene movement has taken its cue from chiropractic. 

The profession cannot be said to have waged a direct campaign against vaccination. Many chiropractors are members of the American Medical Liberty Association, which is leading the fight against inoculation. Many have joined local movements to prevent the obligatory examination and vaccination of school children. The opposition of chiropractic is based on its germ theory and it condemns the practice of introducing virus into the blood for the purpose of immunization. Many shocking accounts of the death of individuals, particularly those of young children following the injection of vaccine and serums, have been given, supplying grave charges against medical authori ties. Concerning vaccination Dr. D. D. Palmer wrote: "It is the biggest piece of quackery and criminal outrage ever foisted upon any civilized people. Medical ignorance is the greatest danger to humanity today. Medical tyranny, by which criminal outrages are murdering our children all over this country by the enforcement of school [246]

PUBLIC HEALTH 

blood-poisoning laws, is the foulest blot on our civilization."* 

Vaccines and Serums 

Frederick R. Green, M.D., in the Journal of the American Medical Association, September 23, 1922, made this admission: "It is difficult to understand when or how the fiction arose that the medical profession is a divinely authorized and chosen class, charged with the protection of the public health and public welfare, even against the desires and wishes of the people themselves. Such a doctrine savors far too much of imperialism to be particularly popular at the present day."+ 

The League Against Compulsory Medication in Minneapolis, Minnesota, cites many sensational results of injections of vaccines and serums. Follows a portion of an article entitled "Stop Misleading and False Medical and Surgical Propaganda in the Public Schools of Our Nation." 

"The public schools and higher educational institutions of the United States have been used for years as centers for the circulation of misleading and false medical and surgical propaganda, and their pupils are regularly and increasingly 

*The Chiropractor's Adjuster, p. 854. 

†Copied by the Chiropractic Digest, organ of the Texas Chiropractic 

College, February, 1930. [247]

subjected to all kinds of doubtful, dangerous and deadly medical and surgical experiments. "Smallpox vaccination, so-called 'immunization' against diphtheria, scarlet fever and other diseases are untruthfully represented as practically certain preventives, and the false claim is made that these processes are 'HARMLESS', to secure the consent of parents to the vaccination and injection of their healthy children with filthy products of human and animal disease that are, literally, a menace to the health and even the life of every child whose pure blood stream is polluted with them. "Encephalitis (sleeping sickness) has followed vaccination in so many cases, and caused so many deaths in the 

Netherlands that compulsory smallpox vaccination of school children has been officially abandoned in that country for at least one year,* and there is a constantly growing belief on the part of competent medical authorities that smallpox vaccination is probably a direct or indirect cause of meningitis, infantile paralysis and other very serious nervous diseases. "In January, 1928, twelve children were killed by toxinantitoxin immunization at Bundaberg, Queensland, Australia.

Before that time a number of persons were killed and many more made seriously ill at Dallas, Texas. Makers of the socalled immunization material used at Dallas paid thousands of dollars to settle pending damage cases out of court. . . .'

The League Against Compulsory Medication refers to a report in the Post of Bridgeport, Connecticut, dated February 18, 1926, as stating that fifty-four hospital nurses were ill following injec- *Verified by direct report from League of Nations' Health Committee. [248]

PUBLIC HEALTH 

tions of an immunizing scarlet fever serum. Thirteen of these "promptly came down with the scarlet fever, from which the serum was supposed to protect them; the remaining forty-one were made so sick by their injections that they were unable to attend to their hospital duties, and outside nurses had to be brought in to keep the hospital running." 

The Tribune of Sioux City, Iowa, is quoted as stating: "It is open season on tonsils in Sioux 

City. . . . At least 1,080 sets have been jerked within the month. . . . Now tonsil operation has become almost as much a part of preparing for the school term as buying new clothes."

In line with this comment, the Journal of the Michigan Medical Society, of December, 1924, offers this startling information: "It has been estimated that fifty per cent of the present day tonsillectomies are unnecessary. This fifty per cent has been classified as the 'gasoline supply' of some doctors. Well, will someone rise up and give us the real facts? What is going to be done to stop these unnecessary tonsillectomies?" 

A statement that toxin-antitoxin immunized nurses in the Illinois Training School developed a higher percentage of diphtheria cases than the nurses in the same institution not immunized, with [249]

both classes working under the same conditions, appears in the Journal of the American Medical 

Association, January 28, 1928.

The fact that the manufacturers of toxin-antitoxin concede that among children above the age of eight years and in adults "local and general reactions occur in about ten percent of individuals," is set forth as of particular significance. 

Prevention-Anti-Prevention 

It is emphasized by the League Against Compulsory Medication that its members are not opposed to medicine and surgery and are disposed to protect reputable doctors and surgeons, and that they will oppose any kind of healing propaganda in the schools. "Good health may be so universal as to be commonplace," wrote James Workman, D.C., in The 

California Chiropractor of August, 1928. This well-known official of the American Bureau of 

Chiropractic, who resides in Santa Ana, California, believes that health education may eventually set man free from the thralldom of disease. However, he declares we have yet to learn to care for the child. Today stress is laid upon the preparation of the mind, while the preparation of the body to [250]

PUBLIC HEALTH 

protect it from disease should be the foremost concern. "If we could grapple with the child situation in its entirety for one generation, our public health, our economic efficiency, the moral character, sanity and stability of our people would advance," Dr. Workman avers. "There is no way out of the present situation of health in which the human race finds itself because of its inability to co-operate and its failure in mutual understanding except through the education and physical care of the child. Everyone has a right to a good start in life, and health education must go forward hand in hand with that of the mind. "The chiropractor of the present day realizes this and forms a supremely important part of the community machinery for the prevention of disease. ... 

Medical Monopoly 

In Chiropractic News, the official publication of the American Bureau of Chiropractic, issue of 

October 30, 1930, is mentioned the address of 

Julius Rosenwald of Chicago before the American

Hospital Association, which appeared in the New York Times. The statement that hospitals are now "in the big business class" with an investment of [251]

more than $3,000,000,000 is aimed by the News at medical monopoly. The prominent philanthropist is quoted as saying that the present 7,000 hospitals in the United States had beds to accommodate 900,000 patients besides out-patient services that care for an even larger number, but that one-third of the beds on an average are unoccupied. The Times says that "the average occupancy for all the general hospitals reporting to the American Medical Association for 1928 was 66 per cent." 

Chiropractic critics aver that endowments for such offices are unjustified under the conditions cited, and allude to recent questionnaires taken in middle western states by which it was demonstrated that less than fifty per cent of the public preferred medicine for the treatment of illness. 

In the absence of appropriations for chiropractic care, the projected expenditure of $23,765,000 by the United States Government to care for disabled soldiers, of which $10,320,000 was announced by the Disabled Veterans' Bureau in August, 1930, as contracted for hospitalization, occasioned further bitter comment on the part of the American Bu reau of Chiropractic. The Chiropractic News, in its leading article of September 18, 1930, says: "It seems about time for the United States Government [252]

PUBLIC HEALTH 

to recognize progress in health matters as well as in other fields. They are handing out the same old slop for medicine, the same old drugs, and doing the same old unimproved hocus-pocus that was in practice fifty or one hundred years ago, and the consequences are that the soldiers are not getting well, are remaining chronic invalids, and their maintenance is a continually mounting expenditure. . . . 

"Old in tradition, old in superstition, and old in errors, medical practice clings to the worst as well as the best within itself and stubbornly refuses to accept any healing art which does not conform to the rule of prescriptions, diagnoses, and surgery. We know in emergency that surgery has its value. 

Common sense also tells us that drugs may be palliating. We do not need a doctor's advice to know this. But when medicine proclaims that knives and drugs are the last words in healing, and that there is no need for any other healing method, it is retrogressive and dangerous to humanity. It is simply performing a colossal deceit to hold dominion over the handling of the human body and thereby build up an empire within a government, controlling everything within that government through a process of fear and blind confidence." [253]

CHAPTER XV The Occupational Field EFORMITIES and disorders characteristic of certain occupations or environments are coming to occupy attention on the part of organization leaders and those chiropractors who take special interest in social problems. Practice in this field has been developing persistently during recent years and there are promises of future extensive work therein on the part of state and national organizations. 

It is natural that chiropractic, with its emphasis upon anatomy, should concern itself with structural and postural abnormalities common to specific types of workers. Chiropractic is closely allied to modern advancement in corrective exercise and hygiene. It is apace with those preventive measures coming more and more to be adopted in family, educational and industrial life. Its prestige is helped by the custom of the patron of the chiropractor to seek a vertebral adjustment, not merely when he feels ill, but because he may desire [254]

to relax his nerves and muscles or to relieve strains due to work or sports. Thus chiropractic in its application becomes largely preventive. 

To the latter-day attention to efficiency and hygiene among industrial workers the study of occupational diseases has come as an important sequel. A generation ago little consideration was given to this vast sphere of research. The average person was aware of the maladies peculiar to miners, phosphorus workers and painters, but remained in total ignorance of a multiplicity of disorders, each caused by a definite kind of employment. In the last quarter of a century along with considerable change in the relations between employer and employee have come better working conditions than were dreamed of by the most sanguine social workers or labor leaders of the previous epoch. In this period the number of persons in gainful occupations in the United States has doubled. Industrial life insurance policies increased in value from $1,468,928,342 in 1900 to $16,685,581,197 in 1928. The manufacturer today conserves human health and human energy, not merely materials and machinery. Since 1880, when the Employers' Liability Act was passed in England, there has been more progress in the care of industrial workers than in all previous time, and [255]

most of this progress has been attained in the last twenty-five years. But there is something more than shower baths, reading rooms and glee clubs for wage earners now commanding the attention of employers and economists. Morale and hygiene are but a part of the plan of conservation. Partly as a result of the precise divisions of labor requiring the worker to confine himself to a single monotonous task, using the same bodily movements throughout the day, the number of occupational deformities has greatly increased. Alternating shifts and rest periods have not materially reduced this human wear and tear. Thus, into the factory has come the chiropractor to ease strained tendons and taut nerves, to suggest counteracting exercises, notably deep breathing and relaxation. Often this service is instituted as a result of the manufacturer or department manager having himself received vertebral adjustments and desiring that his employees receive them also.

From the reports of chiropractors virtually identical subluxations occur among women who have spent much time at the sewing machine, whether the electric or old-fashioned variety, just as stenographers habitually incur a contraction of the upper thoracic vertebrae. Printers setting type by the old method, continually reaching as they [256]

stand before the case, similarly contract the upper spine, causing an occlusion of the nerve which goes to the intestine, and, eventually, chronic constipation. All athletes develop certain muscles at the expense of others, to which impingements are said to be due as extensively as to the hard knocks of competition. Even the rope-walkers are especially addicted to what is known as chronic rotation. 

Instrumental musicians seek out the chiropractor to relieve the effects of long hours of practice, very much as vocalists invite relaxation, so necessary to flexible singing. Violinists and pianists, who out-numbered all other instrumentalists prior to the jazz-crowned saxophone, are particularly affected by the postures required during hours of practice. Paradoxically, the contortionist trap drummer is not immune from rhythm, and we anticipate the “jazz spine” as an interesting rival to "railroad back" and "house-maid's knee."

In various phases of community life sedentary habits offer a serious problem. In church and school, as well as shop, is abundant evidence of old customs of neglect, and modern health crusades have as yet failed to inspire certain groups with a sense of the simple needs of bodily preservation.

In some institutions health is menaced by improper diet which destroys the teeth and lowers vitality. [257]

Seventy-five per cent of the women between the ages of twenty-four and thirty in one eleemosynary occupation possess false teeth in entirety. 

About the same proportion of Swedes lose their teeth before middle age, because of the excessive amount of dairy products consumed in early years. 

Peasants of Ireland, Germany and Switzerland, who rely extensively upon bread and potatoes, experience the early loss of teeth and are particularly subject to tuberculosis.

It is among such groups that organized chiropractic is beginning to plan an extensive service, teaching not only proper diet, but corrective exercises supplemental to the administration of spinal adjustments for maintaining vital energy and the relief of nerve pressure. Chiropractic has not endorsed any given regime, each practitioner merely recommending such food and exercise as seem best suited to the case. Considerable importance is attached to abdominal breathing. In the study and research into occupational disorders, concerning which comparatively few authentic statistics are as yet available, avenues of approach to the various fields-religious, educational, industrial, agricultural, professional-will demand thorough analysis. 

Already chiropractic has learned that even a request for its service may not always be granted, as [258]

among inmates of soldiers' homes, for whom adjustments asked in many cases have been provided only after lengthy negotiations with government authorities. Strong economic considerations are advanced in the case of endowed institutions where money is spent on mental training to the neglect of bodily health.

The progress of chiropractic through these horizontal groups will be beset by the present guardians of public health who are prepared to oppose that which is alien to established methods. 

The fear that the vaccination of employees will be abandoned should chiropractic be extensively adopted in the densely populated spheres and the entire community thereby subjected to the danger of plague, alone will inaugurate new legislative and legal battles, greater in scope and intensity than those conflicts of the past in behalf of recognition.

The Armageddon of chiropractic will require all the resources of the rapidly merging associations of professionals and laymen.

Industrial Compensation-Insurance 

Already the right of the chiropractor to receive pay for services in cases within the scope of the industrial compensation law has occasioned dispute, notably in California where practitioners [259]

were advised by the Industrial Compensation Commission in 1930 that they could not be accorded the same privileges as medical physicians and surgeons. Their bills sent to the commission were not honored, nor did the Chiropractic Examining Board, according to the "straights," take issue in the matter despite clauses in the chiropractic law which were regarded as establishing rights to compensation equal with those of the medical doctors. 

In Section 13 of the act regulating the practice of chiropractic in California it is provided that "Chiropractic licentiates shall observe and shall be subject to all State and municipal regulations relating to all matters pertaining to the public health, and shall sign death certificates and make reports as required by law to the proper authorities, and such reports shall be accepted by the officers of the departments to which the same are made." Section 18 contains the paragraph: "Nothing herein shall be construed as repealing the 'medical practice act' of June 2, 1913, or any subsequent amendments thereof, except in so far as that act or said amendments may conflict with the provisions of this act as applied to persons licensed under this act, to which extent any and all acts or parts of acts in conflict herewith are hereby re- [260]

pealed." In Section 17 it is provided that persons violating the provisions of the chiropractic act be prosecuted, and that the secretary of the board shall aid the district attorneys. 

Recognition of chiropractic has been accorded by insurance organizations in more than half of the states and by many companies and beneficiary associations of a national character. As many as 

145 insurance organizations in the United States and Canada in 1924 were paying claims for illness and accident over the signatures of chiropractors, according to a list published in Davenport. It is probable that a considerably larger number of organizations at present bestow this form of recognition, since the names have been listed mainly from the somewhat sporadic reports of chiropractors in response to inquiries. Many of the most prominent insurance companies of the country are included. 

The states where the greatest number of organizations have acknowledged such claims are Illinois, Massachusetts, New York, Indiana, Wisconsin,

New Jersey and Ohio, indicating that the status of legal recognition in communities has little influence over insurance companies. [261]

CHAPTER XVI Highlights of Progress HE INFLUENCES and happenings which have tended most strongly to advance the cause of chiropractic have been variously identified. Answers to a questionnaire sent by the author to leading educators of chiropractic in different parts of the country would indicate that since 1910 the most important influences have been, namely: better training and facilities for students of chiropractic, the amalgamation of organizations into such larger ones as the National 

Chiropractic Association and the International 

Chiropractic Congress; a generally growing prejudice against drugs, for which the "physical culture movement" and Bernarr Macfadden are cited as outstanding factors; improved means of diagnosis, such as the X-ray; increasing recognition of chiropractic as demonstrated and fostered by legislation; finally, a lessening of the power of schools over the profession at large. 

Dr. C. M. Kightlinger, of the Eastern Chiro practic Institute, New York, among other school [262]

men responding to the questionnaire in January, 1931, acclaims the physical culture movement and a resultant reaction from medication as of foremost influence upon the advancement of chiropractic. This movement began to attain national strength about the time the first chiropractic schools were established. The theories of Bernarr 

Macfadden on exercise and fasting attracted male readers of all ages and eventually many followers among young women. The "back to nature" slogan, health restaurants, simple diet, mastication and the exaggerated Fletcher style of eating, which proved too painstaking for healthy appetites, all reflected a newly rising interest in daily care of the body and less reliance upon patent medicines. Not only did the Macfadden pleas for nature as the greatest healer and exercise as the greatest preventive harmonize with chiropractic, but that selfcured publisher, whose muscles were pictured from Broadway to Golden Gate, openly espoused the Palmer principles, affording in Physical Culture one of those rare and effective mediums of advertising for the hard-pressed practitioners. To what extraordinary degree the sentiment against drugs has been increased by "nature propaganda" can be estimated by the success of this magazine and numerous other Macfadden publications. [263]

Thousands of patients of chiropractors became converted to the subluxation theory as a result of the sensational attacks upon the use of drugs and vaccination. 

Dr. Kightlinger sees the second outstanding influence as similar to the first: publicity regarding cases of unnecessary surgery, fee-splitting and unethical conduct on the part of medical doctors. 

The volume of this criticism has swelled almost continuously for a score of years, until even medical patients are beginning to revolt against dehumanizing clinics, specialists and paraphernalia, and to clamor for the return of the old family doctor-who is able to recall what ails his patient without looking up a chart with a history of the case at each visit. 

This New York chiropractor declares that the decrease in factionalism within the chiropractic profession, accompanied by a growing spirit of co-operation, has greatly strengthened the system. 

Similar sentiment prompts Dr. Willard Carver, dean of faculty of Carver Chiropractic College, Oklahoma City, to name as a foremost influence the organization of the International Chiropractic

Congress, which has engendered since 1926 a greater fraternal feeling among school and college heads, state examiners and the officers of state as- [264]

sociations. In a more sardonic vein, Dr. Carver mentions as the second most important boon to chiropractic since 1910 the exploitation of a nerve testing device by Dr. B. J. Palmer, and observes: 

"thus emphasizing, by contrast, the marvelous value of the fundamental principles of chiropractic." 

Dr. V. C. Schulze, president of the National 

College of Chiropractic, Chicago, considers that the realization of the necessity for higher educational standards has been the strongest influence for progress in the last twenty years. Second he names the amalgamation of the Universal Chiro practors Association and the American Chiropractic Association, which became the National Chiro practic Association in 1930. Dr. Schulze com ments upon the advantages derivable through the breaking away from school influence by chiropractors in the field. "School men," he says, "have been guilty of pitting one set of practitioners against another, thus contributing a great deal of damage to the cause of chiropractic." 

Dr. James N. Firth, vice-president of Lincoln 

Chiropractic College, Indianapolis, shares the opinion that improved education has been the greatest factor in advancing chiropractic. Since 1910 a high school training has become a preliminary re- [265]

quirement for chiropractors, and the course of study in chiropractic schools has been extended until now the standard course covers three y Dr. Firth adds that the curriculum has been so broadened that today's graduate chiropractor has a thorough training in all of the fundamental branches of sciences relating to health and disease, rendering him more competent in the public service with which he is entrusted. Second in importance Dr. Firth believes to be the legislative recognition now attained in the great majority of the states of the Union. "While some of the laws have been unfavorable to the rapid dissemination of chiropractic to the public, because of restrictions placed upon applicants for license, yet they have led to its respect in the public mind," says Dr. 

Firth. He proceeds to name as a happening of epochal importance the unification process taking place in the profession through the organization of the National Chiropractic Association, which is heralded as truly representative and free from the kind of factions and affiliations which have disrupted other organizations. 

Dr. H. C. Harring, president of the Missouri

Chiropractic College, St. Louis, considers that skillful advertising and the wonderful truth of chiropractic are mainly responsible for its success. He yea Ca [266]

cites the effective advertising of chiropractic's most prominent developer, Dr. B. J. Palmer, and refers to the cogent reasoning of the fundamentals relating to life force or intelligence which finds expression through the nervous system. He adds: "Medicine has offered nothing as appealing or logical as this, but has merely dwelt upon the mysteries as of old. Chiropractic became prominent at an opportune time, when people were beginning to tire of the use of drugs. The value of drugless healing methods had been proclaimed by Bernarr Macfadden and others, which made it easier for the people to accept chiropractic."

From the Palmer School of Chiropractic at Dav enport opinions are forwarded by D. D. Palmer, grandson of the discoverer, who is business manager of the institution. It is indicated by letter that the answers to the questionnaire were drafted in the absence of Dr. B. J. Palmer. They describe as "definite" factors which have contributed most to the advancement of chiropractic in the last twenty years, declaring these factors to be research and the development of scientific methods where practice has rested on theory only; the substantiation of the chiropractic theory by the X-ray and nerve testing instruments; proof and location of vertebral subluxations as determined by the X-ray [267]

in radiographing the spine. Finally is mentioned the securing of legislation in most of the United States, which has granted recognition to the practice of chiropractic as a distinct and separate science.

From the South comes an authoritative reply from Dr. James R. Drain, president of the Texas

Chiropractic College. The chief causes for the progress of chiropractic since 1910 are held to be: first, advertising and publicity; second, the persistent fight to keep chiropractic a distinct science uncorrupted by extraneous and incompatible theories; third, the elimination of charlatanry in chiropractic education. 

Dr. T. F. Ratledge, president of Ratledge Chiropractic School, Los Angeles, states: "In my opinion, the most important factors in the advancement of the cause of chiropractic are: The enactment of laws establishing boards of examiners composed of persons educated in chiropractic; organization and intelligent publicity, and the increased requirements in strictly chiropractic subjects by the leading chiropractic schools."

The Modalities 

The following question showed a rather sharp and evenly divided sentiment: "What is the effect [268]

of the chiropractor's use of mechanical accessories on the present and future status of chiropractic?" Dr. Drain emphatically declares: "Mechanical accessories only lead to confusion and the destruction of chiropractic. If continued by chiropractors the science will become absorbed by other professions and become extinct. Chiropractors must adhere to chiropractic and its philosophy." 

Dr. Harring of St. Louis is less categorical, while evidently in no great sympathy with the "modalities." "If used only where indicated and with the clear understanding that such methods are not chiropractic they will do no harm," he advises, adding, however: "Practitioners with the least amount of knowledge regarding the human body, who do not understand enough about structure or function become the victims of mechanical accessory salesmen." Dr. Harring seems to regard the machine as something of a natural enemy to chiropractic, for he remarks upon the loss of enthusiasm on the part of practitioners after they stray from the fundamentals, and how for their failures chiropractic is blamed. "So long as enthusiasm runs high," says Dr. Harring, "they keep up their effective work, and then gradually lose confidence in their ability because they lost sight of the fundamental truth of their practice." [269]

Dr. Carver ironically remarks as follows: "The effect upon chiropractic of the use of mechanical accessories is, by contrast, to emphasize the extreme value of the fundamental principles of chiropractic, and to arouse the layman to a realization that there is a movement that, if permitted headway, would take chiropractic from them." 

From the "Fountain Head" comes a technical compromise, the Davenport opinion being that as long as chiropractors use mechanical means to help locate the precise point of nerve interference, or to determine the exact position of the vertebra, such appliances will be a great help to the chiropractor, "but where they are used for the purpose of treating effects, or for the stimulation of tissue,' continues the Palmer statement, "they are immediately out of the realm of chiropractic and cannot be considered as such. The future status of chiropractic depends greatly upon the chiropractor staying within his own realm."

Unqualified defense of the use of appliances by chiropractors is afforded by Dr. Schulze of Chicago. 

He writes as follows: "We beg to state that the effect of a chiropractor's use of mechanical accessories is bound to help chiropractic and the future status of chiropractic. This is already proven in the steady progress that has been made. [270]

For instance, the old definition of chiropractic was that it was a system of healing by finding, through palpation in the hand, the subluxations of the movable vertebrae of the spine and correcting them by hand. As long as twelve to fourteen years ago the

X-ray was introduced as an accessory in helping to locate deviations from the normal in the human spine. Further progress is shown in the use of other diagnostic methods, such as blood pressure apparatus, the stethescope, laboratory findings, etc., and translating the symptom complex in every case into terms of chiropractic through the addition of the spinal findings. "The average patient going into the office of a 'doctor of chiropractic' and getting an all-round check-up of his condition through mechanical or other accessories is favorably influenced toward chiropractic and toward the practitioner. That which is true of the analysis or diagnosis of a case is true of the treatment. If a patient has diabetes or a degenerative cardiac-vascular-renal trouble, the diet becomes of supreme importance along with the adjustment of the spine. For the control of pain, Nature's methods, such as the application of heat or cold and other accessories, have a splendid effect in enhancing the present and future status of chiropractic." [271]

In the estimation of Dr. Kightlinger of New 

York the machine is a sort of two-faced usurper, one face looking smilingly forward and the other unhappily backward. He contributes an especially interesting opinion. "The chief effect of the chiropractor's use of mechanical accessories," says 

Dr. Kightlinger, "has been to stimulate a more critical study of chiropractic principles by chiropractors and greater precision on their part in performing their work. This effect has been just as profound among non-users of accessories as among users, inasmuch as the work of these two groups became, with the advent of instruments, to a certain extent competitive. Each group has had to justify its position by the superiority of results obtained. I am referring here to those accessories which aim to assist the chiropractor in detecting points in the spine which require his attention. "Accessories of the type generally recognized as belonging to physio-therapy have in most instances tended to submerge the identity of chiropractic, and this will always be true.
